3
2026 Investment Considerations
 We reaffirm our extreme-bullish-outlier view, established over 3 years ago, on the importance of AI to 
society and the global economy. The contrarian predictions made in our original AI Prophecy continue to play out.  
This includes our contention that AI would evolve at light speed, begin to surpass human PhD-level understanding 
across domains, and affect the shape of the economic cycle through a massive workforce productivity boom. AI is 
becoming the single most important discussion point for equity & debt markets. 
 We also predicted “mass-scale white collar job realignment, 4-8 years out” and think the tip of that iceberg has 
started to appear.  While we stated in early-November that momentum-driven AI investment warranted caution, we 
believe actual buildout and adoption of AI remains in early innings as the $35+ Trillion TAM of global labor is 
pursued. The largest Tech companies have nearly ceased hiring of humans, and this may foreshadow the 
behavior of other industries over time.
 Alpha generation was created in the 2nd half of 2025 by 1) resisting the temptation of multi-hundred-Billion-
dollar AI commitments which result in customer concentration and need to be funded (see here); and 2) 
anticipating the potential for Google’s Gemini to be re-assessed higher (see here).  
 For 2026, it is worth considering positive risk/reward in out-of-favor seat-based SaaS application stocks, 
which are sticky and ingrained due to business logic and metadata, and starting to monetize AI.
 Proliferation of AI Agents will surprise positively, equivalent to adding Billions of virtual workers into the 
global workforce. Agents apply logic & reasoning to make faster decisions; decisions result in transactions; 
transactions are the engine of the economy.
 Software stocks have once again reached a 25-year extreme low valuation relative to semiconductor stocks, a 
rare level of positioning that sets the stage for software to outperform.
 JPM strategists see above-trend earnings growth of 13-15% for the S&P 500 for the next 2 years, as “the earnings 
benefit tied to deregulation and broadening AI-related productivity gains remain underappreciated.”
 IPO activity picked up off of depressed levels in 2025, and market willing, momentum may continue in ’26.
 Long term fundamental underpinnings remain healthy: As business confidence improves, companies will likely 
reprioritize digital transformation projects through software that automates, harnesses data, and boosts productivity.
 We favor software stocks that meet 4 criteria: 1) Prioritization in our survey work; 2) High Gross Renewal rates 
(95-99%); 3) Cash flow generators; and 4) AI monetization potential. Stocks that meet a majority of these criteria 
include Microsoft, Salesforce, Intuit, HubSpot, Snowflake, and Datadog.

=== PAGE 4 ===

4
JPM Strategists: Resilient Economy and AI-driven Supercycle 
Fueling Record Capex and Rapid Earnings Expansion
(JPM 2026 Global Equity Outlook)
We are positive on global equities expecting double-digit gains across DM and EM supported by robust earnings 
growth, lower rates, and declining policy headwinds. The U.S. is set to remain the world’s growth engine, driven by a 
resilient economy and an AI-driven supercycle that is fueling record capex and rapid earnings expansion. Both 
corporates and governments across the world are racing to invest in AI in search of productivity gains and out of fear 
of becoming obsolete (“FOBO”). The AI sector’s momentum is spreading geographically and across a diverse list of 
industries, from Technology and Utilities to Banks, Health Care and Logistics, and in the process creating winners and 
losers.  The challenge—this disruption is unfolding within an already unhealthy K -shaped economy, with AI expected 
to amplify this polarization even further. The AI “Wall of Worry” is likely to persist for years to come. In such an 
environment, broad sentiment measures remain prone to sharp swings, as we have seen this year and most recently, 
even though underlying trends remain intact and fundamentals solid.
 U.S. — We are constructive on the S&P 500’s outlook both in terms of price target (7,500 by YE26) and above-
trend earnings growth of 13-15% for at least the next two years (2026 and 2027 EPS of $315 and $355 vs. 
consensus of $309 and $352, respectively). This outlook is anchored on our JPM Economics view of two more 
cuts followed by an extended pause. However, should the Fed ease policy further (due to improving inflation 
dynamics), we see greater upside with the S&P 500 surpassing 8,000 in 2026. 
Despite AI bubble and valuation 
concerns, we see current elevated multiples correctly anticipating above-trend earnings growth, an AI capex boom, 
rising shareholder payouts, and easier fiscal policy (
i.e. OBBBA). More so, the earnings benefit tied to deregulation 
and broadening AI-related productivity gains remain underappreciated.
 Style Views — AI should support the continued dominance of Quality Growth and Momentum in the U.S., while 
investor positioning in Speculative Growth, High Beta, and Low Volatility will remain highly sensitive to monetary 
policy. Elsewhere, we look for Value to stay preferred over Growth.
 Technology (OW) will remain front and center of the AI theme next year, but we continue to like the momentum 
trade of being long Tech Hardware (i.e., Semis and Tech Equipment) on better capex buildout visibility over 
Software, which will continue to see winners (i.e., Microsoft, cybersecurity) and losers (i.e., IT Services). This year, 
we expected Tech to be in the crossfire from both higher domestic tariffs and retaliatory actions (particularly Semis 
and Tech Hardware), a risk we see significantly reduced going into next year. Similarly, Communication Services 
(OW) should also remain a large beneficiary of the AI trade given Google and Meta make up ~3/4 of the sector.
 Capex likely to surprise positively in 2026. AI 30 has invested ~$820B in Capex and R&D over the past year 
(~55/45 split), with investment spending expected to grow by +34% over the next 12 months (95% attributed to 
Hyperscalers). Numbers have been beating analyst estimates since early 2023, with the average Capex surprise 
for AI 30 standing at ~15% YTD (vs. 11.8% in 2024). The question of whether the capex cycle could broaden out 
past just AI remains highly sensitive to monetary policy, but there could be a 
catch up next year as some of the 
policy uncertainty is lifted and some of the promised foreign direct investments go from planning phase to 
implementation.
Dubravko Lakos-Bujas, 
Head of Global Markets Strategy
Published: 11/25/2025
We show portions of the report and 
add elements of our own text emphasis 
that in our view are more relevant to US 
Software investors. Please click the link 
above for the full report and full list of 
contributors.

6
Our Key Fundamental Bull Thesis For Software
 Core projects moving slowly, but getting closer to resumption: automation, 
AI/ML, companies becoming data-driven, Digital Transformations
 Software a deflationary force in an inflationary world, as it enhances productivity 
and streamlines business processes
 Durable cloud migration activity to unleash scalability and agility
 AI driving powerful tech investment wave, unleashing multi-year workforce 
efficiency boom
 Opportunity for AI to increase global GDP substantially
 Global economic growth increasingly driven by “Digital” while non-Digital 
decreases in relevance
 Potential for Tech spending to double as % of GDP in the span of a decade
 Target-rich environment for investors seeking sticky, recurring models with 
increasing gross profit dollars

8
Our Variant Viewpoints to Consider for ’26
 Revenge of the Nerds: The reveal for the first batch of NVIDIA Blackwell-trained models, 
excluding the open-source variants, shocks the market with System 2 thinking capabilities, 
closed-loop actions, and verifiable truth process which massively reduces hallucination…
 …This leads to a midyear rebalancing of the Google/OpenAI thought process, and edges 
closer to a logical middle-ground that appreciates both camps rather than clustering at extremes.
 Meanwhile, the Vera Rubin wave of commits also commences mid-26, underpinning healthy 
hyperscaler growth and supporting the AI bull case.
 Cambrian volatility gains attention: multiple VC-backed AI startups valued too richly are 
marked lower as they struggle with product-market fit.
 Only if necessary, the US Government classifies AI data centers as “strategic sovereign assets” or 
“critical infrastructure”; in tandem with a broader consortium, steps in to backstop debt markets 
and/or make equity investments.
 Seat-based SaaS application stocks reverse course, grind higher off of depressed levels of 
sentiment/valuation, not due to material re-acceleration, but rather re-assessment of business 
logic stickiness, self-funding models, Agentic monetization ramp, charging for data access.
 The Vibe-coding market bifurcates, to the detriment of surface-only vibe-coding tools, which 
have detached or floating code and are only capable of façade development, and thus struggle 
when going into production. Vercel, Figma Make, Supabase, and others gain favor.  
 AI impact on labor markets gains airtime; call center agents, developers, marketers, paralegals, 
data entry in focus, but brunt of impact still beyond the horizon.
 Recovery from Google AI Overviews impact is nowhere to be seen: paid search traffic 
remains stuck in the mud and pivoting to alternate channels takes time.

January ’25: 91% of CIOs See Apps Gaining or Maintaining Importance
In a few brief sentences, please describe how your organization's software stack might change 
due to the rise of AI and Agents?
Key Takeaways
Impact of AI & Agents on Applications Importance within the Software Stack
 This chart depicts CIOs’ 
anticipated impact to the 
importance of applications as they 
deploy AI / Agents, measured as a 
percentage of CIOs who 
mentioned apps in some capacity. 
Based on n = 77.
 34% of CIOs see applications 
gaining importance as they deploy 
AI / Agents vs. only 9% who see 
applications losing importance. 
 We believe this data conveys a 
materially more positive view of 
app software than investors 
currently ascribe. 
 In many cases CIOs are describing 
a scenario wherein the existing 
apps will be infused with Agentic 
capabilities and evolve to more 
dynamic, context-aware interfaces 
even if human interaction is 
reduced. 
Source: J.P. Morgan Research. Note: the full question provided to survey respondents was as follows: In a few brief sentences, please 
describe how your organization's software stack might change due to the rise of AI and Agents? [For example, Do Applications gain or 
lose importance? What about databases or cloud infra consumption models? Is a new Control Point or center of gravity emerging 
within the software stack?]

June ’25: CIOs Will Primarily Buy AI Agents from SaaS App Providers
Please indicate the extent to which you Agree or Disagree with the following statements (1 = 
Disagree, 5 = Agree):
Source: J.P. Morgan Research. Based on n = 168. 
Key Takeaways
 This chart shows the extent to 
which CIOs agree or disagree with 
a series of AI-related statements.
 CIOs appear to primarily agree 
that the public cloud providers 
currently offer sufficient GPU 
availability to meet their 
organizations’ AI needs (no GPU 
shortage among enterprises), and 
that they will primarily buy AI 
Agents from SaaS providers, 
much more so than they plan to 
build their own custom AI Agents. 
 CIOs also indicate that they have 
the appetite and budget 
approval needed to experiment 
with and invest in AI 
technologies over the next 12-24 
months, while they also expect 
their organizations to require 
fewer employees in the next 3-5 
years due to advancements in AI.
 Net/net, CIOs will continue heavy 
investment into AI, primarily using 
public cloud and SaaS Agents, 
resulting in lower headcount 
needs.

14
When Oracle Spiked to $345, We Carefully Considered Risks
“In our view, investors may be misinterpreting the implications of the RPO figures Oracle is reporting, as short-term 
trading responses tend to be headline-driven and controlled by the instinctual mind, while deeper thinking takes longer to 
settle in, in some cases requiring quarters or years. At this point, if we could know about one important consideration, it would be 
how much of Oracle’s RPO balance stems from one single customer, OpenAI: for instance, investors may consider whether it could 
represent 25% of RPO, or 75% of RPO, or sit somewhere else inside or outside of those ranges. If the latest contract for this 
customer, seemingly >$30B annualized, has a duration ranging from 5-10 years (another detail we do not have, and worth noting 
some indications that the Abilene, TX lease is actually 15 years), then clearly this most-recent contract from OpenAI, which was 
previously-disclosed, is quite a substantial factor in the equation. Oracle is also closing business outside of OpenAI, including at 
least 2 other contracts that are worth “multi-billions”, and yet we are in the dark as to whether the multi-billions add up to $4B out of 
the $455B RPO balance, or $10B, $50B, $100B, etc. - a huge swing factor when trying to assess customer concentration risk 
and likelihood of customers being able to fund $455B worth of future payments - plenty of tech companies carry net debt, 
and/or minimal cash, and/or will be burning cash for the next several years - that list is not limited to Oracle itself. Finally, 
while it will take time to understand whether other industry players lost the bidding wars for this business or passed on it for certain 
reasons, and it will be difficult to assess the margin profile of these contracts, we do hold Oracle in very high regard for its 
history of margin discipline, strong capital allocation, and resilience to downturns due to inherently sticky revenue streams, which 
means investors should be open-minded and considering a range of potential future outcomes.”
Source: J.P. Morgan note on Oracle (ORCL – Neutral – $230 Price Target), published September 10, 2025. On 9/10/25, ORCL shares traded as high as $345.72, and closed at $328.33.

15
Takeaways
 This chart depicts the aggregate value of software IPOs and other transactions between 1994 and 2025 YTD. 
 We stated in Dec-21 that the 2020-2021 software IPO super-cycle had been partly driven by elements that would 
not continue: federal government stimulus and a low-interest rate environment fueling an explosion in risk taking 
activity and an expansion in software valuation multiples. IPO activity essentially ceased in 2022 and 2023.
 The 2001-2003 software IPO drought was severe. Two years ago, we commented that we were 50-75% of the 
way through the most recent drought. One year ago, we updated that viewpoint to 60-85% of the way through the 
drought and we indicated that it was possible that the market could open up again in 2025. IPO activity did in fact 
increase notably in 2025, headlined by the two largest IPOs, CoreWeave and Figma. 
Source: J.P. Morgan and Bloomberg Finance L.P. Note: 2025 data reflects YTD as of December 17th, 2025.
Aggregate Value of Software IPOs Priced, 1994-2025 YTD
Software IPOs: The Quiet Period is Over, Dusting off the S-1s

Q3 Seq. Growth of Leading Indicators: Uplift from AI Labs

=== PAGE 21 ===

21
Note: 1) There could be an unusually wide range of outcomes to the predictions above because the technology discussed is evolving so rapidly. 2) Language from Spring Series 2023 is in its original form.
Original AI Prophecy Established Our Bullish-Outlier View
 Seminal moment in tech, more so than the Internet or iPhone (we stated this last year)
 We were not impressed by IoT, Metaverse, Blockchain, 3D printing: GenAI is very different
 GenAI is doing things today that everyone agreed were physically impossible 9-12 months ago
 It is evolving at light speed; ChatGPT capabilities are different today than 3 weeks ago
 ChatGPT vastly outperforms the typical person in the Bar, LSATs, SATs, GRE…
 LLMs can work in >1,200 dimensions; human beings struggle with 3 dimensions
 GenAI is applying Logic & Reasoning, connecting abstract thoughts in ways that its engineers did not predict and 
cannot fully explain
 Microsoft / OpenAI have potential for Escape Velocity, although it will become increasingly competitive
 VC investments pivoting rapidly from Cloud & Crypto to GenAI; material % of Y Combinator companies are AI
 At software user conferences, loudest visceral responses by far coming from GenAI code-generation demos
 GPT-4-32K can understand / store a 50-page document
 Potential for massive workforce productivity boom, next 1-3 years; could affect shape of economic cycle
 Potential for mass-scale white collar job wipeout, 4-8 years out (Customer Service, Data Entry, Content Creation, 
Paralegals, Teachers, Doctors, Authors, Photographers, Graphic Artists, Tax Preparers, and many more)
 Agentic AI, advanced multi-modal integration, and longer-duration inferencing on the horizon, enabling end-to-
end autonomous systems capable of long-term planning and deliberation
 Artificial general intelligence (AGI) and artificial superintelligence (ASI) transitioning from the realm of hypothetical 
to conceivable milestones within the next 1-3 years

Encouraging Proprietary Quotes on AI Adoption & ROI
“We have a biotech client that uses 
Databricks, investing heavily in AI, and seeing 
a lot of value. They’re now able to use AI to 
screen potential molecules to decide what’s 
promising, and what isn’t. It’s essentially 
taking what would otherwise be a multi-year 
process, up to five years, down to a matter of 
weeks and saving millions and millions in 
development dollars.” 
– Databricks Ecosystem Contact
“I’d bet there are some companies that are hiding 
how well their AI projects are going because of 
what it means for the [large number of] jobs that 
could be replaced, it could be dangerous for them 
[politically and socially] and I’m not sure society is 
ready for it. I think that is why sometimes you 
don’t hear about AI projects going well despite the 
fact that we see a lot of success when we work 
with customers.” 
– SI Contact
“I do see AI projects progressing from pilot to 
production at some level of scale. Think going 
from pilots of $250-500K to launching some of 
these at production in the ~$2.5-5M range (~2-
3 dozen deals). Those are on what we consider 
the smaller side, we think of really big deals as 
more in the $20M+ range, but they are 
significant. Most of the deals are customers 
developing internal AI solution using APIs to 
LLMs, mostly OpenAI and some Anthropic.”
 – SI Contact
“The problem everyone is trying to solve in AI… is 
this: repeatable, standard use cases. We have that 
for [non-AI] software; sure you have industry 
specifications and company customizations, but it is 
within a specific framework. For AI, we don’t have 
that. That makes it hard to scale for both partners 
and the [SaaS incumbents]. I think that is why you’re 
seeing so much of companies developing their own 
AI solutions. I don’t think it’s going to be like that for 
the long-run, it just isn’t truly scalable.”
 – SI Contact
“I work with Databricks, and we see a lot of 
demand for AI [among enterprises]. Companies 
have budget for it, or make budgets for it, but 
they find dollars to spend on AI one way or 
another, they make it happen. I know the 
narrative of AI ‘not working’, but I personally 
just don’t see it.” 
– Databricks Ecosystem Contact
“We…created a sales coaching Agent… [The 
client tried it and said] ‘It’s awesome… we 
have to have it’… it does a better job [than a 
human coach].” 
– Salesforce Ecosystem Contact
“AI adoption is shifting from 
experimentation to operational 
automation. I see clients deploying AI to 
reduce cost per process, and Oracle is an 
orchestrator of the processes.”
– Oracle Ecosystem Contact
“AI changes everything… last year AI was 
an idea, but not anything like what’s being 
presented today.”
– Oracle Ecosystem Contact
“If we can replace our outsourced asset 
work with Firefly, that pays for itself, I 
think.” 
– Adobe Customer
Source: J.P. Morgan. Note: These quotes have been gathered by our research team through proprietary, non-public conversations or survey work with ecosystem contacts across the enterprise software landscape 
during September-December 2025.

=== PAGE 24 ===

24
Potential Hurdles to the Bull Thesis
 While buyer behavior appears to have stabilized, guidance and tone continues to show some pockets of macro 
constraint and unevenness across geographies and segments  
 Growth rates have continued to drift lower, albeit with some recent signs of stabilization 
 Hiring activity remains lukewarm across our coverage list following a period of extreme layoffs 
 S/W valuations have returned to fairly normal, but equity market valuations are historically high
 Optimizations and license rationalization seem to have peaked but may continue to some extent
 Mixed economic indicators amidst a steady low growth environment
 Historically, much bear market damage can sometimes occur AFTER Fed Pause/Pivot, as economic damage 
might already have been done
 SBC / GAAP discussion ongoing in the background & a lingering issue for Software vs. other sectors
 AI monetization and pricing models may take time to solidify, while datacenter supply remains in flux and AI 
scaling laws an evolving topic
 Magnificent Seven performance trend may change course, affecting breadth of money flows
 Gross margin percentage may trend structurally lower due to AI processing costs
 Inflation and interest rates may trend differently or remain more elevated than expected
 Evolving policy agenda for the US administration (government spending, trade & tariff, taxation, industry-
specific regulations, antitrust, etc.)
 AI budget prioritization potentially “crowding out” other categories of software spending
 Investor sentiment relating to “AI Bubble” concerns, data center funding costs, debt levels, AI disruption 
risk, seat compression potential create extra volatility

Appendix
 This appendix contains the most recent content from our deep-dive due diligence and 
channel check interviews for each company.
Note: These comments have been reproduced in their original form and have not been edited. Comments should not be attributed to J.P. Morgan and are not representative of its views.

=== PAGE 37 ===

37
Industry Contact 1 
General
• Contact represents an Adobe Silver Solutions Partner firm, with a focus primarily on the marketing technology suite of Adobe products, including the Adobe Experience 
Platform (AEP), Marketo, Adobe Real-Time CDP, and several other solutions.
• Contact’s firm provides co-sell, implementation, and support services for Adobe and several other SaaS companies, with a primary focus on the mid-market, with some 
large enterprise exposure.
Demand Environment
• Regarding the pace of business during FQ1 (late Feb-end), relays that their firm’sAdobe practice has been “stronger” than the prior year, with “more deals” and 
“more interest earlier in the year” relative to the recent past.
• Characterizes the demand environment for his Adobe practice as “definitely bouncing back” when compared to the last two years, though notes that it remains below 
more frothy levels seen prior to that.
• Shares that momentum with “net new customers” in the quarter was better than both 2024 and 2023,evidenced by an uptick in “sales volumes,” “calls,” and “new 
opportunities” entering the pipeline for his firm. Indicates that his practice is seeing a~1.8x increase in the volume of new deals per week as compared to the same 
time last year.
• However, notes that FQ1 tends to have a lot of customer renewals for his firm and that on average “renewals have been down,” noting that some of this has been driven 
by competitive pressures.
• Highlights that his organization’srevenue related to services provided for Adobe products was up 30-40% last year and that he hopes his practice will grow this 
amount by “at least 30-40%” this year as well. Notes that his firm is focused on shifting toward “more moderate to large” deals rather than numerous “smaller ones.”
Product Momentum
• Describes Adobe as “going through a transition” over the last several years, including having acquired some products relating to its Digital Experience suite and focusing 
on integrating them into the platform.
• Within marketing, characterizes “Marketo as probably the biggest product” in terms of his customer base, though suggests that Adobe has increasingly focused on the 
Adobe Experience Platform (AEP).
• Believes that within the marketing suite, the potential “long-term growth area” for Adobe revolves around its AEP offering, reiterating the recent disclosure from 
the company that AEP and native apps have surpassed a $1B book of business.
• More broadly across the Adobe ecosystem, describes the company as having created a “new product space” with its AI toolkit, including Firefly, and expects this to 
be an increasing focus for the company this year.
• Believes there is a “good opportunity to grow” Adobe’s AI business, though notes the long-term potential for Adobe to “run out of big companies to go after” and the 
need for it to develop lower-priced solutions that enable it to eventually go after a smaller cohort of customers, perhaps outside the Fortune 500.
Competitive Landscape
• In the enterprise segment, describes Adobe as “being right there” relative to the competition, with products like AEP well catering to enterprise needs. However, 
suggests that in the midmarket and SMB spaces, Adobe “hasn’t evolved as quickly” as some other solutions available in the market today, including HubSpot 
and Salesforce in some instances.
• However, suggests that Adobe has been intentional in “going upmarket,” for which he notes “that’s where a lot of the revenue is,” though shares that some 
midmarket customers find it difficult to afford some of Adobe’s more enterprise-grade products and services.
• In addition to HubSpot and Salesforce competition in the marketing automation space, also describes competition in the Customer Data Platform (CDP) space from 
both Microsoft and Salesforce, though believes that “Salesforce is the biggest player” in the CDP market specifically among his customers.
Adobe (ADBE): Deep Dive Interviews (March 2025)

=== PAGE 38 ===

38
Industry Contact 1 – Cont’d
Competitive Landscape (cont’d)
• In other product areas, describes competition for Adobe Target from lower-end of the market players such as Optimizely and other niche offerings.
• Expresses a positive view on HubSpot’s overall competitive positioning in the marketing technology space among midmarket customers, describing the cohesion across 
the Marketing, Sales, CMS, and Service Hubs, as well as noting the more favorable price point relative to Salesforce for “quite on par” features and functionality.
Industry Contact 2
General
• Contact represents a partner firm of Adobe, which provides consulting services across applications, though primarily focuses on the marketing and design technology 
stack.
• Contact also has exposure to Adobe as an active customer, including with core marketing technology offerings, as well as newer GenAI product sets of Adobe.
Demand Environment
• Describes that over the last “couple of quarters” and “in the last few months,” demand for his firm’s Adobe practice has been “pretty stable.”
• Shares that his firm has seen “more projects in the space now” as companies are considering evaluating more tools, though that this is partially offset by new-AI 
products leading customers to evaluate other options available in the market beyond Adobe.
• Overall, describes “pretty consistent performance” over the start of this year and the “back-end of last year,” reiterating that the last 12 months have been “stable overall.”
• Describes companies being “willing to spend a bit more on their Adobe suite”and other companies that have “reached their tipping point” and need to invest in 
upgrading their design tools, which is being sped up as a result of tools like Firefly.
• In terms of the amount of Adobe spend his firm manages across projects as a partner, believes his Adobe partner practice will see a “5-10% increase,” though notes 
that his firm tends to be “conservative” in its projections. Attributes some of this growth to his own firm scaling, as well as hisclients “spending more with Adobe” 
now than they were 24 months ago, resulting in “larger deals overall.”
• Describes the increase in spending levels on Adobe among his customers as partially driven by macroeconomic factors, noting “things aren’t quite as tough as they 
were” as of early Feb, especially in the technology industry.
• Among start-ups and scale ups in the technology space, notes that limited access to fundraising capital previously was causing every budget to be “squeezed,” and notes 
that while this “hasn’t massively improved” it has “slightly improved,” driving these companies to either invest in new tools or spend more on their existing product suite.
• At the enterprise segment, shares that as a result of the “AI element,” he is seeing more decision makers that are a “lot more keen to adopt new technologies,” 
even in “real legacy companies” where adoption rates tend to be slow as they are facing a “snowball effect” where people feel as though they are missing out if not 
spending something on an AI tool.
• As a result of these factors, shares that his firm is seeing a “little bit of a larger spend” among his customers and that companies who had“very static Adobe spend over 
long periods of time” are now willing to now “spend a bit more.”
Product Momentum
• In regard to the AI content generation landscape, does not believe there is a “huge amount of differentiation” between the productsin the market nor a “ton of 
difference” from a pure technical perspective.
• However, suggests that where “Adobe has a big advantage” is its ability to fit these tools into the Adobe ecosystem, noting that for enterprise use-cases, there is 
a lot of “comfort” that comes from using an Adobe product due to the company’s “brand recognition credibility.”
Adobe (ADBE): Deep Dive Interviews (March 2025)

=== PAGE 39 ===

39
Industry Contact 2 – Cont’d
Product Momentum
• Notes that it is “a lot easier,” for example, to sell an enterprise client on the idea of adopting Firefly over taking a “somewhat mysterious” AI company’s model, 
where questions arise as to what are you giving the model access to. Shares that Adobe is addressing “some of the needs” when it comes to which images are they 
giving the model access to and if everything is “above board” from a copyright perspective, creating“a lot more comfort with Adobe” for large-scale use cases.
• Believes that for the most part, at the moment most of these content generation products are “overselling themselves”and marketing themselves well in terms of 
what their product can really do, while it remains difficult to roll this out on a lot of large-scale use cases. However, believes many of these tools play a nice role in the 
iterative design process, including around basic editing and speeding up processes.
• From a model output perspective, does not believe that competitive models to Firefly offer much better quality output, noting that when used on a consistent basis, “you 
see errors across all of them."
• Indicates that Midjourney does a better job having a “slightly more artistic element” to its images, but that the ability to use this on a real scale is “limited” for a real design 
or marketing team. Notes that when many of these tools are rolled out on larger projects, the time it takes to resolve the errors in the content “offsets” the time the 
generator saved the user in the first place.
• Overall, at the moment does not see these content generation tools as a “real game changer”but that Adobe is “well positioned” to benefit as this technology 
improves to have it be a part of its broader ecosystem.
Competitive Landscape
• In terms of Adobe’s competitive positioning, describes Adobe as “slightly losing," mentioning that products like Canva have been “highly disruptive” for Adobe in terms of 
the low-code and low-skill approach to designing.
• Believes that “Adobe is slow to react to most things,” describing this as being evidenced by Adobe Experience Manager (AEM) and its CMS offerings.
• Suggests that the company has a strong approach from its leadership team to maintain a “high price range” and position itself as an “elite product” and that it continued to 
follow this strategy despite the landscape shifting “pretty heavily” to make design accessible to a wider user range.
• As a result of these factors, describes Adobe as “definitely having lost market share."
• Notes that the biggest change over the last five years is that it would have been “inconceivable” for an enterprise to even evaluate other tools in marketing 
design technology and that you would have just “default chosen Adobe," with an expectation that everyone you hired was an Adobe expert. However, notes that there is a 
shift in this framework, as he sees large companies “at least considering other tools even if they aren’t shifting away from Adobe."
• Shares that there isn’t quite the same “insistence” that everyone joining a design team has to be a “pure Adobe expert” and that there are teams where marketers are 
doing more with these low-skill tools, while Adobe is being used to make the finishing product for high-skilled designers.
• Notes this dynamic is impacting the whole Adobe suite of tools due to the fact that as users may in some cases see some drift from the core Adobe Creative Cloud 
ecosystem, the incentives to choose the rest of the tools are lessened due to the company’s previously advantageous “suite-based approach."
• Despite some of these concerns, indicates that he is “not too negative on Adobe” and that the company is “reacting” to match some of these low-skilled tools, but his sense 
is Adobe’s position is “weaker than it was 24-36-48 months ago."
• Shares that with a lot of his firm’s customers who evaluate other options, they “still end up sticking with Adobe," particularly enterprise users, but that they just 
want to see “what else is out there."
• Indicates that customers know they are “paying a real premium with Adobe”and often explore other options as they start to realize there are alternatives that are “viable” 
for their needs. However, shares that often times after going through evaluations and RFPs of competitive products for their customers, they ultimately “stick with Adobe 
because it is comfortable."
Adobe (ADBE): Deep Dive Interviews (March 2025)

=== PAGE 40 ===

40
Industry Contact 1
General
• Contact represents an Adobe Platinum Partner firm, with visibility across the three primary Adobe Clouds, including cross-cloud AI innovations such as GenStudio.
• Contact’s firm provides co-sell, implementation, and support services for Adobe and several other SaaS companies, with a primary focus on the large-enterprise market in 
the U.S., with some additional visibility into international market trends.
Demand Environment
• Describes that “not much has changed” in terms of the length of deal cycles and deal closures for his firm’s Adobe practice, suggesting that“trends remain quite 
stable” relative to last year.
• However, shares that there are “pockets that feel a bit better” in the U.S. but that EMEA is still in “some rough waters”as it pertains to their digital marketing spend.
• Characterizes a large portion of the revenue his firm’s Adobe practice is seeing as of recently as “maintenance work," noting a “more modest attach rate of newer 
products”; the latter of which he describes as “small things here and there."
• Suggests that net-new customers are “not necessarily leaning into mega-deals right now," however, indicates “healthy renewals and expansions” among the 
existing base of Adobe customers that his firm has worked with for years.
• On the topic of tariffs and macro uncertainty, shares that the impact has been “anecdotal” and “contained," noting that on average “the uncertainty has not been enough 
to halt deals just yet," though acknowledges a lot of unknowns exist that could change things.
• Expects Adobe to be relatively resilient to tariff and trade-war tensions as a software vendor overall, however speculates it could “perhaps alter the direction” of some 
marketing spend, which should serve as more of a “reallocation rather than reduction”in his view.
• Describes the selling environment over the last two years as “tricky," meaning the methods and language by which vendors are selling their tools “matter more than ever” to 
prove out differentiation. Building on this, suggests that customers want the ROI to be “personalized," which is leading partner firms to be ready for longer RFPs and 
“more hands-on for heavily configured implementations” for Adobe and other SaaS product-sets.
Competition
• Indicates that Adobe remains the “predominant vendor of choice” among his firm’s large enterprise customers, noting that Adobe “cements itself across the 
organization” and that this trend has “not changed much over the last year."
• Notes that for “very large multi-national companies," there may be some instances where in specific markets a team has “gone out and procured a bit of competing 
software” to use locally, but generally theenterprise-wide marketing mandate “remains with Adobe.”
• Believes that Canva’s recent price increases have created some “trepidation” at the lower end of the market, noting that customers in multiple instances have hypothesized 
that “Canva could follow the Adobe playbook of broad adoption to then monetization.”
• As a result of increased competition in the Creative Cloud space, as well as the broad “evaluation period” that has been kicked off by AI, notes that on balance “there 
has been a notable increase” in the evaluation of different digital marketing technologies over the last couple of years, and that “perhaps that will result in some 
full churn over time” off of Adobe, but as of now he sees greater likelihood of “maybe fewer dollars in pockets but not terminations.”
Broader AI Market Dynamics
• Shares that AI has caused organizations to “take a really clean look at their [tech] stack” to identify what is and isn’t needed in an effort to reallocate spend toward AI and 
identify what vendors could be “prime for consolidating toward” due to their AI offerings.
• As a result of these conversations, suggests that Adobe is “benefitting from AI consolidation tailwinds”as it provides the “most comprehensive AI stack across 
marketing workflows” even if “some of the products need work down the road to reach point-product parity.”
• Believes that the emergence of a new set of models, including DeepSeek, has actually catalyzed conversations among his customers around model ingestion and data 
retention safety standards, which in his view “plays right into the Adobe camp," as well as some other incumbent software vendors.
Adobe (ADBE): Deep Dive Interviews (June 2025)

Industry Contact 1
General
• Partner leads a boutique technology consulting practice that is focused predominantly on Cloudflare, though it works with other software companies as well.
• Business is focused on North America.
Demand Environment
• Contact comments that they have noticed a discernible, positive shift in customer “activity” while emphasizing that this hasn’t translated directly into deals or 
dollars for them just yet.
• In discussing the shift in activity, the industry expert states that during the last 12-18 months activity seemed to be at a “standstill” or moving very slowly, with significant 
caution and uncertainty.
• Now, the partner opines that activity levels have upticked and are more reminiscent of “previous [non-pandemic] times” when there was more business momentum: new 
projects are being discussed, certain projects are moving into production that were stalling before, and customers appear to have more positive tone.
• The contact places the timing of the shift in activity levels to just after the US presidential election, though they add that they don’t know whether it’s less 
uncertainty, different policies, or some other variable that led to the change in behavior.
• It is caveated that while the trend has been sustained and consistent since after the election, it will take time to see exactly how this plays out, and customers continue to 
be focused on ROI and being deliberate in their decision making.
• The contact wonders aloud whether it will take 3-6 months before the activity begins to convert in contracts and deals, implying that there may start to be more 
discernible proof points of improved customer behavior in the April-May-June time frame.
• Summing up, the partner says they feel “less scared” this year than they did at the start of last year.
Zero Trust
• It is explained that the seemingly continuous cybersecurity incidents that are occurring and occupying the headlines are “definitely” driving more interest in digital 
security generally and Zero Trust (ZT) technologies specifically, with Cloudflare benefiting from momentum in both.
• Believes that Cloudflare is seeing not only increased traction and adoption of its ZT products, but also incremental mindshare among cybersecurity buyers / stakeholders 
and resellers, which is described as essential for success within the space.
• When asked whether Cloudflare’s ZT offerings are continuing to mature and become more competitive, the industry expert opines that this is happening and has been 
continuously happening, with customers increasingly preferring Cloudflare’s products over more legacy solutions where the gap is very clear, and to a degree versus other 
modern ZT solutions as well.
• Expands that Cloudflare’s offerings benefit from a unified, organically developed platform and that customers are starting to appreciate that, in their view.
Partner Ecosystem & Move Upmarket
• Cloudflare appears to be refining its channel partnerships in ways that would (a) incent partner firms to scale up as well as pursue larger deals / customers and (b) appeal 
more to larger, global system integrators.
• This is suggested to be another step in a clear strategy of moving upmarket, gaining mindshare among larger customers, and a good seat at the table with high-level 
decision makers at customer companies.
• Emphasizes that this move is especially important for its ZT business and expects that it will become important for AI technologies as well given the interest 
the topic generates at the C-Suite / Board level, where GSIs tend to have deep relationships and can influence purchasing decisions.
• More broadly, the contact opines that Cloudflare is making many moves across its business to move upmarket and deepen its relationships with existing large customers.
Cloudflare (NET): Deep Dive Interviews (February 2025)

Industry Contact 1 – Cont’d
AI Customer Readiness
• Partner explains that while their firm hasn’t leaned into selling AI products significantly, it is a topic that comes up “everywhere” as customers inquire about it, employees at

Industry Contact 1
General
• The partner, who focuses on the APAC region, mentioned that his practice derives solely services revenue by focusing on Confluent implementations, migrations, and 
managed service work.
• Additionally, he noted that his Confluent practice is in the low seven-figure zone.
Demand Environment
• With respect to the demand environment, he noted that his Confluent practice had a slow start to 2025. Additionally, he indicated that this slow start was driven by 
geopolitical uncertainty, which caused clients to pause their spending.
• He observed a ramp in client activity for his Confluent practice since the start of June. While he isn’t yet sure what’s driving that uptick and doesn’t observe an 
improvement in the macro, he explains that “people haven’t spent their money, their budget. So, it’s a fair bit of that going on now where they’ve loaded 
themselves up for the year and now need to spend. They are looking to do cost out, to see if they can reduce cost using Confluent.”
• The partner mentioned that at the beginning of 2025, he had 20-25 employees in his practice that are specialists on Confluent but also has a broader engineering group 
that includes a few hundred employees. As of now, he has 25-30 Confluent specialists and the number of engineers remains the same.
Bridging the “Data World and Operational World,” Underscored by Databricks Relationship
• “What we are doing [with respect to his work with Confluent] is closer to our AI and data folks [in his org] and we see a convergence happening between the 
data world and operational world. In the past, they were quite separate.”
• He noted that his org has built out a “pretty strong” Databricks practice and opines that Confluent works “really well” with Databricks.
• “So, we are often finding opportunities to plug those two products [Confluent and Databricks] together as part of a broader digital transformation project for 
companies.”
• Opines that Confluent’s relationship with Databricks,especially its joint go-to-market arrangement between the two companies, is an unlock for Confluent. He adds, 
“apparently, it’s [the joint go-to-market relationship] becoming quite successful in the US.”
• The contact explained that his practice has yet to “activate” the Confluent-Databricks joint go-to-market initiative in his region, but he is on the cusp of doing so. “Confluent 
resides with one team and Databricks does with another team, and so we need to bring that together first and that’s happening soon.”
Confluent Becoming the Communication Layer
• “I absolutely think they [Confluent] can be the communication layer for this new AI stack. I don’t know who else would be.The performance and real-time aspect, 
which is key to these agentic sort of platforms, is difficult to replicate. And also, the other thing that plays into its [Confluent’s] favor is it’s not locked into a hyperscaler, 
especially if you’re building something that leverages applications across clouds.”
Partner Observes Some Marketing Challenges with Confluent Beyond Kafka
• From a marketing perspective, he wonders if Confluent will be able to differentiate from the hyperscaler offerings for streaming. More specifically, he observes that some 
customers opt into the streaming offerings from their hyperscaler of choice because it’s easy to do so and because they aren’t fully aware of everything that 
Confluent offers beyond Kafka. However, from a technical perspective, the partner opines that Confluent is more advanced than what the hyperscalers offer.
• He added ,“for people that understand Confluent and understand the pieces it’s putting together [Ex: Flink for stream processing, WarpStream], they are the sophisticated 
users. They get it. But, the majority of clients still think of Confluent as Kafka, and it’s hard to sort of just sell that. I love the product, but it’s hard to sell it as a stand-alone 
solution,” and as such, he opines that Confluent faces the challenge of communicating the full breadth of its capabilities to potential clients, beyond the association with 
Kafka.
Confluent (CFLT): Deep Dive Interviews (July 2025)

Industry Contact 1
Demand Environment
• Contact has seen “great demand” for Confluent recently, and mentions that his team just recently closed one of the largest deals, within the top 5, in Confluent 
history at a Fortune 50 company.

Large Fortune 50 Deal Closed in Q3
• “We just closed what I’ve been told isone of the largest deals, top five in Confluent history, at (a Fortune 50 Company) - a [mid-single-digit] million dollar deal.”
• “It was all open-source Kafka before. The new CIO of the company came in, moved to an offshore model, and lost a lot of good engineers - that made Confluent more 
compelling.”
• “To get into [the Fortune 50 company], first you have to get on the vendor list. Procurement there is very mature. That’s hard to do.”
• Contact notes that the deal is being hosted on Microsoft Azure and is a Confluent Cloud deal, closed at the end of Q3.
• Expects and is working on closing an additional $5M attach to this deal, expected to close in Q1.
Market Position & Competitive Landscape
• “Confluent continues to be the 800-pound gorilla in the space. There’s no competitor to it outside of its open-source Kafka.”
• He comments that his practice is also an Amazon Advanced Partner, however notes that he does not see a lot of business involving MSK (Managed Streaming for Apache 
Kafka).
• “Confluent (Kafka) in a lot of ways is just a pipe. It’s a damn good pipe - it moves data quickly in real time. Informatica made a lot of money being the same thing.”
• “When you compare to other SaaS and consumption-based models like Salesforce, Salesforce offers a zillion different reasons why you can call it a platform. 
On the other hand, Kafka is just a pipe.”
Sales Execution & Organizational Challenges
• “To sell Confluent to a customer, it’s akin to a religious experience. You’ve got to convince them and make them a believer - we call it the ‘aha moment.’ Once they get it, 
selling is downhill. But you need to build trust first.”
• Shares that Confluent is experiencing “too much churn” on the sales side currently and opines that the company cannot keep all salespeople completely 
happy.
• Many of his long-term relationships in sales at Confluent have turned over in the last 6 months, with some not being able to meet high quotas, and others leaving for 
Snowflake, Databricks, and the hyperscalers.
• “They moved to a consumption model from a license model, and you can see the fingerprints of that shift in all the different sales leaders - too much churn for 
a company that needs to be in growth mode and stable.”
Confluent (CFLT): Deep Dive Interviews (October 2025)

52
Quotable Quotes Sourced From Multiple Industry Contacts
General Thoughts and Use Cases
• (=) I’m an [executive at a foundational model company]; we started this company about 15 months ago, and we’ve been training frontier AI models, and I’ve been in charge 
of contact with all our CSPs for that entire time. [When looking for a CSP], we contacted a few other people in similar positions at similar startups, and we got positive 
feedback from people at [another AI startup] on CoreWeave. They were also excited about working with us in Europe because there aren’t that many customers here, and 
we spoke with AWS and Azure at the time, and the reason we selected CoreWeave was that we had good feedback on them, and they were at the time a smaller company, 
so we thought they would take more care of their first customers. That was 15 months ago, and since then, we’ve expanded our agreements with them and added new 
contracts because they are by far the best at what they do.
• (=) I work in the research division of [a major software provider], which is tasked with being the organic growth engine of [a major software provider] and inventing what’s 
next in IT for the organization. It’s about [a few thousand] people out of the [several hundreds of thousands] or so we have globally, and I often say I look after the IT for the 
researchers, but these days, I look after the computer infrastructure they need for their research and deal with the GPUs and ML models, including training, fine-tuning, and 
LLMs... We’ve known of CoreWeave for a while, as we’ve been in this space for some time, and we’ve invested in both [our] Cloud GPU infrastructure and our own on-
premise clusters dedicated to the research division. About last year, we started looking at where our next investments would be, and around March of last year, we began 
considering renting options and started negotiations with potential partners, and we really got to know CoreWeave well at the NVIDIA GTC conference last year, which was 
almost exactly a year ago.
• (=) I’m the VP of Finance at [a major enterprise AI company] and very involved in purchasing our compute resources for the company and also in running the finance 
function, including fundraising. We met CoreWeave when we were looking to buy some compute capacity early last year and started exploring different cloud providers, 
and we had two large cloud providers as our only sources of compute at that time and were exploring what a smaller CSP could bring to the table. Initially, we were looking 
at them for a smaller capacity related to inference, and then we expanded the relationship to see how they could help with our larger training clusters.
• (=) [Our company] makes it really easy for software developers to use AI. Think of us as a hosting platform, but at a higher level than something like CoreWeave. You can 
think of us as a layer on top of companies like CoreWeave. We interact with companies like CoreWeave by using their GPUs and wrapping them with software to make 
them easier to use.
Solutions Replaced
• (=) CoreWeave was our first cluster provider, I think we secured compute with them before having secured our seed round, and our spending has been increasing. We’ve 
mostly been making two- to three-year reservations, and recently, for one cluster, we decided to diversify a bit because there were large incentives to do this outside of 
CoreWeave; the future will tell whether it was a reasonable decision or not, but most of our compute for training today is on CoreWeave.
• (=) Let’s take it chronologically,since about 2018, we’ve been investing in [our] Research’s on-premise GPU infrastructure at relatively small levels which increased in 2021 
and 2022, and that was for the data center I run where we built a cognitive compute cluster. Around 2022, we started working with [our] Cloud, making bigger investments 
for dedicated research clusters in [our] Cloud, which they’ve also sold to other customers, and we invested heavily in H100s, with [another sum] invested in the Dallas data 
center. That was our big on-premise push in 2023 and 2024, which came online in the middle of last year, and the next phase for us is another big investment in rented 
capacity, which we’re doing with CoreWeave, and we’re working with them now, and it’s scheduled to come online in April of this year.
• (=) We have public relationships with several providers, and initially, we were training with Google using TPUs, and we were some of the largest users of TPUs a few years 
ago, and we also have a public relationship with Oracle and have used Oracle for training. CoreWeave was the third provider we did serious evaluations with, and we’ve 
evaluated multiple providers several times, and each has unique benefits and pros and cons… We started looking at other provi ders and began the normal quote discovery 
process; we were looking at A100s, which is older hardware for our inference needs at the time, and that’s how we developed a relationship with CoreWeave. The pricing 
for A100s was the most favorable, and the delivery timelines were also favorable, so we went with them for that, and it was a small purchase, but it helped us get to know 
the team better. When we looked for additional compute capacity later in the year, it was natural for us to want to expand with them, but the downside compared to our 
other compute providers is that CoreWeave requires some prepayment, which is not dissimilar to other clouds, but it was initially a barrier for us. We were able to overcome 
that based on the on-time delivery and otherwise good terms.
CoreWeave (CRWV): Deep Dive Interviews (April 2025)

Quotable Quotes Sourced From Multiple Industry Contacts – Cont’d
Solutions Replaced (cont’d)
• (=) To give you some background, we started on Google Cloud because my co-founder was from [a major music app] and used Google Cloud there. Initially, we were 
entirely on Google Cloud, which worked well from 2020 to 2022. In 2023, there was a big GPU crunch, and Google Cloud ran out of GPUs. We were forced to look 
elsewhere, and there was one particular day with a huge crunch. We called up other providers, needing hundreds of A100s that day, and CoreWeave was the only one 
who could deliver immediately. That’s why we switched a large portion of our infrastructure to CoreWeave. Since then, we’ve been getting GPUs wherever we can. For 
example, when H100s first came out, we couldn’t get them on some platforms but could on others. As capacity became available, we consolidated more on CoreWeave. 
We still have a little spread across other providers, but CoreWeave is a significant part of our infrastructure now.
ROI and Cost Savings
• (+) At least at the beginning, [CoreWeave] was usually cheaper than what we would get with the major CSPs; we can get somewhat cheaper prices with European CSPs, 
but the quality didn’t justify the price difference...It’s fully about reliability: when you launch a job on multiple thousands of GPUs, if one fails, you have to stop the 
entire thing and so having something stable for [potentially] seven days at that scale is very rare. I don’t think we’ve had that experience anywhere other than 
CoreWeave.
• (+) Last year, we had been catching up in terms of [our] Research’s needs to fulfill our ambitions around model training and where we want to be positioned as a model 
training company, specifically in generative models (series of models we’ve been open sourcing). My primary concern has been how quickly I can get technology or 
infrastructure available to the researchers, and both [our] Cloud and Oracle were able to provide us with short-term interim capacity while we waited for our on-premises 
infrastructure to come online, and this gave us exposure to the rental market and the advantages of renting capacity vs. building it ourselves. However, they were very 
expensive options when you consider the cost per GPU hour, and we didn’t have much choice last year to get that rented capacity, but it did expose us to the idea of 
renting infrastructure from others instead of building it ourselves. We decided CoreWeave was the best investment… It was clear that if we were to buy the next tranche of 
infrastructure, it would take us much longer to build it out than people who do this full-time for a living. In terms of our own data center space and purchasing power, 
building it ourselves would likely delay our full capacity by a year. A company like CoreWeave, operating at a larger scale, could provide full capacity a year in 
advance of our own build options; that was a compelling reason to go the rental route.
• (+) When considering pricing structures, we also look at ancillary costs, like the fully loaded cost of operating the cluster, and normalize that by the number of chips. We 
consider networking configurations in different clouds, as they have different topologies that affect cluster size and job performance. We do our best to normalize these 
factors across providers. We’ve evolved to think about different cloud providers for different reasons. This means normalizing by price isn’t always helpful; instead, we look 
within a use case to determine what offers good performance for cost and compare like-for-like. We’ve done comparisons of CoreWeave vs. other providers of similar 
ilk. We found that CoreWeave’s time to delivery was superior, as was the amount of capacity they had available when we needed it. While these factors are 
outside the pricing construct, they are important economic considerations. The opportunity cost of not having a cluster ready in time is significant, and 
CoreWeave really checks the box for us in this area. They were on time with our smaller cluster, on time with our larger cluster, and things are going well with 
our next cluster. These are crucial considerations beyond just the embedded networking cost.
• (+) The prices are cheaper with CoreWeave. The commitments we can get are significantly cheaper. I’m looking at similar contracts with GCP and CoreWeave, and 
CoreWeave is almost half the price, around 40% cheaper... We prefer shorter commit terms, and CoreWeave is good about that. We’ve done one-year commits and even 
as short as six months, which is really helpful for us… It’s more like 60% of the price of GCP. I think CoreWeave is accepting lower margins and they’re scrappier. There’s a 
bit of a “you don’t get fired for picking AWS” mentality, where AWS can charge more because they’re a known quantity and large companies trust them. CoreWeave isn’t 
there yet, and that’s my best guess at what’s happening.AWS margins are crazy high, around 50%, which is remarkable for selling a commodity. CoreWeave might be 
more efficient because they’re starting from scratch, whereas AWS is a large, established machine. CoreWeave can do things more efficiently because they’re scrappier.
CoreWeave (CRWV): Deep Dive Interviews (April 2025)

Quotable Quotes Sourced From Multiple Industry Contacts – Cont’d
AI Training Workloads
• (+) It’s fully for training for us today… I would say 80% [of the model training happens on CoreWeave] . We used those clusters in some capacity for all of them. Some work 
was done on other clusters, but it was a minority… [The amount of GPUs we are using from CoreWeave] depends on the clusters: We have one cluster with 5,000 and 
another with 1,000, but both are CoreWeave clusters that we use for training... [In terms of stable run time], it depends on the number of GPUs in sync, so for something 
like 4,000, two to three days is typical, and the max we’ve seen was seven days with CoreWeave... It’s less important, but the infrastructure is still nicer than others, but I 
guess it’s less critical to have something as high-quality infrastructure as CoreWeave for [training] smaller models. At the scale of 120 GPUs up to <1,000 GPUs, any 
provider would roughly work, although it might be painful, but beyond a thousand, it becomes important to have higher quality [such as CoreWeave].
• (+) We use CoreWeave for training. For us, we’re in an interesting position because we’re not only training AI but also working with [our] Cloud on other technology. 
However, [our] Cloud wasn’t in a position to act as quickly as other vendors, especially with the rapid changes in technology. NVIDIA, for example, was releasing new 
configurations or chips almost monthly, and even as we were evaluating technology in the second half of last year, it was changing almost month by month in terms of the 
configurations and the way that was sold through to NVIDIA partners. Having someone else wrangle these changes, along with data center reconfigurations, was very 
attractive to us, given our primary focus on procuring infrastructure for training models… We have about a thousand researchers working on AI , but only about 100 users 
will be on that system doing large-scale training for various model sizes. We’ll likely go up to about 450B parameters, which isn’t that large today, but as a company and 
research division, we believe smaller models are better and more commercially attractive, so we’ll focus on training smaller, industry- or task-focused models.
• (+) We use all three providers [Google GCP, Oracle OCI, and CoreWeave], butwe actively train on both [OCI and CoreWeave]. We aim to ensure our training processes 
are tightly coupled with healthy hardware and can automatically respond to changes in the underlying hardware, especially when it may fail. When you’re training on 
GPUs, you’re pushing a lot through those machines, and failure rates are higher compared to CPU-only machines, so you need a really good process for 
handling technical failures at any level of the stack, and these processes must be tightly coupled with higher-level processes to be aware of failures further 
down the stack since failures can occur in areas that are the responsibility of the CSP. We found that CoreWeave offers more automation and better integration 
for handling such failures. They are quick to provide automations or mechanisms for us to hook into, which has been beneficial. Oracle is also coming along in 
this area, but CoreWeave was a bit ahead.
• (=) It’s not that CoreWeave isn’t good at inference, butthey do training better than the hyperscalers because you can get big clusters. I’m not entirely sure because we 
don’t do any training; we merely serve other people’s models.CoreWeave does inference as well as anyone else. At the end of the day, it’s just GPUs, and whether they 
serve inference or training, it’s all the same kind of thing.
AI Inferencing Workloads
• (+) We use Azure and GCP for inferencing; it’s more flexible because it doesn’t require as much interconnection, making it easier to handle, since providing inference 
capacity is not as challenging as training. It’s the same kinds of GPUs, but they’re less densely interconnected; for training, you need a large single cluster, but for 
inferencing, you don’t need that much… I think one cluster will have 50% of our compute capacity, which will be considered to be on-prem, and then 50% on CoreWeave, 
and it’s one of the inference FLOPs… We could consider using them [for inference]: one large benefit of CoreWeave is having their platform on Kubernetes, which would
allow us to overflow whatever production we have for training clusters and potentially interrupt some less priority training tasks to serve queries. It’s not something we’ve 
needed yet, but we want to do it in the future. The flexibility is appealing before we consider a cluster fully for inferencing at CoreWeave. There are also two other strategic 
reasons of why we would want to do this, but having some inferencing capacity on major clouds helps with shield there.
• (=) It’s not for inference. Despite what NVIDIA might say, this infrastructure isn’t cost-effective for inference, the analogy I use is like running websites on mainframes—it’s 
possible, but not cost-effective because with inference, you can’t keep the machines busy enough to justify the extra cost. While they would perform well, it’s not practical… 
We’ve used older generations, from V100s to A140-180s, then H100s and H200s, and now GB200, and as people doing training move up the generations, it creates space 
at the bottom of the chain for us to do inference-type operations. Much of our development around [our GenAI model] relies more on inferencing workloads, and those 
older GPU generations have been effective in supporting that development activity while being not so cost-prohibitive, and that’s our experience. We also have our own 
development of accelerators, which we will likely use those more for inferencing workloads as we move forward.
CoreWeave (CRWV): Deep Dive Interviews (April 2025)

AI Inferencing Workloads (cont’d)
• (=) First, we were going to work with them for our inference needs. In terms of inference, it’s primarily for customer-facing inference workloads; inference is something you 
need to do heavily during internal training processes, but it’s more external-facing vs. internal workloads, like training vs. inference… In this rapidly evolving field, there’s a 
lot of specialization in newer chips. Even on the inference side, you need access to newer chips to handle workloads or take advantage of capabilities, especially 
with generative and LLM models. In the past, there were fewer hard technical requirements, like needing specialized networking for training, which wasn’t as relevant for 
inference. However, we’re now seeing techniques emerging to support larger or more complex inference workloads that do require that specialization. I see the 
need for this kind of specialization increasing on the inference side. This is driven by the growing interest in using generative AI at larger scales. From an inference 
perspective, we’re starting to utilize some of these specialized requirements and hardware setups in ways we haven’t needed in the past. It’s becoming a stronger need 
going forward. We’re starting to use some of the special technical setups on our training clusters for inference in ways we weren’t before.
• (+) Performance is less relevant for us since we’re almost entirely focused on inference workloads. CoreWeave does well with big, interconnected training clusters, which is 
harder on bigger clouds, but that’s not as important for us. For our needs, performance is more or less the same across providers… This is something we really appreciate 
about CoreWeave. The big clouds want you to commit to large blocks of GPUs, which makes sense for training workloads where you need a cluster of 10,000 GPUs for a 
set period. However, for inference workloads, demand varies by time of day and customer activity, so we prefer flexibility.
Areas for Improvement
• (+) I’m not using the full breadth of what they provide because we’re still a smaller company and don’t have extensive engineering resources, but it works so much better 
than anything I’ve tested, and that’s the only thing I care about as a customer.
• (=) If I compare them to AWS, for example, AWS has a well-specified support process and is the gold standard in training support, but we don’t have experience with 
CoreWeave’s formal support yet, but what we’ve seen is good support from people up to the top for all the stuff that we needed. I don’t feel at risk because there’s no 
formally documented support mechanism, but in terms of company maturity and offering maturity, that’s a difference I’ve noticed compared to larger companies like AWS or 
Oracle… In terms of upgrades, three out of the four vendors, including CoreWeave, offered upgrade options, but CoreWeave was slightly behind AWS, which guaranteed 
[GPU] upgrades regardless vs. CoreWeave would provide an upgrade if they could find another buyer for the existing [GPU] technology, which introduces a bit more risk in 
the upgrade path for us… Our experience with these vendors, and running our own infrastructure, is that it’s tough, there are many variables that make things difficult to 
track down when something doesn’t work.Software platforms that claim to do what SageMaker claims, or what CoreWeave might claim, haven’t proven themselves yet, 
and my intuition is that it won’t be as great as it sounds for CoreWeave, and these capabilities aren’t something we’d pay extra for.
• (=) Oracle has a very large, mature support organization with specialized groups in various areas vs. CoreWeave is smaller, which comes with pros and cons. We found 
that in some scenarios, a smaller engineering organization like CoreWeave’s can better understand our needs compared to a larger, more mature organization. For 
production processes, larger, more mature organizations are better for critical production needs. However, for more bleeding-edge research needs, CoreWeave’s smaller 
engineering organization and their position higher up the stack meet our needs better, but it’s a mix of what we’re trying to achieve.
• (=) CoreWeave has been more unreliable than GCP, but that’s a tradeoff we accept because they’re a relatively small, growing company. We trade off price for 
scrappiness. The advantage is that when something breaks on GCP, it’s a “Kafkaesque experience” trying to get it fixed. We had to involve our VCs to contact Google 
Cloud execs for help, and it felt like they showed contempt for the customer. In contrast, with CoreWeave, if something breaks, we have the engineer fixing it in the Slack 
channel, and we’re talking directly to them.Things might break more often, but they get fixed faster, and it’s much more pleasant to interact with them. That’s the trade off 
we prefer as a startup, and it works very well for us.
CoreWeave (CRWV): Deep Dive Interviews (April 2025)

Quotable Quotes Sourced From Multiple Industry Contacts – Cont’d
Comparison to Hyperscalers (Amazon AWS, Google GCP, Microsoft Azure, Oracle OCI)
• (+) We’ve used Azure for training, we haven’t used GCP or AWS for training; we’ve used GCP for inference only, we haven’t used AWS at all. At the time of [initial 
consideration], the closest competitor was AWS, but I think it would have been a very wrong choice for us. The H100 was fairly recent, and I had feedback from friends 
at Meta who were using the H100 on AWS around the same time we would have gotten them, and they were not usable or stable without significant engineering 
effort. With CoreWeave, it just works and we were able to start immediately. Azure is the most stable of the competitors; it’s quite well built, buton Azure, I would 
criticize the management stack, which is outdated and painful, as well as storage, which didn’t work well and was too expensive… I don’t know if the pure measure of 
expected time to failure is enough; it’s also about automatic detection and quick replacement of failed systems… For the bigger players, I’m honestly surprised they don’t 
do it as well today; I don’t think it’s really their focus vs.CoreWeave is focused on providing good training infrastructure, and Azure, AWS, and GCP haven’t been 
able to provide this reliably… I think they’ve [CoreWeave] got a large edge there which lets them build expertise to always be the first at achieving stable 
deployment. I don’t think the largest CSPs really want to replicate what CoreWeave provides, or at least I haven’t seen much interest in it.
• (+) We evaluated several vendors, including AWS, Lambda, Oracle, and CoreWeave… I believe CoreWeave excels because they’ve dedicated themselves to this specific 
task, so when you focus on doing one thing well, you tend to do it expertly and you don’t need huge teams to achieve that; they have enough people to do it effectively. I 
can’t speak for Google, as I haven’t used their services, butfor AWS and other cloud providers, the architecture decisions they’ve made for general-purpose 
computing carry over into their GPU provisioning. For example, Oracle uses specialized networking that requires constant redevelopment, and as we use their 
software, we find gaps that need to be addressed by their network team, which adds overhead (like connection to their billing system), so this complexity can 
hinder straightforward implementation of what has been tested by NVIDIA themselves, requiring extra work.
• (=) We were on [GCP] TPUs and then made a strategic decision to diversify to H100s with NVIDIA, and we held a procurement process at the time, and we evaluated six 
different companies and got proposals, which led us to choose Oracle initially. When we decided to harmonize around NVIDIA after moving away from TPUs, we had 
another process and chose CoreWeave, and we’ve considered pretty much everyone, including Microsoft, AWS, and NVIDIA themselves, in terms of options. When we 
decided first for OCI and then for CoreWeave we conducted a broad evaluation with quite a few providers and continued to do so. We are actively training with both and 
have experience going into production on multiple clouds… With OCI, it was a three-year commitment, and we contracted with CoreWeave a year into that commitment, so 
there were still two years left, and we expanded quickly… Strategically, we like the idea of not having all our eggs in one basket and having some diversification in our 
supplier base. This is different from some competitors who have taken large checks from one cloud provider to be exclusive. It comes down to performance and finding the 
best compute provider for training and inference.
• (=) We struggled with Google Cloud because, as a scrappy startup, we felt like a tiny fish dealing with the giant machine that is Google. Even though we were spending 
millions a month, we didn’t feel like they cared about us. In contrast, with CoreWeave, we have a Slack channel with their engineers and co-founders, and we get incredible 
responses. It feels like we’re interacting with a fast-moving startup, which is very helpful and less stressful. Last year, we moved a large portion of our workloads from 
Google Cloud to CoreWeave. We had a commitment ending on Google Cloud and chose to move to CoreWeave partly because of the price, but also because, given 
equal pricing, we’d much rather work with CoreWeave. They care about us and are easier to deal with than large corporations… They’re very flexible, unlike GCP and 
AWS, which are unwilling to commit to less than two years. As a fast-growing startup, it’s hard to commit for that long, especially with changing models and GPU needs.
Comparison to Smaller/Niche Cloud Service Providers
• (+) We’ve tested a few smaller CSPs like Scaleway, FluidStack, Nebius, and Sesterce… On smaller clouds like Scaleway and others, stability is less than a day, and we 
would worry about things completely breaking overnight, wasting hours because no one would be there to fix it… With CoreWeave , we’re confident that we can 
go to sleep and the jobs will still be running in the morning, which is rare otherwise because we don’t know what kind of “hell can be unleashed during the 
night”… The smaller players lack the scale that CoreWeave benefits from, and the scale at which CoreWeave operates lets them discover and learn a lot about 
the hardware, giving them an edge in terms of knowledge of normal operation vs. unhealthy… [For our on- prem clusters], we have another company FluidStack that 
handles program management and cluster operations for us. We own the chips, and a company similar to CoreWeave but smaller operates the cluster, but we might also 
do some operations ourselves.
• (+) We evaluated several vendors, including AWS, Lambda, Oracle, and CoreWeave… Certainly between CoreWeave as the earliest deliverer and the last deliverer 
[among the other vendors], there was about 6 months in delay, so that’s significant for us, and there was a spread where CoreWeave was the best price and the 
next best price would be 150% of that, quite a big spread. If you were to do it as a cluster chart, you would have CoreWeave, then a close follower, somebody halfway 
down and then the laggard.
CoreWeave (CRWV): Deep Dive Interviews (April 2025)
