Comparison to Smaller/Niche Cloud Service Providers (cont’d)
• (=) We met CoreWeave when we were looking to buy some compute capacity early last year and started exploring different cloud providers, and we had two large cloud 
providers as our only sources of compute at that time and were exploring what a smaller CSP could bring to the table... There are many cloud providers moving quickly in 
different dimensions. Networking is crucial for training at scale, as a fast network makes a big difference. Machine colocation is important for capacity and performance. 
Storage is also critical. The generation of hardware and who rolls it out fastest is key. CoreWeave has been great at being first to market with next-generation 
hardware, which we really like. Other companies are also building large clusters.
• (+) We also work with smaller providers when necessary, like when we needed H100s. The trade-off between providers is that big, reliable hyperscalers like AWS are 
known for their reliability and trustworthiness, which is important for sensitive workloads. On the other end, small startups might offer dirt-cheap prices but 
could be unreliable and lack support and compliance guarantees. CoreWeave is a sweet spot in the middle. They might not be as reliable as the big clouds, and we 
struggled with reliability in the early days, but they’re growing fast and offer better support and flexibility. They’re also grown-up enough to have reasonable security and 
compliance, like SOC 2, which smaller providers might not have. They strike a balance between big clouds and tiny clouds… We’ ve talked to a bunch, like FluidStack and 
Vast.ai. I believe we’ve used Lambda at some point. Lambda is more in the same spectrum as CoreWeave, while FluidStack and Vast.ai are smaller. We’ve been talking to 
them and have used them at some point.
Implementation
• (=) We had to rebuild our entire stack, but it worked mostly out of the box with the usual tools; there were no surprises. It might be more complex depending on how pre-
made the tools are and how much adaptation is needed, but we just use PyTorch and standard open-source stacks, so there were no difficulties for us. We have about 100 
users on the training platform, which is quite low; for most of those 15 months, we were below 30 users - I don’t think that was the most complex part. One challenging 
aspect was the mix of CPU-heavy and GPU-heavy jobs, which was new for CoreWeave; I think we were their first customers doing this.
• (+) We signed the deal at the beginning of this year, in January. The procurement process at [our company] can be a struggle, but we started the contractual process with 
CoreWeave in November, and we were always looking at an April deadline for the delivery of this technology. I’ve dealt with sales forces in the past, like Salesforce, which 
can be very intrusive, and CoreWeave’s approach wasn’t like that; we were in daily touch with [the company] throughout the process, and everyone we needed was always 
available, it was a good cadence, not to the point of being annoying. My experience with the sales team was positive; in a good selling organization like Salesforce, they 
remove blockers before you even know you have them, and CoreWeave wasn’t as organizationally connected, but Max [CoreWeave employee name] was very good at 
building relationships with my senior stakeholders. The CEO Mike came in to talk to the head of research and made convincing presentations to the SVPs, so it was good 
and sufficient, but not annoying, if that makes sense.
• (+) Our contract with CoreWeave offers helpful flexibility to both upgrade and expand. Upgrading means moving to the next technology, and expanding means increasing 
our holdings. I was discussing this with them yesterday, considering which option makes the most sense based on available capacity. From a contractual standpoint, we 
have that flexibility, and we intend to use it.
• (=) Implementation went smoothly. As a scrappy startup, we’ve grown up a bit but still need to move quickly and adapt. CoreWeave has been great in meeting our needs… 
I think the way to understand this is by considering the scale and level of sophistication of customers. If you’re a startup or an R&D lab inside a Fortune 500 company, it 
can be complex to set up on CoreWeave because you need specialist knowledge about booting models and infrastructure. Those customers might use [our platform] to 
simplify the process. As they grow, they might stick with [our company]. However, more sophisticated or larger customers might go directly to CoreWeave because they 
have the expertise to manage their infrastructure. They operate at a scale where it makes sense to work directly with a lower-level provider. An analogy might be Stripe, 
which is high-level and easy for startups, while a company like Amazon might work directly with banks due to their scale.
CoreWeave (CRWV): Deep Dive Interviews (April 2025)

Differentiators
• (+) Building these training clusters and making them available, the main thing people want is stability, and the feedback we received was that with CoreWeave, 
we wouldn’t have to worry about the infrastructure and it would be stable and just work, which is a dream in that world. With other providers, it would require a lot 
more engineering work on our end to manage the infrastructure. One aspect of CoreWeave’s offering that I didn’t fully appreciate at the time is that their 
management stack is more modern than other competitors, making it really nice to work with… The fact that it’s a good experience and stable is the most important 
factor, and I don’t see a world in which CoreWeave can be compared in terms of quality with other similar providers… These systems are complex, and there 
are lots of little breaks that can go wrong, and we’ve seen these breaks with other providers: we’ve seen storage fail, authentication issues, and GPUs not 
performing as expected with other providers, but none of that has happened with CoreWeave.
• (+) We considered several factors in our decision; price was about 50% of the consideration, as you can imagine, given the price sensitivity of this decision, and another 
important factor was the commitment to an NVIDIA reference architecture, which reflects an “invested-ness” in the technology itself. We also looked at the potential for 
partnership, evaluating whether the company we chose would be a good partner for us in terms of general support and developing s oftware, as well as using some of our 
hardware to accelerate AI training as an industry practice in the future, and the upgrade path was another critical aspect, as we evaluated whether vendors could offer 
upgrades during the contract term. In all these categories, CoreWeave stood out, but in terms of price, they were slightly ahead of the next competitor; with technology, 
they demonstrated an implementation of the reference architecture, which gave us confidence that the technology would work and that we could use industry-standard 
software to train models on it. Regarding partnerships, CoreWeave was very interested in working with our full stack, including the OpenShift AI stack. Interestingly, they 
were also open to and going to take [our] storage as part of our custom configuration, since we feel that we have a good offering in the storage space for AI training and 
we’ve used that ourselves internally and externally and felt that it was because it worked very well and they were one of the two partners that were interested, and they 
were the most open to using our storage as part of this custom configuration.
• (+) From the beginning, it was clear they had a very strong technical team, which was great, and we’ve enjoyed working with them from that perspective. It’s been a 
bit different from larger providers, with pros and cons to each, and CoreWeave operates at a fast pace and likes to be directly connected with engineers on the other 
side, which has been a standout feature. They manage their compute in a modern, technical way, with automation and integration that makes it easy to connect 
with our systems, so we don’t require as many engineers to manage a large amount of compute, as they take on some of that burden. This means the teams 
engaging with CoreWeave don’t have to be as large or have as much expertise across the board… On the CoreWeave side, when we need the latest generation of 
technology or hardware and are rapidly iterating, working with their support team is easier. We can directly interface with their engineering teams, which are smaller and 
more focused. This allows for a broader context when working with one group of engineers, as they have an understanding of the whole picture. In contrast, larger 
enterprise groups may focus on specific areas, which can be limiting when you need to address multiple technical areas. It’s beneficial to work in both dimensions, 
depending on the type of project and the need for broader context, which tends to be easier with CoreWeave.
• (+) This is something we really appreciate about CoreWeave. The big clouds want you to commit to large blocks of GPUs, which makes sense for training workloads where 
you need a cluster of 10,000 GPUs for a set period. However, for inference workloads, demand varies by time of day and customer activity, so we prefer flexibility. 
CoreWeave allows us to commit to a significant amount of GPUs but also burst on top of that using their available capacity. This flexibility is crucial for us, as we don’t have 
to commit to using a fixed amount… We talk to [CoreWeave] almost every day. For example, today we asked for a minor feature request, and they replied immediately, 
showing us how to do it. Yesterday, they proactively informed us about a problem with one of our clusters. Last week, they told us about another issue. It’s like every other 
day we’re in contact with them, and they’re very responsive.
CoreWeave (CRWV): Deep Dive Interviews (April 2025)

Integrations
• (+) The SUNK product is very useful to us; I think they were the first to implement it, and it has become popular, their implementation is the best today, although I haven’t 
tested many others. They provide a lot of pre-built monitoring on Grafana, which is great because building good monitoring is very difficult.
• (=) The simple answer is that we intend to use as much of CoreWeave’s stack as possible; where there are gaps, we’ll work with them, as they have plans to open 
source parts of their stack. Currently, we don’t see many gaps, and we’ll manage our own storage because we’re using something that’s not their strategic storage offering 
at the moment, we hope it will be, but it’s not yet. The hope is to run with most of what CoreWeave offers because we benefit from them managing node fallouts 
and bringing in buffer. One reason for choosing rental is that when you own the infrastructure, you have to maintain the buffer yourself; renting from AWS, CoreWeave, or 
others means they maintain the buffer, providing an extra 10-20% capacity. We aren’t expecting to use much of that as we possibly can, so far, we’ve not seen a reason 
not to do that. If necessary, we could strip everything back and use our own stack, as we do with our on-premise solution, but we don’t see enough advantage in doing that 
at the moment.
• (=) CoreWeave’s hyper-specialization in these areas aligns with our needs for leading-edge capabilities in GPU training, inference, and production workloads. When 
compared to other providers, there are some limitations. For example, CoreWeave doesn’t provide managed database services, and from conversations with [CoreWeave], 
it’s not something they plan to offer in the near term. Their focus is on providing a top-notch experience with GPUs. For us, CoreWeave is a go-to provider when we need 
specialized GPU capabilities. We consider them heavily for new projects because of their expertise. However, they don’t offer every service across the stack, so we use 
them in concert with other providers. They’ve done well in helping us operate across multiple clouds, ensuring that their tooling for observability, log management, and data 
transfer works seamlessly with our other providers. We’re currently working on an experimental project with CoreWeave to access and load data stored in another cloud 
provider, making it quickly accessible in a cost-effective way for workloads running in the CoreWeave cloud. This project has been going well, and we appreciate their 
innovation and engagement.
• (=) [Switching costs are] relatively small. We actually got set up with CoreWeave in a matter of days. It’s about setting up all our internal services on a new provider. Our 
software layers are containerized, so it’s relatively easy to get set up. The challenge is dealing with any quirks in the infrastructure that might differ from another provider or 
adapting storage systems that behave differently. We need to create a shim to make it work with a new provider. It’s not a huge effort, just a couple of days for our small 
team, but it’s not trivial either.
CoreWeave (CRWV): Deep Dive Interviews (April 2025)

60
Industry Contact 1
General
• Contact is a technology consultant with a focus on Datadog as well as other Observability players, with insight across the infrastructure and infrastructure-adjacent software 
markets.
• Contact primarily works with enterprise-grade customers.
Demand
• Contact communicates that the flow of work for Datadog has remained consistent and at a high level overall, without significant disruptions or fluctuations in the recent 
past.
• “There is [y/y] growth in every Datadog contract that I see.”
• The industry expert explains that they do see leading modern solutions, such as Datadog, Dynatrace, Grafana, as well as Splunk in some cases, gaining market share.
• Adds that in nearly all situations, the market share gain occurs when customers migrate or bring online new workloads, while existing workloads for incumbents remain 
very sticky.
• Emphasizes that despite the displacements, most of the work that they do is with existing customers and expansions, which aligns with Datadog’s strategy of landing small 
and expanding across departments and products.
• Beyond infrastructure monitoring, which is Datadog’s most commonly used product, customers leverage logging, user monitoring, and database monitoring.
Cloud Migrations
• When asked whether they’ve seen any changes to the environment since the US election, the contact opines that they “haven’t seen anything change in the actual 
implementations or migrations” since the election or any of the related events.
• Does add that what they have in fact noticed non-US customers becoming more “skittish” and contemplating moving their cloud environment to non-US providers, though 
nothing has come of any of these discussions yet and the options for attractive alternatives are limited.
• Notes that this discussion on behalf of non-US customers seeking non-US software is more focused on hyperscaler exposure, vs. other software providers such as 
Datadog.
• Speaking more broadly in terms of cloud migrations, the contact comments that lift-and-shifts have become far less common because “you don’t get any benefit of just 
moving a workload to the cloud [and doing nothing else]."
AI Workloads
• It is explained that when looking at Observability within the AI landscape, it is helpful to split it into two different markets.
• The first is Observability that is targeted at AI development teams that are working on AI training, testing, or other aspects of the development lifecycle.
• The second is when the AI is put into production and the workloads can be, for all intents and purposes, treated like any other traditional workloads.
• Comments that Datadog is more tailored to serving the second part of the AI Observability market (i.e. production / inference workloads).
• Hypothesizes that over the next year, Observability companies that are tailored to AI developers will see significant growth.
• Over 2-3 years, expects that the focus will shift to the production side of the AI equation, which will mean that players like Datadog will start seeing more 
scaled traction with AI workloads.
• Notes that they do have clients who are using Datadog for AI, but not many, and this is purely driven by the fact that so few companies have AI tools in production at 
scale.
• Believes there will be significant opportunity for AI-related Observability over time.
Datadog (DDOG): Deep Dive Interviews (May 2025)

Security
• Comments that one notable product family they don’t see very frequently for Datadog? is security.Opines that the driver of this is the fact that most customers do

• The teams who oversee security purchasing tend to stick to the well-known security vendors, such as CrowdStrike and Fortinet [please note these are covered by JPM 
Security Software Analyst Brian Essex].
• When asked about whether the opposite scenario applies, in other words security vendors are encroaching on the Observability space, the industry expert states that while 
some security vendors have tried to move into the space, “they have not been successful [getting into the Observability space]." 
• Roughly speaking, less than 10% of the contact’s customers have Security as a significant portion of their total spend with Datadog. 
Hyperscaler Competition
• The industry expert does believe that the hyperscalers are a source of real competition for Datadog, commenting that most customers use native hyperscaler Observability 
tools in their respective cloud ecosystems.
• Highlights Amazon as one of the hyperscalers that is relatively more focused on Observability and typically invests more to build out its offerings.
• However, the contact explains that most customers prefer to have some significant portion of their Observability operations structured so that it sits above / outside of their 
cloud environment.
• “There’s a little bit of concern for a lot of companies, ‘who watches the watchers’?… [as well as] if your monitoring system is built into the [hyperscaler’s] 
cloud… [what happens] if the cloud goes down for whatever reason?”
Partner Channel Ecosystem
• The contact observes that Datadog has one of the least-developed partner ecosystems in the marketplace considering Datadog’s breadth and size.
• Suggests that part of the issue is that so much of Datadog’s products are i.e. low touch and easy to use, there is less need for partner involvement vs. other vendors.
• Does see it as a bit of an inhibitor on the Security side for Datadog given so much security purchasing by customers is done via channel partners.
Competition
• In terms of competition, Dynatrace [please note this is covered by JPM SMID Cap Software Analyst Pinjalim Bora] is seen as the most comparable / common direct 
competitor; also calls out Grafana as continuing to gain traction with customers.
• Other notable companies called out include Splunk, Honeycomb, and Chronosphere.
Other
• Does believe there is room to evolve Datadog markets and structures its contracts for large enterprise customers, noting that often Datadog will sell based on list price and 
discounts for individual products vs. the more traditional enterprise approach of discounting based on TOTAL spend across all products.
Datadog (DDOG): Deep Dive Interviews (May 2025)

Industry Contact 1
General
• The customer is an F150 company.
• The contact has been managing the organization’s Observability deployment for the entire company for just under five years.
Dive into Observability Environment
• Mentions that when he stepped into his role of managing the Observability environment for the company, there were various tools being used such as New Relic, Sumo 
Logic, InfluxDB, Grafana, SignalFX, InfluxDB, Instana, among many others. His team went through “a journey to see what the best solutions” for his company’s needs 
were. Ultimately, he chose Datadog to help consolidate the number of tools being used for logs, traces, infrastructure monitoring, and application performance 
monitoring.
• Additionally, explains that his company uses Datadog for cost-performance scale needs, primarily for metrics and traces use-cases. In some cases, his company uses it for 
logs, but it’s not the most common logging solution.
• Indicates that his organization primarily uses a mix of Splunk and homegrown solutions for log management. Additionally, the customer notes that he migrated some of the 
log use-cases from Sumo Logic and InfluxDB into Splunk for logging.
• Notes that his company generates a few petabytes (PB) of logs per day and none of the commercially available solutions are able to handle all of this data for his 
company, thus his company relies on homegrown solutions.
• For application security, the contact uses Splunk but he is evaluating alternatives.
Datadog Accounts for Half of the Observability Budget
• Notes that his company’s total annual Observability budget is in the high-8 figure zone and Observability accounts for ~5% of the annual IT budget.
• The customer indicates that his company’s spend withDatadog comprises ~50% of the total Observability budget. The remaining amount includes Splunk, homegrown 
solutions, and a few other tools.
• “Datadog gave us a lot of visibility we didn’t have with APM Real User Monitoring (RUM), and AI workloads. These are things that would take us years to build 
in-house and would require a lot of focus and attention. But, then there’s a lot of things we don’t need to use. We could divide the use-cases into tier one, tier two, and 
tier three. Maybe we can move some of the tier two or tier three use-cases out of Datadog into something else.”
• “We do control some of the costs [for Datadog] by doing some of the sampling processes for traces where we sample the traces very aggressively and are pushing only 
less than 10% of the trace data into Datadog. We keep the homegrown solution to store some of these traces. It’s not going to have all of the bells and whistles of 
Datadog, but provides us all of the data that we need.”
Growth of Cloud Spend to be Greater Than Observability Spend
• His company hasn’t fully migrated from on-premise to cloud and as a result, he believes that his company’s spend on cloud hyperscalers will grow faster than his 
spend on Observability for the next five years.
Datadog (DDOG): Deep Dive Interviews (August 2025)

Exploring Other Tools, Consolidation
• “Dynatrace is making big bets on log management, right? That’s where they are putting a lot of the efforts,which is true because logs is where you have most of the

• He explains that his company spoke with Datadog about handling more of the log management data generated, but at the time, Datadog indicated that they couldn’t

in position to take share from Splunk and the homegrown solution.
• “That [Datadog being used for a larger portion of log management] gives us a one-stop shop for everything Observability, that is always the goal.”
• While the customer is continuously evaluating ways to replace Splunk with Datadog for security, his company has built many “homegrown frameworks” that integrate into 
Splunk, which makes it harder to rip and replace.
• At the same time, he opines that there are other security vendors in the space that are “moving at a faster pace” than Datadog. For example, he mentions 
Anvilogic, Databricks [data platform with security use-cases], and Chronicle [Google]. Additionally, he opines that “Elastic was actually pretty good in their 
security offerings, and their on-prem. So, Elastic stood out as a close second competitor at that point of time.”
• When asked if Datadog could be disrupted or see greater competition from a Snowflake or Databricks, the customer opines that it is theoretically possible. More 
specifically, he notes that Observability data is still somewhat structured and with OpenTelemetry standards, there is a data collection, processing, and storage process. He 
adds, “I mean you can push in a lot of different data within OpenTelemetry as well, but it’s in a structured fashion.”
• In explaining why his firm uses a range of solutions including Datadog, other third parties, and homegrown solutions, the partner notes “Datadog may be a Ferrari, the cost 
and the bells and whistles and everything, but do you necessarily always need a Ferrari for your day-to-day going to work? I mean, perhaps someone would drive a Civic 
that is durable and still give you everything you need, but not with all the bells and whistles.”
AI Impact on the Observability Space
• On the AI front, explains that his company is integrating some of its AI models with Datadog. More specifically, Datadog is providing Observability capabilities 
for all types of LLMs in his organization and he indicates that this is a growing area. He adds “in the last couple of months, that has picked up a lot more.”
• With respect to the Observability space long-term, the customer opines that engineers, product managers, and technical teams will focus on the most optimal way to store 
data and may not necessarily “go out of their ecosystem, whether it’s cloud, Visual Studio, etc.” He believes that AI agents will be deployed everywhere and users of 
Observability tools can “just talk to their agent, which will then in turn find all the information that they’re looking for and present it to them.”
• “AI is going to challenge the Observability leaders, like how they are innovating and they have to bring in AI natively, otherwise somebody else will.”
Datadog (DDOG): Deep Dive Interviews (August 2025)

General

Uses Datadog Basic Features Only, For Now

• “I think we use Datadog conservatively. We only use the basic features.”
• Indicates that 5-10% of his company’s Infrastructure budget is dedicated towards Observability. His company’s Infrastructure budget accounts for almost entirely all of its

• “Our spending [with Datadog] is still growing. It’s just linearly with our cloud usage.”
• His company doesn’t use Datadog for security because it’s too expensive from their perspective. He notes that there are other options available in the market that have 
similar services to what Datadog provides.
• The customer is familiar with Datadog features like Flex Logs and Database monitoring, but is not currently using them.
• Notices that vendors, such as Datadog, are racing to add AI-driven features such as an AI site reliability engineer (SRE) [likely a reference to Bits AI SRE] for automatic 
root-cause analysis.
Opines That the Largest Compute-Scale AI Companies Like OpenAI Could Conceivably Be Motivated to Replicate Certain Observability Features In-House
• When asked if OpenAI has the capability to reduce its dependence on Datadog with an in-house Observability solution, the contact seems to have some familiarity with the 
situation and, as such, references what is known publicly and relays some of the high-level industry logic we might consider. Along these lines, he discusses how OpenAI 
acquired Rockset, which is a real-time analytics database. He adds that “oftentimes, a lot of these databases and large warehouses like ClickHouse, try to support a lot of 
Observability use-cases,” and thus it is conceptually doable.
• At the same, he mentions how it also depends on the personnel and engineering talent, stating that “IF OpenAI decides to go that route, it’s [conceivably] do-able, 
HOWEVER, it’s more about the HR hours and Engineering hours and those aspects” which might be the gating factors.
• Observes that Rockset (before being acquired by OpenAI) and ClickHouse have already started exploring capabilities to optimize query patterns and these efforts focus 
mainly on the ingestion layer.
Open-Source Can Be Expensive and Not as Reliable
• He opines that building an Observability system that supports open-source protocols or standards like OpenTelemetry isn’t hard. However, it’s more difficult to have high 
reliability, consistency, and durability, using open-source protocols, which matters more.
• Highlights that APM is an expensive area of Observability spend and his company decided to adopt OpenTelemetry standards, but even found that to be too costly. He 
adds “we can ingest information and provide visualizations and interpretations for customers [using OpenTelemetry]. So, we started doing that in terms of APM...But then 
we realized APM was really expensive so we stopped using that.”
Datadog (DDOG): Deep Dive Interviews (August 2025)

back end.”

the sales cycle he typically observes.

Observability Is Still Relatively New in APAC

longest and as such, he continues to lean into Dynatrace as the “go-to” solution.

tools yet or at least the best tools, so I think there’s room for growth” [for his Observability practice, in general].
• Mentions that Datadog is a newer entrant in APAC, at least for his practice. “They [Datadog] are just starting to build a presence. The current portfolio and customer base is 
limited.”

General

monitoring. The contact helps lead the alliance with Observability vendors such as Dynatrace, Datadog, BigPanda, and Splunk, among others.

Demand Environment
• Heading into 2025, the partner expected his Observability and DevOps practice to grow 8-9% y/y, while targeting 16% y/y growth for Dynatrace and 12% y/y 
growth for Datadog.
• Despite the macro uncertainty, the partner has not seen a slowdown in spend on Observability because it remains a prioritized category of the overall CIO 
software budget.
• The partner highlights one of his hospitality clients - “One of the most important functions is booking a room, right? Now what they’re [the Hospitality company] looking at is 
the business transaction, or the end-to-end business flow of what does it take for me when I go into their website and say, ‘I’m going to book a hotel room.’ If that operation 
fails, they might as well shut down the company. So, Observability is becoming not just this generic, ‘tell me everything that is happening everywhere,’ which is wonderful, 
but it’s really looking at critical business functions that they can say ‘this is where I need help.’”
• While the partner explains that he is on track to achieve his 2025 growth rate targets, he has seen deals being pushed out particularly in the federal sector, but not 
enough for him to reduce his annual growth targets.
• The partner observes that stricter US federal requirements are making it somewhat harder for international vendors to sell into the US, lengthening sales 
cycles.
• When asked what his thoughts are on the overall budget environment for Observability solutions in the next twelve months, the partner mentions how he expects it to 
remain strong and underscores this by mentioning how he closed a Dynatrace deal with a customer [non-public sector] in Q2 that will commence in the 1H of 2026. 
Additionally, this client is reducing the number of Observability tools from 15 to , which will include Dynatrace and Datadog [each serve different areas of the 
organization].
• Mentions that he is working with a multi-national hedge fund and financial services company on “very interesting” work around Observability for trading.
Dynatrace Preferred for Hybrid Cloud Environments
• Clients mention to him that Dynatrace is easy to use for hybrid cloud environments. The partner describes how enterprises that have both a cloud and on-premise 
deployment need Observability capabilities that traverse on-premise and cloud. As such, a company can then look at the “entire business transaction” regardless of 
whether it happens on the cloud, on-premise, and you can track it across the board.
• He opines that open-source tools like Grafana, Prometheus, Cerebro, Telegraph, and others “don’t meet the mark” when it comes hybrid cloud deployments.
Ecosystem Is Maturing
• The contact indicates that the early adopters of Observability tools initially focused on leveraging monitoring capabilities, which were deployed as a small instance for a 
specific application or infrastructure. But, as the ecosystem matured and with the advent of DevOps as well as GitOps, clients started talking more about bringing in 
metrics, logs, and traces together in a single pane of glass.
• Indicates that IT systems have gotten more complex, which has raised the bar for companies meeting service level objectives. More specifically, the partner notes that 
“organizations have trouble with scaling, and that is a primary reason driving big demand right now [for Observability tools in general].”
• As an example, he describes how he has one enterprise customer in the Fast Food industry that has tens of thousands of retail stores, including hundreds of thousands of 
point of sales systems. As that customer has increased the scale of their operations as well as systems and transactions they monitor, their use of tools like Dynatrace and 
Datadog have naturally increased over time.

Quotable Quotes Sourced From Multiple Industry Contacts
General Thoughts and Use Cases
• (+) Why do we use Figma? It scales design, and when you make thinking visual, you can explore ideas really fast without having to write code. You save a lot of effort. The 
worst thing is having an engineer work on something and then find out that it isn’t a market fit or find out that it doesn’t have the right usability or considerations for who’s 
using it, you’ve wasted a lot of time and money. Whereas with Figma, you can explore ideas really fast and throw them away, get a really good effect, which is a really 
realistic experience, an interactive prototype that someone can click and feels real and is really high fidelity, and then test it with real people or show it to stakeholders so 
they can imagine a possible future, and then not waste a lot of time and resources and very expensive development time. So ultimately, you’re speeding up a lot, and that’s 
the value that, whether a product designer or a UX designer using tools like these, that’s what it results in.
• (+) Our management said ‘user experience is really important’ and so they brought in UX designers and then we got introduced to Figma. I would say other teams in our 
space may not yet have it, but you can definitely see the difference in apps, for example. I know there’s some other teams building Pegasystems workflows and they don’t 
have a UX designer, but if I were to show you some of our apps, you would probably see the differences. If we can make apps that look as good as tech (apps made by the 
central IT team), then it makes us a little more usable. We’re not part of the technology organization. We don’t follow SDLC [software development lifecycle]. We follow 
more citizen developer route.
• (+) The first time I came across Figma was with my previous employer... And we were looking at capabilities that were really coming primarily from Sketch, which is the tool 
that was popularized maybe four or five years ago, and Figma just kind of really used this multi-platform story where they had web browser and a lot of collaboration and 
also these kind of native applications as well. And I think that was the kind of first compelling story that brought us over to start looking into Figma.
• (+) Majority of users are marketing but we also have an internal operations side that also uses Figma. It’s just very different, like working on internal apps for our factory, it’s 
just a different type of user, but they’re using it as well. 50% of the corporate office that’s using Figma, the majority of those users are marketing just because that’s where 
the most head count is, and then all developers, and all creatives are using it as well.
• (+) (Talking about penetration into the customer’s company) Just looking at our numbers, it’s very high level ranges.We probably have I want to say 40%, maybe 50% of 
our corporate office on Figma and using it, not just viewing it. All of our developers are using it, the creative team is a big bulk, they’re all using it too, and then our 
acquisition/retention team they’re all also using it.
• (+) I think that I’ve been using Figma and my teams have been using Figma since Figma launched, and I think that the widespread adoption, like wall-to-wall within the 
design team, was maybe within a year and a half of when Figma launched, but they were kind of on our radar really early. That community [the design community] is pretty 
tight-knit so tools tend to spread pretty virally.
• (+) (On why Figma) You know, there’s a lot of restrictions [being a large organization]. Also,we just don’t want to jump on what’s brand new because it’s like here today, 
gone tomorrow, we need stuff that’s sustainable, but at the same time, you know, to make really innovative products and services, you need some of the latest, greatest 
stuff that’s coming out.
• (+) Initially, when I joined this team about three years ago, there was some pushback about getting it [a Figma license]. There was just, you know, the price associated with 
it. More recently, within the last year and a half, people have just, whenever they ask, they get it.
• (+) So we’re a big customer. In some respects, in terms of numbers of users, we use it as much as we can.We use no other product [outside of Figma] in that space, in the 
UX design space today. It’s used firm-wide.
• (+) I’ve been here at [the company] since 2020. Believe it or not, it [Figma] was already set up, and we were already actively using Figma when I got here. When I was first 
using Figma, it was back around 2018.
• (+) It [Figma] is the number one tool that we use, that I use day to day as a designer, 40 hours a week. So FigJam also, that’s second. So Figma Design primary, FigJam 
that would be a number two, definitely.
• (+) So the ROI is that if you want to design at [my company], you need Figma. It’s a one-to-one relationship. We literally can’t do our job without it. It’s so intrinsic to what 
designers do.
• (+) By the time I got to [my current company], it [Figma] was really kind of what I would call the tool of choice for the design consistency and had already been 
implemented.
Figma (FIG): Deep Dive Interviews (August 2025)

• (=/+) Our team is an operations team. We’re not core tech, although we are looking to start doing React builds ourselves, so we’re going to start doing more of that UI/UX 
design and development. I do see that if we do get in that realm, we will be using this [Figma] more often, especially with the proper design standards and things like 
that. It’s just been hard because the platforms that we use [outside of Figma], they don’t feel permanent right now so people are reluctant to upskill in Figma. I do see that 
shifting though in the direction of Figma.
• (=/+) The reason we use Figma is because of Figma Design.
Collaboration
• (+) We did have the same, I remember having the same conversation at [my previous company] when we were also like, “Hey, SketchUp, everything is all bloated, it’s 
everywhere.” When the pandemic came around, no one had any idea how to get where the latest file was because it was all attached to a server, and it was just uneasy. 
And then they’re like, “Well, we need to look into Figma because no one’s going to have a souped-up computer, and there’s so many files. How are we going to be able to 
do this during the pandemic?” And then that’s when they pivoted to Figma, and we have to start using this because now we’re going to be working from home. So that’s an 
example of when I was at [my previous company] when we had to pivot, and that migration process took about a good three months. But for Figma, there’s so much that 
we have in there that it would just cripple us if Figma just went away for one day.
• (+) We have the UX designers and we do have one or two projects where we work with them specifically, while we also use it on our own and perhaps we are not as 
advanced as they are, but they’ll work on it, they’ll design all these screens, and we can come in and can add comments. So,it’s really collaborative from that point of view, 
because they’ll be like, “what do you think about this” and we can come in and give them feedback on the design.It’s also good to see some of the text because you can 
say “actually remove that specific bit of text because our team doesn’t run on SLA.” It [Figma] allows you to work very fast, and you can go in and build and you’re not 
spending so much time reconfiguring.
• (+) Beyond that initial multiplayer environment, it then progressed into the single source of truth for a design system where all of our company’s design repository, the main 
global tokens of the design system, would exist and live. That became really important, and they started to expand outward into the handoffs, into eng [engineering], so as 
we would be developing designs, we could more easily hand off to our eng partners. So, the Dev mode that they launched was critical for us in making sure that whatever 
we designed would be implemented the way that we had designed it. And we had more confidence that we wouldn’t have a lot of back and forth with eng, our product 
development process.
• (+) I would say I definitely plug in every single day [into Figma] because I get those comments [from projects]. It helps that we do have a designer that is helping, 
collaborating with us on a project, and she'll go in and do design and we’ll comment, others comment, so it’s constant. You get a notification when someone comments, so 
it drives you back into the tool as well, because it’s like a nice little reminder of “hey, stuff’s going on with my design” and we’re continually working on it and iterating 
through it.
• (+) There’s nothing lacking in Figma.It had more capabilities and the sharing components, which is huge because it allowed us to do links with our stakeholders and 
design with our stakeholders very easily, and then also invite other people to work on things or insert copy or insert imagery that you needed and sort of opened up a whole 
other line of communication between us and even the creative graphic designers.
• (+) (On the ROI Figma provides) I mean, the core design of Figma, it is intrinsic. Like I said, it is the industry standard tool that we use and every company I can name uses 
as far as the design tool. That is because of the creator community that goes alongside Figma with all the plugins, how we’re able to use it, the collaboration. I mean, it’s 
just seamless to the way we work.
• (+) Our team on-boarded two user experience individuals and they introduced us to Figma and we were like, this is really cool. Very easy to collaborate and then they 
brought in FigJam which gives you a lot.
• (+) I think it was a great idea to collaborate and avoid redundant files, versioning issues. That sort of remote and asynchronous collaboration on design was really the kind 
of breakout feature.
Figma (FIG): Deep Dive Interviews (August 2025)

Replacing Other Vendors
• (+) So originally, I think we started with a program called Sketch, and we used that in combination with InVision, which was a prototyping tool. Sketch was the design tool, 
InVision is the prototyping tool, and it has some dev features. So, we had a lot of capabilities as well, like software engineering code, some CSS visibility, but not a lot. The 
program also was desktop-based, so there was no collaboration. There was no sharing the links to do things, it was hard files that we had to store somewhere. It was just 
like a bit more of a clunky process, it wasn’t great for cross-collaboration. So, we were definitely in the market to find something new. Adobe had a product out there, but it 
wasn’t great either, and then whena colleague of mine started five years ago, when we adopted Figma, he actually was the one who said, “Hey, I’ve used this program 
before called Figma,” and he introduced me to it. It was wonderful. It was wonderful because it’s free to start, and we tried it out and used it and figured out what the 
capabilities are. There was no barrier there, so we tried it and kind of quickly realized that it was very easy to use, very easy to learn, and this allowed us to share the files 
very easily to create and cross-collaborate. It wasn’t until later, actually, that it had a bigger transparency to the software engineering part, but that was great. That was just 
a bonus as it came along. When we tried it, we liked it, and it enabled us to use the software as a design tool and the prototyping tool, which we need both. It was all in 
one.
• (+) (On transitioning from Sketch to Figma) Actually, it was pretty easy. I want to say there might have been something, some sort of integration at the time that I could 
export a file from Sketch into Figma and it would create it as a Figma file. But we only had like a year or one instance of work in Sketch that we then needed to transition. 
We sort of just started from scratch. But the overhaul, it was pretty minimal, actually, just to do that. And then having the prototyping tool within, which is so easy to use. We 
did (transitioned) InVision first, the prototyping tool. And transitioned from Sketch to Figma for design.
• (+) Figma is the number one, the core application for us to do our jobs and to do our jobs effectively. Before that, there was an application, Sketch, which was used to 
create design mock-ups. Throughout the last few years, we transitioned from Sketch as the primary tool for designing, for creating and designing hi-fi mock-ups to now 
we’ve transitioned as an industry in a way, and also within the firm, transitioned into Figma.
• (+) (Specific software vendors that you think are strategic in that supply chain or in that overall system) So for us, like I said, Figma is the key one. It’s our main tool. It’s our 
top to bottom tool. That’s it. It’s Figma. Figma is so ingrained. It’s how we work from concept all the way to delivery through development. There’s no other tools in our tool 
chain.
• (+) FigJam was easy, lightweight mood boarding or brainstorming or even for things that are just simple, that non-designers are going to be using for org charts or things 
like that, and that was able to knock out tools like Miro or other whiteboarding tools.
• (+) We use Figma Slides now as a group. It’s a lot easier for us to get the granular controls that we want from, say something like Keynote. But it’s way easier because 
we’re in that tool.
• (+) Sketch was a great product, people were transitioning to Sketch from Adobe XD at the time, and, you know, Figma came in and it was pretty insane.
• (=/+) (Transition to Figma) It was six or more years ago, we were using a bunch of Figma and InVision, and then we went for Figma. There might still be some Sketch 
around as well.
AI / Figma Make
• (+) (Agentforce AI uptake vs Figma AI uptake) The difference between Figma and say Salesforce is Figma owns the experiences for designers. The trouble with Agentforce 
is so much of the data that I want GenAI to operate on to set up insights to my [employees] doesn’t sit in Salesforce. But 100% of experience that designers have sits in 
Figma. They have a natural place that’s going to use AI to drive efficiency, because that’s where designers are spending their time.So, we’re comfortable with Figma as 
they come out with AI capabilities that will make designers, whatever, 10, 20, 30% more efficient in what they’re doing in the tool to use that capability within Figma. In 
Salesforce, we’ll want to push aggressively on, ‘oh, we should be building it on top of [the organization’s LLM] and the firm’s agent builder’ and so on. We’re naturally 
skeptical of anything that locks us further into Salesforce.
• (+) I can’t help but think about AI and the Figma Make and the Figma Sites. They have another thing that’s called Code Connect and when you get engineers who are 
maybe more towards what is a real full stack engineer or a real front end engineer, sometimes called a design engineer, they’re working on the front end, and there is a lot 
of work right there that maybe our company is not really good at, and all of a sudden Figma’s Code Connect enables you to draw a UI in Figma and Figma generates all 
the front end code using our design system, just saved thousands of hours of engineering time for a simple website. That’s not data heavy, a website that’s just like a 
marketing page or like a very editorial style page. All of a sudden you don’t need engineering for that.
Figma (FIG): Deep Dive Interviews (August 2025)

AI / Figma Make (cont’d)
• (+) Figma Make is where we’re probably most deeply engaged on. This is I think the golden goose of the whole industry right now.There’s a lot of companies moving in this 
direction, but essentially, AI and software is definitely where we see the industry moving, and like I said, not just Figma, but a lot of companies in this space. But Figma is, I 
think everybody is looking at Figma as well positioned to make high impact in this area, and so, like I said, comp-generated designs and comp-generated UI creation is 
definitely, I mean, it’s a huge internal effort to make sure that we’re on the leading edge of this.
• (+) (On additional products that the customer would adopt) Definitely that AI tool because it hits just a lot of different capabilities including prototyping. It actually makes the 
prototyping easier and faster. And then also even generating placeholder copy or imagery to be able to get a sense of what we’re looking for. Editing imagery within the tool 
and not having to go elsewhere just makes things a lot more efficient. So that’s [Figma Make] probably the biggest one right now that we’re just looking forward to diving 
more into.
• (+) (On AI’s impact on headcount)If anything, I see an uptick in work because people are becoming more efficient. It’s like if we can get this much done in this amount of 
time, what else could we be doing? Just sort of get more out there, have bigger campaigns, that sort of thing, bigger outreach.
• (+) (On Lucid and other AI startups vs. Figma) We want to stick to what we’ve been using for the AI features [Figma]. It [Figma] is the enterprise standard. I mean, we look 
at startups all the time in this space, we’re aware of some startups, but there’s really been no demand to investigate.
• (+) (On AI) Honestly.I see it [AI] as more of a supporting tool, than, maybe I sound naive, taking over anyone’s role.I hope that people see it as a supporting tool to sort of 
get ideas down quickly and then have the designer iterate on their own from there.
• (+) The role of the designer is always going to be critical. You can generate something and then you need to be able to modify and change and reprompting AI over and 
over again with a “That button needs to be on the left” or “That doesn’t quite work right.”
• (+) I think AI is a large tool that I can see just becoming better and better as we use it, and just having it [Figma] be almost like the tool that we would suggest for anybody 
we need across the company.
• (+) Sometimes, it’s really expensive to have an engineer work on something and so it’s like I said before, have a designer be able to riff on something real fast and do it 
really cheap and fast.
• (=/+) There’s really two roles in a company. There’s someone that there’s a team, like my team, that builds all the building blocks, and then there’s the product team that 
assembles those building blocks into usable experiences. In the future, in the next three to five years, the role of the product designer is more likely to be directly disrupted 
by AI because a lot of designers today see their role as Figma experts. In fact, there’s a lot of companies that start to title people “Figma experts.” Figma is that integrated 
into a designer value statement. They can run on Figma, they can create on Figma, Figma files quicker than other designers, right? That’s their value proposition, but that 
value proposition really goes away with UI generated screens, or begins to go away, and really it comes back to what design always has been, which is the user appetite. 
Really understanding the problems that we’re trying to solve and not just of having an AI that can generate some random screen. It’s why you have that screen in the first 
place. What problem is that solving? Being a domain expert in that area as well. Understanding the different backgrounds that you need to be able to prompt something to 
give you the right screen. If you don’t know the regulations in different industries and don’t intimately know, ERP or supply chain or these different areas and then the 
customer needs, you’re never going to be able to prompt to something that would create value. So, the role of the designer really continuing to be that user advocate and 
that user expert that’s really looking to solve problems and understand best practices and understand experience, not UI designing, but experience, really caring what that 
end result is to the customer. That’s where the role of the designer is going to have to shift.For the design system purpose, the people that are building the components, 
you still need something that is going to feed these AI machines with what components to put together and build. So, for a while, the design system team is actually going 
to become more critical than less critical, and then I think that will eventually also fall to AI generation, but it’s further out on all these companies’ trajectory than the 
assembled UI that we’re seeing today which everybody’s going after.
Figma (FIG): Deep Dive Interviews (August 2025)

Quotable Quotes Sourced From Multiple Industry Contacts – Cont’d
AI / Figma Make (cont’d)
• (=/+) Figma Make today, does an amazing job if I wanted to create just a website, if I needed to update my portfolio, if I needed to create a local business a one-off 
website. Figma Make absolutely does that and achieves that today, and there’s a whole ecosystem of makers that are like freelancers, small website builders, or even 
prototyping startup ideas and things like that, that Figma Make would do beautifully. But at an enterprise level, you need to follow the design system or you literally can’t 
implement that design, because not only are things designed with a certain button or a certain block down or a certain table or data visualization, they’re also coded in that 
way, and what you don’t want to have happen is designers pass something to a developer that they have no way to code because they do these reusable coded 
components that match the actual design. So, there’s a coded button that matches the design button, and so if the designer passes something that’s not the same button 
that we have in code, a developer doesn’t know what to do. It goes back and forth. It’s tough to implement it properly, like it’s a mess. So, with Figma Make today, like I 
said, if you’re doing one-off design generation, you can use it beautifully. What they are starting on the path is they’re starting to release really basics when it comes to 
more following the design system. I think it was just recently put out that you can have Figma Make use your basic colors, and I think typography is kind of basic design 
language elements which are really the basic layer, but still the UI.
• (=/+) It really is AI as a first draft. Just like I’m sure everyone on this call uses GPT to draft different documents, and it is often a first draft as much input as you want to put 
into your own writing file and things like that. It’s the same for design. So, you’re going to need that combination of AI generation and then still being able to go in and craft 
and modify without having to continually process a really painful work. Especially when a designer is really quick in being able to make the modifications that they will 
intrinsically know how to make. So Figma is still at the heart of things. Even with AI, Figma will have to get this Figma Make right or integrate with one of these other AI 
companies to really continue to have the stronghold on the community that they have today.
• (=/+) We expect for their AI stuff, they’re charging a 30% premium. They haven’t told us that, but our Head of Design said we’re expecting 30% cost uplift, and one of our 
core AI initiatives is to drive the efficiency of design and the time of design. So, let’s say we end up paying that. So that’s a 30% uplift. If it works, if we can get for all the 
[organization] kind of control bureaucracy and so on. Whether we can or we can’t, I think that’s obviously a very big bet for the company, and then there’s always been the 
disconnect between design and development, so for us, there’s always potential adoption of more and more Dev Modes as developers see the benefits of that.
• (=/+) If I ask Figma Make to generate a screen, using the same color and font is important, but it needs to use the same header and the same buttons and the same layout 
and it needs to understand the full design system to be able to execute a screen that would be usable at scale. So, with Figma Make it’s working towards design system 
integration and they’ve got a roadmap that works towards that.First it speaks by language and then it goes more into, “You need to upload this component,” and some of 
the guidelines that we have for use. But today we’re still waiting on those things.
• (=/+) I can see early stage startups skipping the whole design branding phase to just say, “Give me a feel like this and spit out a style guide,” but it’s the same way like you 
can make a logo in Canva if you wanted to, but no one’s gonna take you seriously. At a certain level as a business, if you’re outsourcing to AI, it’s not super realistic at our 
scale.
• (=) (Will there be capacity gained from AI in design and the adjacent things, just like any of the other things that you mentioned?) Absolutely. It’s going to impact it. It’s going 
to be a matter of if the company is going to look to reduce or invest with that additional capacity fit. So, there could be a potential reduction, in the same way, there could be 
an expansion.
• (=) (On Figma Make) I think it’s just it’s still early you know. I mean I know everything feels like it’s moving so fast, but nobody really understands how AI and all this stuff 
fits, whereas the real value comes from the core platform. It’s to make prototypes and model and experience instead of wasting a lot of resources coding.
• (-/=) We’ve been in the Figma Make Alpha...The initial impression is that they are not too far behind, but they are playing a bit of catch up with tools like Lovable. When a 
lot of these vibe coding tools started to roll, obviously we are really quick to start adopting many of these and we started building prototypes in those tools, and obviously 
Figma does our prototyping phase and so what we had was a lot of PMs and Engineers and many others starting to build prototypes. The problem we had is they are 
totally disjointed from our design system, and so we were eagerly anticipating something from Figma so that we could use our repository of a design system and build, 
using vibe coding to build up those prototypes. So, when Figma Make came out, I think we were hoping for a richer experience using the design system, but right now the 
way that it works is somewhat disjointed, so you’re essentially taking a screenshot of a board within Figma design into Figma Make and it kind of recreates it. However, you 
can easily see where they’re going, and I think the Figma Slides started the same way too. It was a little bit, you know, rough, and now the transition into a design mode 
and presentation mode or slide mode is very elegant, and essentially it’s super easy to see how they’re going to retain everyone who is designing in Figma and then going 
into Make to build those web-based prototypes and maybe some mobile apps. So yeah, expectations are I think it’s early and they have a little bit of catching up to do.
Figma (FIG): Deep Dive Interviews (August 2025)

Competition
• (+) They’re [Figma] a company that is really on top of it and thinking about things in such a modern way, whereas maybe a company like Adobe has lost their way, and all 
of a sudden Figma can just eat Adobe’s lunch.When it comes to something like Adobe can make a very enterprise-y version of a thing, Microsoft can make a very 
enterprise-y version of a thing, but when Figma makes their version of it, it’s like, “Oh yeah, they really did this right.” They made this really fit the people that are using it, 
who are literally making very modern ways of working, reducing that friction. Like the new Figma Draw, for instance, everybody went, “Oh crap, Adobe Illustrator, goodbye. 
We are not going to need that super expensive license anymore. $900 a year to have Adobe products and another $900 a year to have Figma products.” Now you can just 
have the Figma license and do what you need to do when you’re making modern digital things.We are not like the old days where it’s like doing print design or trying to 
hack something like Photoshop or Illustrator to prototype websites. Now we have tools that are really focused on the kind of things that we want to do. I can’t help but think 
about our new challenge in employee experience, we are really trying to make a consumer grade experience for our employees – which could be something as simple as 
booking a conference room or organizing an event – something like Figma allows you to keep the integrity of our brand by handing off a toolkit of assets to content 
creators, content writers, site leaders here.
• (+) (Tools they are using) Today, I would say, the actual Figma [Design], FigJam, and we are playing with Figma Buzz, however we find that a lot of the Dev tools within 
Figma can get us there a little bit faster. If I had to be bullish on one thing, it’s the Figma Buzz.They’re already on the right path. Their competitors, there are software 
platforms out there, such as Sultra, or Storyteq, or FlashTalking, they’re very terrible, at design, they’re very clunky, design programs, that you can build out. The power of 
those platforms is they automatically push to every single platform. The problem is none of my designers or anyone wants to touch their terrible web UI design tools, they 
all want to work with Figma. Once Figma is able to automatically take my design and push to Facebook and Google Ads, they’re going to be taking that market share and 
will definitely be a huge competitor towards Adobe’s Gen Studio, which is supposedly the best thing that can do this, but I have yet to see from Adobe’s offering, ability to 
push to social platforms.
• (+) It would be great to, like this ProtoPie, for example, is new on the scene. You can’t bet on something that’s that small. Maybe a small company could, but when 
somebody joins our firm, it’s very likely that they’re familiar with Figma and they don’t want to have to learn another tool, you know, or do such, because we need 
somebody to get up to speed. We need a little bit of standardization for our teams and across LOBs and cost savings across the firm. So, maybe ProtoPie is way better, 
but at the same time, we need consistency so that across the firm we can have a tool that will last us for five years and know that if we’re working with similar things across 
the firm, that we’ve got consistency and interoperability and we’re getting cost savings because we only have one contract and we don’t have a team over in [a different 
LOB] using this thing and over to [another LOB] reusing this thing. That’s a recipe for disaster.
Figma (FIG): Deep Dive Interviews (August 2025)

Quotable Quotes Sourced From Multiple Industry Contacts – Cont’d
Competition (cont’d)
• (+) The problem with Adobe Gen Studio is it’s all code HTML5. It’s not to build the actual design of the ad. You’re going to have to know actual web dev code because they 
don’t have a good design system... I think it’s old flash and there’s no way for me to get designs that are also in Figma into Adobe Gen Studio, and to build those ads out in 
Adobe Gen would be so hard that I don’t think it’s worth the time, because all it does is build off those ads, which I can do very simply in Figma natively, but I would have to 
know how to code those banners and I would have to know how to figure out a way to make it work. So yes, you can do all that,  but I would have to hire basically a creative 
developer who just knows HTML and how to code for that type of platform, whereas Figma, I don’t need to hire anyone. Everyone knows Figma. So, one of the benefits of 
Figma being it’s really kind of easy to use, figure out.
• (+) People who are just doing that community building, you can give them the tools to easily stay on brand and be cohesive and make it easy to quickly send out assets 
with something like Figma Buzz and boom, easy, ship it, done. Everything is templated, smart, modern, fast. I always say Figma does a really good job of embedding 
workflows within the tool. You can do everything with Figma and not have to be tracking things in a Microsoft Excel file over here, Confluence page over here, SharePoint 
site over here to figure out what’s the status of a thing. You’ve got a tool that has everything you need built into it or really smart integrations, you can automatically know 
the status of something because you’ve got a really smart integration turned on with Atlassian Jira or to Asana or something like that. It’s just like, well, this thing anticipated 
my needs and it gives you a lot of power.
• (+) The best thing I can say is I think any tech company at this moment is using Figma for their marketing. Say, for example, if Figma just disappeared, you would see a lot 
of ad campaigns, a lot of videos, a lot of actual marketing materials start slowly disappearing. Why? Because the things that we can do in Figma allow us to make ads for 
Facebook, Google ads, Snapchat, all in the same place, because each one has its own ads builder, which doesn’t really help us because we can’t use our font within the 
platforms to create our own branded material, it’s just not possible, and we can’t do the things that we can do in Photoshop, because in Photoshop, it takes so much longer 
to make these designs.
• (+) So let’s just imagine I’m at home and may do a little freelance on the side. Maybe I would use Penpot, it’s free, and it is like a straight up Figma clone. It is like GIMP 
versus Photoshop. GIMP is completely free, like 60% as good as Photoshop, probably good enough for a home casual user. As an industry professional though, if I 
needed to go get a new job next month, I sure as hell better know Figma like the back of my hand. So that’s why at home I pay for a Figma license so that I can do my 
learning. It’s table stakes.So, on the point of free tools, in an enterprise, it matters a bit less.
• (+) We migrated away from Sketch to Figma, a very deliberate choice that ended up happening at the right time and, thank goodness, because the industry moved so 
fast. It [Figma] also really helped us with really important capabilities like digital whiteboarding, which is similar but related, so talking about that work group, we were able 
to get an app like Lucidspark, which I’m sure you’re aware of, and there’s also competitive products out there like Mural or Miro or FigJam, but it [Figma] filled just incredibly 
a gap that we needed to be able to brainstorm and ideate on different things.
• (+) (On ease of switching off of Figma) I almost want to say the word existential because, in short, it wouldn’t be as simple as, let’s say a light switch, right? It would be a 
significant amount of time to off board and then to onboard onto a new tool. It would mean migrating our folders and files somehow onto this new platform, possibly 
recreating these files from scratch onto this new platform. It would also require more strategic planning on how to get our design systems, multiple design systems, all the 
different UI components, even any functional aspects into the new platform.
• (+) The thing is that we can put a full campaign into one project, so you can see all of the different digital touch points of when the creative campaign is going out, the 
emails, and ads, and those on the website. It can house all of that stuff, whereas a lot of the Adobe programs, they just couldn’t handle it from a storage capacity, or it’d be 
really slow. Figma seems to be able to just have a big storage capacity for an individual product or project that allows everything within the campaign to be visible, so we 
share those links with our stakeholders.
• (+) So the main tool from Figma is the design part of Figma, which is the design tool. It’s embedded as deeply as you can imagine into the designer’s workflow. It is the 
main tool that we use for everything, right, when it comes to designing product screens, even if there’s marketing screens or things like that, but really product and UIs, 
there’s nothing else to use other than Figma...So, it’s like I said, as engrained as possible into our designer workflow.
• (+) Having common tools and frameworks is really valuable, especially in a space that’s moving so fast and we can’t just be switching at the drop of a hat or using some 
crazy add on or animation. People love something like Framer, you’ll hear about some latest cool, sexy thing, and yes, that’s cool. It’s cool and maybe you explore that on 
your own time, but we’re [a large organization], we have to move it an even keel.
Figma (FIG): Deep Dive Interviews (August 2025)

Quotable Quotes Sourced From Multiple Industry Contacts – Cont’d
Competition (cont’d)
• (+) (On ease of moving from Figma to Lovable) For Lovable to rebuild everything that we have, we’re a large business now, so my design org is maybe like 60, 70 people, 
I’ve got PMs on top of that using seats, and for them to unseat Figma would be, I mean, it would be Herculean.
• (+) Canva is a well-known competitor... I have a distaste for Canva because it waters down the design experience, but if I were to use Canva, I’d still have to use Figma.
• (+) I gotta be honest, it’s [Figma] kind of [the dominant standard], maybe Canva is gaining some steam but not among product designers certainly.
• (+) Figma does have crazy market share.
• (=/+) We do still use Adobe, but we don’t use Adobe as much as we use Figma. I would say depending on the whole, if it’s with the animator, the brand, probably gonna 
use Adobe After Effects about 75% of the time, but you’re gonna be getting the content, the assets, the designs, you’re gonna be using Figma 25% of the time. But if you’re 
an art director, or if you’re a copywriter, you’re gonna probably spend most of your time in Figma.
• (=/+) FigJam’s closest competitor internally is Lucid. I think Lucid’s being pushed very hard [internally] and it’s very easy I think to get (internally), but doesn’t have some 
similar capabilities as FigJam. Maybe not on the design front.
• (=) Actually, even with Adobe, it’s a lot less imagecreation or video creation. It’s a lot more imagemanipulation. I have an image of my [product] for New York, Gen Z kind 
of target population. I want to manipulate different versions of it for different social channels for different times of the year for different personas and so. It’s that kind of 
continual manipulation of images, resizing of images, recoloring of images from your master images that will still be created in whatever creative solution, which is probably 
Adobe Creative Cloud.
• (=) Lucid doesn’t offer those UI/UX features, and if we had Canva, I feel like Canva has been doing a lot of crazy things lately. Maybe they’re a direct competitor, I haven’t 
actually seen their UI/UX features yet, but if they have it, then Canva’s probably a better option if they have a competitive product, only because they have all their AI 
features... I know that Canva’s really pushing the boundaries. I’ve heard good things lately.
• (=) I do like Lucid as well for some purposes, mostly collaboration purposes, but then there’s no app design features. So, you can’t really do web, UI design and things like 
that. Something I do like also about Figma, and I have seen some of our style/CSS developers use is the CSS switches within it. So, you can actually get styling from 
Figma itself. That’s something that is used every once in a while as well.
• (=) I’ve seen other companies try to make apps for less of the power users, whether it be like Adobe’s products for mobile apps that are much simpler, they can’t compete 
with something that’s like Canva. It’s kind of a play in those other user segments and not going to be able to get penetration there, but that’s just my gut instinct based on 
the past.
• (=) Canva’s [footprint is] very minimal [internally]. There’s a desire as well, which you can appreciate to have some alternatives to Adobe.
• (-/=) (In terms of risks that could disrupt Figma) Lovable would be one. They are big, however, there’s no connection for me to be able to bring our video and animation 
programs as easy as it is for Figma. If they had a little bit more openness with the platform, they could take some folks from Figma, but at the moment, I would say that 
would be very hard.
• (-/=) We use FigJam just to plan and document things and collaborate with people. Although, Lucid has been taking a little bit of a bite out of that lately because, you know, 
there’s some collaboration features on there as well, so we use that at times because it’s a little bit more real-time than Figma is.
• (-) (On replacing Figma) There are a couple areas where I think there could be real disruption. One would be in and around growth. So, growth design is a subcategory of 
product design wherein you take an initial product that has launched and you just continually optimize it, constantly running experiments. Someone is eventually going to 
build an optimization engine powered by AI that is generating new designs every second of every day and billions of permutations trying to find the absolute best way to 
onboard a user, do pricing, billing, the acquisition funnel or the monetization, whatever it’s going to be. We use Design Systems today to help us accelerate that a little bit, 
so that the developer’s job is easy, so we’re making these Lego kits that they can go off and build and do the things that are housed in Figma. Well, I think if someone 
could do that, I could see growth teams moving away from Figma or Design Systems because they would just be bolting it in and then take just a brand style guide, even if 
it was a static PDF, and they’d take that and run with it.
Figma (FIG): Deep Dive Interviews (August 2025)
