Competition (cont’d)
• (-) (On other software companies replacing Figma) I would say the biggest existential threat would be maybe OpenAI’s acquisition of Multi, which was originally intended to 
create a multiplayer operating system, except for I think that they’ll hand over the cursor to their language model, so they’ll turn it over to ChatGPT and it’s possible that the 
broader category of design sees disruption when you can vibe design everything and you can skip the tooling entirely and have an operating system that retains that 
information and can be shared across the team – but that is theoretical, maybe eventual, but that’s a ways out... That’s in a post-SaaS app era.
• (-) Lucid is definitely the one that people use most often because they have access to it for free [internally]. I also kind of l ike Lucid because even though I have Figma 
web-based, I feel like Lucid’s just a little bit more snappy for those purposes. So, people do tend to use that for that purpose.
• (-) (On competition for Figma Sites) For Sites, I would say Framer and Webflow definitely have a leg up, and then beyond that you are just competing with customer 
solutions.
• (-) I would say that with Lovable it’s super easy. It’s super easy. The view pane of Lovable is the real draw.Whatever you are prompting is rendered in real time there.
• (-) (On other software companies replacing Figma) It would be death by a thousand cuts if I had to guess by point solutions.
Areas for Improvement
• (=) If I were to suggest something to Figma is once you start leveraging the actual data, and what I mean by that is you’re targeting, then that’s when I would even get more 
interested. I want to be able to add contextual data, I want to change, the headline and add ads in our design saying, “Shop at blank,” and it would be a retailer and then I 
would have to say, “in your area blank,” so location. I want to make thousands of different ads saying different cities and different retailers and be able to have different 
languages and be able to make ads for different target audiences, like gender. You can pull these levers in Facebook and Google Ads. You can say, “I want this ad to target 
a middle-aged 30-year-old male who’s in video games,” and make that and we can pull that data and feed it to our Figma design for marketing. It does have to add a way 
of, like, well, you can pull data. But my team can make ads now with our data that we have. We can use that to our advantage. That’s what would bring, I think, more power 
to Figma’s Buzz than being able to provide all the data. Like we’re gonna provide you all the different temperatures that are for next week, from 77 to 100, and what I mean 
by that is it’ll make an ad for one with ice cream. It’ll push those ads out through Facebook. It’s like, “Hey, it’s really cold in your area, here’s some hot tea.” You know?
• (=) So right now, I think that motion design and videography would be a really easy extension of their business [Figma]. So, I worked on a product years ago called 
Dropbox Replay. They have a competitor at Adobe called Frame.io that they acquired. So, I think it would be pretty simple for them [Figma] to build a video collaboration 
tool if I had to guess, because they probably weren’t working on it when it was part of the Adobe acquisition possibility, and now they see like, ‘hey, we can go head to head 
there,' Really, if you think about that, it could be podcasts or it could be video editing. There’s a lot of design that happens with video editing. That would be intuitive... 
Motion design. Yeah, same thing. Motion design is a sub-discipline and so there’s tools like Lottie or others that help, there’s an entire school cottage industry, Imaginary 
Forces or Box Studios or these teams that often have to build their own tooling. Pixar is famous for this and I think that it’s really, really the most complex design workflow, 
and in many ways, the most technical in a lot of situations. So, it’s interesting for them to go into that category and probably pretty simple stuff there. Maybe even like 
launching a simple editor for writing, something like that.
• (=) So, one of the biggest issues that tools like Lovable or Bolt have is that it’s either prompt or code. Those are the methods of control. So, with those two modalities, any 
focused designers, once they prompt, they can go into the code, and they can do some fine tuning, and it’s relatively simple.It’s all CSS or HTML or whatever it is.But 
where the magic will eventually happen is when I can write a prompt for a mobile application, I can see the code of the mobile application, I can see the render of the 
mobile application, but I can hit a toggle in the same way that Figma Slides does today and I can go into design mode. Then I can grab something, I can manipulate it on 
canvas – let’s say that I wanted to slide an avatar from the bottom right to the top left – then I can switch it back, and it would render that back in code. That, I think, is 
going to be the real power, is the in-canvas, direct-canvas design editing experience, rather than the semantic prompting or the code.
• (=) The one thing we still have to use InDesign from Adobe for is print materials, like books, pamphlets, Figma doesn’t support the CMYK [Cyan, Magenta, Yellow, Key 
(Black)] printing process. Using Illustrator with their CMYK is the only way to get it back into InDesign for our printers to actually print out. If you were to print out anything 
from Figma, it’s going to be an RGB, which will not match when converted to print material. But I feel like if Figma did come out with CMYK, I wouldn’t want to touch 
InDesign again because it is so old and I can’t see what my production designers are doing, for billboards and everything, I have to take their word for it, with their InDesign 
files.
Figma (FIG): Deep Dive Interviews (August 2025)

Areas for Improvement (cont’d)
• (=) (On things Figma can improve feature-parity-wise) Transitioning between web and mobile, building out some additional functionality, connecting to Supabase or cloud 
binaries for a database or adding in API keys from a map tool, things like that. So, I think that the integrations (in Figma) are going to need to expand, but I think even when 
you’re using Bolt or Lovable, they don’t have the design system and that’s the biggest problem. So that, they’re still on par, and that’s going to be the killer advantage 
because all of it is kind of useless until we can actually use our design system.
• (=) (In terms of opportunities for Figma to disrupt) I constantly struggle with After Effects, even today. They have been needing a competitor for that for a long time. Not just 
that but digital asset management, that’s also a huge ripe area for Figma just to take market share. I’m using two different digital asset management services like Orange 
Logic and Adobe’s, and I’m always taking it and I have to pull it from the dam back to Figma. They do have nice plugins for it, but I wish it was all just native.
• (=) It (Figma Buzz) had a lot of positives, but I think it only scratched the surface, I think they put it together very quickly. The CSV import, yes that’s great, but that was not 
really a miss but more of a foul ball, and the reason I say that is we have to import data and there is a way in Figma to do that from other providers like Google Sheets, but 
for Figma Buzz, I have to download that CSV and re-upload it, and I feel like that kind of goes against what Figma is about.
• (=) (On Figma Draw vs Adobe Illustrator) They’ve [Figma] just scratched the surface, but a lot of the tools that I need are like, I need to be able to use a custom path, but 
also I need to add patterns to that path, or if I’ve got a line, and I have a pattern of flowers, I want the flowers to follow that path, or if I need to make a dynamic gradient, 
that needs to be then made for print, that is what Figma can’t do.
• (-/=) We are running very easily north of (high hundreds of millions) ARR, and the confidence we need in our sites and analytics and the CMS and the experimentation are 
far too robust for [Figma] Sites right now. I think Sites is great for portfolio sites or new startups or those things but in its maturity it’s not nearly close enough to do that, it 
could be there eventually but if I had to guess there is going to be some really, really stiff competition in that particular category. I doubt that they would get wall-to-wall 
adoption for large-scale enterprises to be using Figma Sites to run that for some time, but what they can do is knock off maybe the design blog or a one-off campaign site 
or something like that with it over time.
Platform Approach
• (+) So they [Figma] recently restructured the seat preferences. Now I think it’s like Design, Collaboration, Dev Mode... and we use all of them and there are people, and 
there are contributors that are just using Slides and FigJam. So, there are stakeholders that will use it for brainstorming and presentation. We’ve also onboarded the whole 
creative team onto Figma, so they honestly rarely use Adobe – we’ve talked about possibly dropping it, which is just historically just very boring concept to think about just 
given its presence and its place in the design world. But Figma has adopted so many of those tools, including creating vectors, pen tools, editing imagery – you know that 
we would typically use Photoshop for – retouching. And now with their AI tool, I don’t even know what I don’t know yet.With the AI tool, we’ve dabbled in it, and honestly it’s 
one of our KPIs to leverage AI more. But really, I feel like we’re using those, depending on the role of the individual who has an account with Figma, we’re using a full 
gamut of what they provide, Slides, FigJam, the Dev tool, Design, and Draw.
• (+) I think what is nice about Figma is we know that they are iterating on the product and they’re keeping up with technology and technology is so important to our 
business. They are including a lot of different features for other tools that we use. We’re kind of thinking of the place with those other tools. It’s [Figma] really becoming just 
a centralized tool for so many things, and that then just opens up the possibility of other roles being able to use it as wel l. It’s sort of like, we’re already using Figma, but this 
one team really needs to use PowerPoint. Well, let’s just onboard them to Figma, that’s what we need.It’s sort of like a natural way of how we would adopt more things. Or, 
you know, there’s still a decent amount of remote people and we need to collaborate on something then we use Figma.There are other tools out there, but it’s nice to have 
it all in one place and it’s all accessible.
• (+) Interestingly, with presentations [Slides], it just grows and grows, because so many people are chipping into a presentation, and they need access to the tool – unlike a 
design file where maybe at most you need to view it, but in Make it’s a way lower barrier to entry for the technical skill level – hence more people want to use it/need to use 
it. You know we have people in legal or HR that are now using Figma Slides. With FigJam, it’s just annoying to have to do different tools, tool fragmentation is a perpetual 
problem, so we encourage folks to avoid using Miro or other whiteboarding tools when we can just have it all in one place [with FigJam].
• (+) The best way I can describe it is, I have to go into Photoshop, and then I need to pull the design from Illustrator back into Photoshop. For Figma, it’s kind of like a one 
place to do it all. The only reason why we use Photoshop is for my photography teams, who are using it for retouching our photography, or Illustrator would be mostly used 
for our actual illustration design, rather than Figma. The tools are a little bit more advanced in Adobe, but if I were to say that’s why we don’t use Photoshop to make ads, 
because it’s so complex.
Figma (FIG): Deep Dive Interviews (August 2025)

• (+) The fact that we’re working on the same foundation, that our system platform all comes from the same place and you can see what the truth is in Figma the same way it 
is in code is really powerful. I think Figma is the only one who’s really doing that because it’s all an integrated system.
• (+) I would say FigJam is also a tool that we have used weekly for years. That goes out of the design community and into the total product lifecycle. And we use that as a 
digital whiteboard, for lack of a better term. We use that for meetings and workshops and things like that.

• (=/+) (On Dev Mode) I think we’ve been waiting on the value return that we would get from that. And I think really with the pace of AI and AI tooling on the market today, I 
think we’re constantly looking at if Dev Mode will really be a door opener for us.

• (=) I talked about Make, we really, with Buzz, we don’t use a lot.We also utilize the Slides. We also utilize it only in the design sense. It’s a handy tool for us to have as 
designers. But again, at scale, we don’t see that outpacing PowerPoint or some of our internal tooling there.

Spending Intentions
• (+) Figma famously really doesn’t do discounting at scale.They are pretty firm on a lot of their pricing, which makes it a really interesting relationship. With a vendor, it’s 
kind of unique in that positioning. I negotiate much harder with all my other vendors than Figma, quite frankly. But the thing that, again, the tool cost is high for us. Figma is 
not a minor expense for us. It is something that is a significant portion of our tooling costs. But in the same way, like I said, it is as intrinsic as a designer working. If you pay 
for the headcount of the designer, you pay for Figma for that designer. It’s just a one-to-one relationship. We can’t work without it. So, I think, is there a lot of debate around 
that [the cost of Figma] right now? Quite frankly, no, because, again, it’s the only tool on the market that we would be able to utilize. Plus, we have all those UI kits. 
Everything that we do at scale is all built on the Figma system. So, at this point, I don’t see another Figma tool disruptor coming in and disrupting that even at a different 
price point, essentially.
• (+) (In terms of if pricing pressure would cause them to move off Figma) If I were to be honest, there wouldn’t be any perspective. Like I said, it is ingrained in all marketing 
to be using Figma. Even a lot of people who are not designers are asking for designer seats and FigJam seats because of things like being able to script all on a mood 
board or a process or making a journey user map. Even our director of marketing, she’s like, “I use it all the time, too.”We cannot work without having it. If we were a 
startup, and if Lovable is offering a lower price and different models, yeah, I’m sure, but when you are a public company, it’s even harder to turn the whole ship just one 
degree, because then we’re going to have to get all the product design team on board.Then we have to consider, well, what is the price structure? What would it take to 
migrate? And the cost of being able to migrate off of Figma to another platform? It would take, I would have to say it would take about a year because of how much 
[content] is in Figma.
• (+) Right now, we’re somewhere [just under 10,000] seats on an enterprise license for Figma. The spend is like (LSD seven figures). Numbers have gone up significantly in 
the last year. There’s other licenses that are just for the digital whiteboarding platform, whether it be Lucidspark, or Figma’s FigJam or something like that... It’s [Figma] 
critical, you know, there’s no replacement for what you can do with Figma.So, I think it’s luckily getting a little bit of the attention it deserves, to have the single sign on, to 
be able to easily onboard it, that it might be like as you show up as a new employee, based on your role, you would automatic ally be provisioned an editor license. That’s 
something that we got recently and it is a huge benefit when somebody can get up to speed really fast and they’re not messing around with bureaucracy of trying to get a 
license to this thing, and of course, every UX designer should just show up day one and Figma is already provisioned. Right. It’s a quality of life thing.
• (+) We have [tens of thousands of] registered users, of which [mid-teens percent] are users of Figma Design, Dev Mode, or FigJam. We have [mid-single-digit percentage] 
using Figma Design, and then [low-single-digit percentage] using Dev Mode, [mid-single-digit percentage] using FigJam, and it gets very good feedback from the design 
teams. We’re actively evaluating the AI products. I was on a call today, I think they’re going to go GA in the next couple of weeks with those products, but they’re still beta 
mode at the moment. Figma AI or Figma Make is the way we described it internally. We don’t know the commercial structure for it yet. But we think we can drive significant 
cost savings in our design processes and efficiencies in our design processes in particular from some of the Figma capabilities that they come on out with.
• (+) If we look at our contract with Figma, today we have, like I said, full saturation into the design community here at [my company] because to be a designer, you need to 
use Figma. So, we are carefully utilizing licenses of that. Our content designers, is a large population internally that’s using more and more Figma as their choice, and then 
even our product managers, being able to go in, review, comment, modify, use Figma. Generally, we do have quarter over quarter natural growth of Figma usage because 
of those states that are playing.
• (+) I track it [the Figma contract size] loosely, but there’s not a lot to do about it.We’re, maybe like a quarter million to $300K annually. As I said,with finance, it’s never 
questioned. It’s a larger line item, certainly... The nearest thing to that is $50K a year and that was ProtoPie and we’re going to cancel it because of Figma Make.

=== PAGE 81 ===

81
Industry Contact 1
General
• Contact represents a Platinum Solutions Partner of HubSpot, which serves as a strategic consultancy across business functions, including marketing and sales, and 
provides various onboarding services.
• Contact tends to support U.S. based enterprise and midmarket customers within the HubSpot ecosystem across digital transformation initiatives, with insight into other 
application software products as well.
Demand Environment
• Expresses optimism over the business environment post the U.S. Presidential election, noting there was a “pick-up in activity in mid-to-late November," though 
that it was “fairly gradual and not a step-function spike by any means."
• Describes the election as a “relief” in terms of customer sentiment, where customers felt as though they “at least now have some sense of where things are going” in the 
macro environment.
• Suggests there was a “subtle shift” in his customer conversations from a risk-off investment posture to more active leaning into strategic investments in recent 
months, which he believes would have been true regardless of the election outcome, as either way it removed some overhang in visibility.
• In terms of calendar Q4, notes that the holidays did present “some unusual dynamics” in terms of linearity in the quarter for his firm’s practice, suggesting that given 
Christmas and New Years fell mid-week, there was a “somewhat expected slowdown” in quarter-end as it felt like “everyone was out of office for the last two weeks of the 
year."
• However, despite what he describes as a “ghost town” at the end of December, relays this was“contemplated in their plans” and that activity “mostly returned to 
business as usual” into January as some excess budgets also appeared to have freed up, though overall his sense is that “things are good in terms of quota 
attainment, but not gangbusters."
• Expects his firm’s HubSpot practice specifically to grow “a bit above 20% in 2025,” which implies fairly limited deceleration relative to their 2024 performance, 
though they are “hopeful” they could net-out to some acceleration “all things said and done.”
Innovation Capacity
• Shares that “HubSpot is a dream team of innovators," describing the rapid pace of product development that in many cases is outpacing that of peers, including more 
legacy vendors in the market.
• Characterizes disruptive recent innovations, including DeepSeek’s R1 model and OpenAI Operator / DeepResearch, as “welcomed seeds to sow and grow” for 
HubSpot; noting HubSpot’s agility to plug in these technologies and bring the tools directly into the hands of customers “with immense speed."
• Presents a favorable view on the downstream impacts from DeepSeek’s R1 model, suggesting for a company like HubSpot, itallows for more effective models to 
augment products like Breeze AI and Agent.ai, as well as lower input costs to encourage broader-scale deployment and in turn adoption.
• Notes that customers could be “complaining about a missing feature or functionality” in the HubSpot platform on Monday and that “by Wednesday [HubSpot] has already 
addressed it” and that the company is constantly working with customers to “meet them where they need."
• Shares that last year there was some friction for HubSpot, including a change to its partner relationships, go-to-market (GTM) approach, and seat-based pricing models, 
though that these seemed to have “now been worked out” with abetter “control over the steering wheel."
• Notes that the HubSpot platform is “clearly enterprise ready” with ongoing momentum upmarket for his practice, sharing that the “innovation is there and 
customers are coming if HubSpot can lower costs for them vs. their existing products."
HubSpot (HUBS): Deep Dive Interviews (February 2025)

AI Products

• For HubSpot’s core portfolio, shares positive commentary within his practice for Sales and Marketing Hub, noting that his customers seem most receptive to AI functionality

Competition
• Perceives legacy peers as more vulnerable to disruption related to AI compared to HubSpot, which has “ingrained itself as the system of record across the front 
office” due to its cohesive multi-Hub approach and as a result tends to be quite sticky.
• Expects AI-related seat cannibalization to more heavily impact legacy players, which tend to carry a “much higher price," asCIOs have already expressed frustration 
around the services and ongoing implementation costs of these bulkier, heavier product sets relative to a HubSpot.
• Relays pushback from customers in the market with “scaled contact center footprints” as to the consumption pricing being offered by some of HubSpot’s competitors, 
noting that “$2/conversation is way too expensive for the amount of activity” in large-enterprises and that these customers are “waiting it out until prices taper."
• However, describes some Agentic AI tools from peers, including Salesforce Agentforce, as being the “most fully-baked agent solution out there today” and sees 
a “promising vision” developing as companies like Salesforce are able to leverage cheaper input costs on the model side to deliver a more cost-effective output to its 
customers.
• Shares that his firm closed several deals in the second half of last year that were competitive displacements of peer products, including Pardot, noting that HubSpot 
brought the “TCO much lower” for the customerand that there is more of these to come based on his firm’s current pipeline.

Industry Contact 1
General
• The contact is an Elite Solutions partner of HubSpot. The partner indicates that ~80% of his HubSpot practice derives from implementation and consulting services, while

• While the partner’s organization serves customers globally, his HubSpot practice is focused primarily in the US, with some exposure to South America.
Demand Environment
• The partner observed flat RFP activity in April relative to March, but has since seen a sequential uptick in both May and June. He does not yet have enough clarity 
on how July is performing thus far, given it was only been two weeks into the month at the time of this discussion.
• Attributes the uptick in RFP activity as driven by two areas: 1) demand for Sales and Marketing Hub and 2) “purse strings” opening up given a more stable macro 
environment since the end of April based off his observations from client activity.
• While the contact doesn’t believe there is yet an inflection in demand trends for HubSpot and the macro environment remains uncertain, he notes “I feel better now about 
my practice [with HubSpot]” than he did at the beginning of 2025.
• Indicates that his Q2 HubSpot practice came in above his expectations due to professional services related work for Sales and Marketing Hub. With respect to the 
license portion of his practice, that performed in-line with his Q2 expectations.
• Heading into 2025, his company expected to grow its HubSpot practice by 15% y/y. He is not updating that goal, given that his Q2 outperformance is offset by a weaker 
than expected Q1. However, the partner expresses more confidence in meeting that 15% target for 2025, based on recent RFP aforementioned.
Highlights HubSpot Is Moving into the Upper Mid-Market
• Observes that within his HubSpot practice, the implementations and integrations with HubSpot are widening across a greater “surface area” of workflows, which is 
increasing the number of users being onboarded within organizations.
• Comments that the size of customers he is working with are larger today than they were a year ago from an employee count perspective. Additionally, he notices that new 
accounts to his practice are typically larger than what they were a year ago. Given this, he opines that HubSpot is moving up-market to the “upper end of mid-market 
companies.”
• “I’d say the level of engagements with our mid-market customers is elevating.”
Competition
• With respect to the competitive landscape, the partner indicates that he typically sees HubSpot competing with Salesforce, Zoho, ZoomInfo, Mailchimp, and Marketo. He 
opines that HubSpot is easier to implement than alternative solutions and is easier to integrate with third-party applications.
• He expects to see greater competition with Salesforce, as HubSpot moves more in the upper-mid market.
International Business Is Mixed
• While the partner handles accounts in the US, his company’s overall HubSpot practice has exposure in South America. He has heard from his colleagues that there has 
been more budget scrutiny amongst HubSpot clients in South America and while he isn’t 100% certain, he suspects South America performed below expectations in 
Q2.
• “We have a smaller presence internationally, in South America, and it’s been more choppy there, so I’ve heard.”
Positive on Breeze
• Indicates that Breeze has thus far had a successful rollout among his clients based on the feedback he’s received. More specifically, he observes Breeze being used for 
crafting personalized outreach and generating marketing materials (landing pages and blog posts).
• “I’ve heard that it’s freeing up time for people in marketing and sales, automating repetitive tasks and making it available freeing up time [for people] to do 
higher ROI tasks.”

Industry Contact 1
General
• The contact is an Elite Solutions partner of HubSpot in the US.

Demand Environment

• “Q2 was unusually slow.”
• “When it comes to purchasing something as big as HubSpot, you don’t make that decision when you’re unsure of what the tariffs are really going to be. It has gotten a lot 
better already though. I’ve done more business this month [July] than I did the entirety of Q2, and it’s because of less uncertainty.”
• The contact was surprised by how strong deal activity has been post July 4th for his HubSpot practice. Based on the activity levels he’s been seeing since July 4th, he 
feels that Q3 is starting off great.
• He senses that customers view that the worst is behind them in terms of the macro/tariff uncertainty, for now.
• Following Sales and Marketing Hubs, he notes that Service, Ops, and Content Hubs are also popular, in that order.
• Indicates that his company is aspiring to grow the HubSpot practice by 20% y/y in 2025, and despite the mixed 1H performance he believes that his company 
will “clear” that target.
Seat Growth “Fairly Stable”
• Based on his purview, while he observed a pause in spend during Q2, the partner did not see seat contraction by customers. Indicates that seat growth has been “fairly 
stable” year-to-date.
• When asked if the advent of Agentic AI may pressure seat expansion for software, in general, in the medium-to-long-term, the contact opines that this is more of a risk at 
the enterprise level rather than in the mid-market. Explains that even one repetitive function can be automated with AI and reduce several redundant seats at once at a 
larger company.
• Additionally, he senses that headcount reduction will occur in certain roles like engineering, web developers, and developers focused on coding.
HubSpot “Doing a Decent Job” Penetrating Up-Market
• “I think they are doing a decent job” with respect to HubSpot moving into the upper mid-market. Additionally, the partner indicates that he closed a few up-market deals 
in Q2, although didn’t specify his definition.
• “Their focus is up-market right now and I think that they’re doing a decent job of taking some business away from Salesforce...In just the last year alone, we’ve 
been included in more up-market deals than the previous three years combined. So, we are seeing a shift.”
Feedback on AI
• Opines that HubSpot’s Breeze AI product suite is somewhat overpriced, and he doesn’t see many customers using it as of now because of security reasons.
• At the same time, he notes, “I think it’s a great first step [Breeze]. I think it’s great what they’re doing, but it’s the beginning and I think it needs to be built out more.”
• The partner touts the HubSpot Content Remix feature, which he opines “is a big deal.” He adds that it’s very easy for anyone to use it to create marketing content using 
keywords within the HubSpot portal.

Industry Contact 1
General
• The contact is an Elite partner of HubSpot and while his company does resell HubSpot licenses, his practice derives revenue primarily from managed services. His practice

• This contact oversees five different consulting teams for his practice, equating to 10-20 employees. The team skews toward enterprise clients.
• A few months ago, his company made a conscious effort to reorganize the delivery side of the business and develop practice areas that can help better service clients.
Demand Environment

• He explains “We’ve had a couple of clients who have said, I gotta wait on this until XYZ date or something like that. Unclear if that’s related to tariff limitations, macro

budgets get maybe further constricted.”
• Mentions that his Q2 HubSpot practice came in above his expectations, which was mainly driven by strategic shifts within his practice and what he believes was 
a “little” pull forward in spend from Q3. At the same time, he admits that Q3 is the quietest quarter for his HubSpot practice, so he is not 100% certain.
• Given the recent outperformance for his HubSpot practice, the contact expects to exceed his initial 2025 targets that were established at the beginning of the year.
• “Budgets have been good, but I think could always be bigger and it takes a lot of effort to implement a CRM in a way that really works well and everyone’s trained on it.”
Observes HubSpot Having Success Moving Up-Market
• Three years ago, his company saw “more and more traction” with HubSpot, and as such, decided to allocate more resources to the HubSpot practice of his 
organization. More specifically, he observed that HubSpot was competing well up-market, which he defines as a few thousand employees for a client.
• “Some of the data architecture improvements and product releases that they’ve [HubSpot] done over the past couple of years have really helped to create a 
more flexible platform from a HubSpot standpoint. And then some of the API capabilities / endpoints that they’ve developed and some of the extensibility 
features that they’ve built into the platform, I think have really allowed partners like ourselves to push and extend the platform beyond what it can do natively 
for enterprises.”
• “HubSpot can work as an off-the-shelf solution for a lot of SMBs. But over the years, they’ve really made a push in moving more up-market. And we’re starting to see that 
tide shift a lot in terms of larger and larger mid-market and enterprise companies looking at and considering moving to HubSpot.”
• When it comes to HubSpot’s product innovation cadence, the contact opines that the company has been“really intentional,” while at the same time, describing 
that HubSpot is able to release new solutions at a “blistering” rate, which he believes is a positive for enterprise adoption.

=== PAGE 87 ===

87
Industry Contact 1 – Cont’d
Competitive Environment
• When asked who he typically sees competing with HubSpot, the contact explains “Salesforce has been the stalwart in the CRM space, right? And that’s what everybody is 
chasing after. So we see them a lot.”
• With respect to Salesforce more specifically, the partner opines that “the carrying cost and the implementation cost for Salesforce is quite significant for clients. 
There’s a lot of flexibility. There’s a lot of power underneath, but you really need a major investment to get it to work correctly for your business, right? I think 
the idea of an off-the-shelf type of solution in Salesforce is a misnomer and is not really something that I think many businesses would look at.”
• The partner compared HubSpot and Salesforce to NFL teams. He notes that Salesforce is like the Green Bay Packers. He adds “You’ve got your storied historical team 
that’s always been great. The brand is really strong. People love it. They’re tied into it.HubSpot is like the Eagles. They still have some history, people are getting on 
board with the bandwagon. They’ve been really good over the past several years and are consistent.”
• “Some of the customization and toolsets that HubSpot offers sales teams are starting to sway more people to want to go toward HubSpot instead of 
Salesforce.”
• To a lesser extent, the contact mentions that he competes with Microsoft 365 and Marketo.
Commentary on HubSpot’s Product Offerings
• Observes that Sales Hub and Marketing Hub are the two highest utilized and highest purchased offerings by clients in that order.
• At the same time, the partner opines that HubSpot needs to develop “a little bit” more around certain product areas such as the ability to natively support price books that 
sales teams need. He adds “I think their [HubSpot] quoting tool also could come a long way. They’ve been making investments in it, but I think there’s still a little 
bit to be desired in terms of supporting a natural CPQ motion, quoting, the ability to leverage price books.”
• He explains that the payment solution from HubSpot is still nascent and more SMB focused and as such, he opines that there’s more that can be developed there.
• With respect to Service Hub, he is seeing an increasing amount of clients using it, especially as the company rolled out features like help desk.
• On Ops Hub he indicates “The thing that flies under the hood for a lot of people is Ops Hub because it’s not really truly like a dedicated Hub, right? But, it powers so many 
things that you do at an enterprise level or even just the mid-market level. I think Ops Hub is the thing, the layer from a product perspective, that has allowed a lot of 
that move up-market to happen. You can essentially run your own code. From a serverless function perspective and from a custom coded automation 
perspective, you can do a lot of things within HubSpot natively now because you can now run your own code from within the platform.”
• He observes that essentially all of his clients are taking a multi-hub approach to their HubSpot deployment. There are few instances where a client just has a Sales 
Hub or a Marketing Hub.
Clients Still Trying to Figure Out Breeze
• On the topic of Breeze, which is HubSpot’s AI product suite, the partner notes that there is a lot of hope around it from clients and some teams are benefiting from it. 
However, he admits that it’s very new and most clients are still trying to figure out how to best implement it into their existing workflows.
• “There’s a change management component to it that I don’t think any companies have figured out yet. I think the tool [Breeze] is there, but I just don’t know if many 
customers have figured it out yet.”
HubSpot (HUBS): Deep Dive Interviews (August 2025)

88
Industry Contact 1
General
• The contact is an Elite HubSpot partner that helps implement solutions across the full stack.
• The contact’s firm is primarily focused on larger US customers, with some exposure to international customers.
Demand Environment
• The contact states that while the beginning of 2025 was not terrible, there were some adverse impacts to the partner firm’s business due to uncertainty created by the new 
administration, explaining that while demand didn’t lessen, customers’ decision-making time increased, and thus led to extended sales cycles.
• However, the partner indicates that once the peak of the policy uncertainty was perceived to have passed, customer and deal activity picked up, with sales cycles 
shortening relative to their elevated levels earlier in the year: “Conversations in January closed in March, conversations in March closed in June. Conversations 
we’re having now are closing in 35 and 45 days versus 90 days.”
• The industry expert attributes the better dynamics to the fact that (a) customers are starting to look toward the 2H of the year during which much of consumer spending 
occurs due to holidays and other catalysts, and (b) stakeholders have a desire to spend their existing budgets because if budgets aren’t fully utilized they’re at risk at being 
downsized in the next fiscal year, with the result that customers “have to make a decision,” which is resulting in incremental spending activity.
• Summing their view, the partner is optimistic for their HubSpot business for the remainder of the year, stating that “all indications are middle of August through end of the 
year is going to be pretty [good].”
• The contact expects these trends to serve as a tailwind to HubSpot’s overall business, calling out Breeze as a potential incremental tailwind for HubSpot through the 2H of 
the year as well, and believes that there is a chance for HubSpot to close the year “a little stronger” than some might think.
Customer Buying Patterns in Environment of Elevated Uncertainty
• The industry expert opines that US policy volatility has created uncertainty among businesses and that the lack of visibility to more stable US policy decision-making, such 
as around tariffs and interest rates, is likely to mean this uncertainty is likely to be persistent.
• As a result, businesses are reacting by being less willing to make long-term spending commitments. We note that this is a comment on the current environment and not 
necessarily suggesting an incremental degradation in the environment.
• However, the contact makes the point that the partner firm has adjusted how it approaches customer engagements and contracts and that overall customer spend is not 
reduced, but rather broken up into smaller pieces that are more manageable from a customer perspective while also adopting new products: “Annually we will continue 
to do well, it’s just less predictable.”
HubSpot (HUBS): Deep Dive Interviews (August 2025)

89
Industry Contact 1 – Cont’d
AI & HubSpot Breeze
• Contact continues to see very intense customer interest in AI that is offset by challenges of figuring out how to implement AI in a practical and scalable manner across their 
organizations.
• Notes multiple vectors of pressure on stakeholders to figure out how to implement and adopt AI, the chief of which are (i) boards and CEOs demanding to see progress on 
the AI front and (ii) the potential for competitors to figure it out first and gain a relative advantage.
• The industry expert believes that HubSpot is very well positioned within AI given its integrated, broad platform, history of innovation, and customer trust built over time.
• Opines that the INBOUND conference could serve as a catalyst for incremental AI product adoption as HubSpot rolls out new products/features and customers see their 
competitors utilizing AI to drive real outcomes, explaining that if a customer goes to INBOUND and comes away seeing “a competitor that looks just like their 
company does using [AI solutions] to generate revenue and reduce sales processes from 90 days to two weeks” that could drive meaningful pressure for 
customers to adopt AI, including Breeze.
• The contact is highly constructive on HubSpot Breeze and states that customers are past being pitched about AI as a silver bullet that can solve every problem magically 
and are looking for viable solutions.
• “[Customers] need a realistically scalable piece of technology to help them grow their business, and that’s what Breeze is for them.”
Regarding Salesforce’s Focus on Lower End of Market
• The industry expert views Salesforce as a behemoth whose solution, when properly leveraged, works very well and should not be underestimated.
• However, the contact’s view is that it takes substantial effort, expertise, and resources to run Salesforce properly, which can be a significant hurdle to adoption and proper 
utilization, particularly as you move down market to small and midsized businesses.
• Overall, the partner’s view is that it would take up to 18 months for Salesforce to reconfigure parts of its products to better fit requirements at the lower end of 
the market and to more effectively compete with HubSpot, viewing Salesforce as dominant in enterprise and HubSpot as well positioned within the small and 
midsized business market.

Industry Contact 1
General
• The contact is an Elite HubSpot partner based in the US.
• The contact has completed over a hundred HubSpot implementations throughout his career.
Demand Environment
• Notes that by the end of August, his firm had exceeded 2024’s revenue on an absolute dollar basis. In addition, he is currently operating at a 20%-40% growth 
pace, implying ~30% y/y growth at the midpoint.
• “This has been the best year we’ve ever had.Not only in terms of growth, but beating our projected numbers.”
• Indicates that HubSpot’s revenue growth could accelerate in 2026as its AI Agent marketplace matures and partners build more agents that HubSpot monetizes. The 
contact adds that as AI enables more people to start new businesses, those new companies will need CRMs like HubSpot, creating a loop between AI adoption, 
business formation, and CRM demand.
Google AI Overviews and LLMs
• With respect to Google AI Overview concerns, he does not see it as being an issue for HubSpot.
• “When you type in ‘CRM’ into your ChatGPT or any LLM, HubSpot’s coming up. In fact, we’ve [his practice] gotten more web traffic over the last two to three 
months.”
• He opines that traditional web traffic search will continue to decline as more people use LLMs instead.
Breeze AI Agents
• The partner believes that the most important part of HubSpot’s INBOUND conference was HubSpot’s announcement that 18 out of 21 of the company’s Breeze AI Agents 
are now free for customers to use. As such, only three of the Breeze Agents require payment in the form of credits.
• “That’s [the free Breeze AI Agents] something that people just don’t even understand, that there’s free agents that people can use like for the enrichment of 
records. They [HubSpot] were charging for that and now they’re not. And so, that’s a huge deal.”
• He opines that HubSpot’s medium to long-term goal is to eventually charge customers who are driving high usage with the free Breeze AI Agents. The contact adds, 
“HubSpot won’t start charging customers for these AI Agents until some are proven out [demonstrating value for customers].”
• The partner opines that HubSpot’s new strategy for its Breeze AI Agents is how an “app marketplace” should be. He explains that this will foster more adoption 
of Breeze AI Agents by partners and customers.
• Observes that HubSpot’s Enrichment Agent is seeing broad adoption by his clients.
Contemplating the Price and Value for AI
• The partner mentions that customers, in general, are unsure how to value or price AI tools, especially given that outcomes can now be delivered instantly.
• He compares this to commissioning art: “Let’s say an artist gets commissioned to paint something. The client says, ‘I want a painting, how much does it cost?’ The painter 
says ‘$2,000.’ An hour later, the painter comes back and says, ‘Done.’ The client says, ‘I paid you $2,000 for an hour?’ And the painter says, 'Well yeah, that’s what the 
painting costs.’
• The contact indicates that he is currently working with a customer and this customer believes that he is doing a “full-blown migration” with HubSpot. “They 
[the customer] believe I’m gonna go into the platform and do all these things. Nope. I’m going to go into the platform, I’m going to look at all of the properties 
that they want and I’m going to use AI as middleware and hit a button.” Essentially, he is able to use AI middleware as a bridge layer that connects HubSpot 
with other software platforms without manual coding or API knowledge.

Industry Contact 1
General
• Contact represents an artificial intelligence (AI) company that provides technology related to AI inferencing.
• Contact has visibility into various initiatives across the hyperscaler platforms, including Microsoft Azure, with his organization often serving as a key strategic AI partner 
alongside foundational LLM providers.
Public Cloud Usage / Related AI-Spend
• Shares that among his organization’s sizable footprint of developers, internal use cases have driven usage across the three primary hyperscalers, but that Microsoft Azure 
spend specifically is “small but growing."
• Indicates that among enterprise customers and sovereign clouds, many larger customers are indicating they would like their primary AI-related purchasing to occur 
directly with the hyperscalers, namely Azure, requiring the contact’s organization to pull in the hyperscalers into their own sales efforts.
• Relays that customers are showing “increasing demand” for an AI marketplace, which often centers around their existing hyperscaler footprint; shares that he is seeing this 
demand “for all three” major cloud providers.
• Notes that larger customers in some cases are not looking to create “another budget”for adjacent AI-related hardware and software solutions used in 
inferencing but rather prefer to “buy directly” or “draw down” from their “large and growing” intended public cloud budget.
• Suggests that large customers often “do not want to have different clusters of AI spend in all sorts of different places” but instead want to leverage their existing 
relationships and centralize the contracts or budgets.
• Shares that AI leadership across larger customers, including among those in the Fortune 1000 or Fortune 100, provide feedback to his organization often on the usage and 
strategic direction of the hyperscalers and foundational LLM providers, which often results in discussions related to OpenAI given its market presence and 
awareness.
Federal Efficiency Efforts (DoGE) / Agentic AI
• Believes that as it relates to the new administration’s recently discussed government efficiency initiatives, spending for more modern software solutions 
will “certainly go way up” relating to “application, Agentic, or Copilot use cases," for which “Microsoft is certainly in the lead” on various of these tools.
• Shares that “almost everywhere you turn” there is a push into agents, referencing examples such as telecommunications companies with call centers or Copilots as 
well as large technology companies suggesting they don’t need“middle coders or managers and can cut those people out."
• Indicates that if “agents really take off” and that “certainly there are a lot of bets being made," this could mean thatthe spend will be on applications in terms of what 
you can do to deploy agents and that the data providers and hyperscaler providers underneath “will benefit from all of that compute."
• In terms of who will be the related application software beneficiaries, believes that for the larger, existing systems of record software providers, their success will depend 
on “can they disintermediate themselves or do they get disintermediated” but that they “certainly have the position” of disintermediating themselves to provide 
AI agents that “more or less cut down what a customer would spend on their applications”but in doing so “end up increasing the overall TAM." This could have 
the impact of “cannibalizing existing lines of business to make things more efficient for the customer,"which could be viewed favorably over time as it responds to 
constant customer demands for “better, faster, cheaper."
Microsoft (MSFT): Deep Dive Interviews (January 2025)

unpredictable cost” of OpenAI is giving a lot of customers “heartburn and heart pain."

providers, including OpenAI, sharing a recent example of a customer who described the situation as “unwieldy” and requested alternatives to avoid “going down this path."
• Indicates that some customers mention that “open source could be a great alternative” but that the “quality is not reliable."
• Relays that one of the curious things his organization is seeing that will play out is that “these Chinese models that are open source are actually doing much 
better from a quality perspective than is Meta for example."
• Believes that the “rising challenger” to vendors like OpenAI as of now is Meta and Llama 3.3 in particular, and that from a side-by-side comparison, “if you are 
willing to trade off bumps and bruises on quality for price-performance," “[Llama] is kind of hard to beat from a price standpoint."
• Shares that he is seeing among some customers that “for less sensitive” applications, they are fine to use Llama because it could be “one-tenth or one-twentieth 
of the cost” of OpenAI and even if that “quality is 85%” that could be “good enough” for the specific use case in some instances.
• However, shares that if the customer is “trying to create a world class experience," that is still where “OpenAI dominates."
• Suggests that in six to twelve months, the desire of customers to diversify and bring the prices down with some model providers like OpenAI will continue, as it is likely to 
happen with other AI-related vendors like NVIDIA at the hardware layer, and this could result in CIOs being more willing to identify alternatives that can do “remotely close 
to what” some of the best of breed solutions offer.
• Believes that some of these Chinese models “will force Meta on the open-source side to go a lot faster," noting that others like Mistral “haven’t done much 
lately” and that some of the “open-source players are suffering from lack of quality and have to respond to that."
Oracle Cloud Infrastructure (OCI) / Stargate
• Indicates that Oracle has “done a good job of trying to become relevant again” and that they “have won some large deals," noting that he sees Oracle in some of his 
customer conversations with larger customers.
• However, in the case of Oracle, notes that from a “production” standpoint and given the typical delay of getting these workloads into production, he would not be surprised 
if Oracle is seeing “bookings that are really strong” but “revenue that would lag that significantly."
• Believes that Oracle has a “catch-up to do from an execution perspective” and likely needs to get “a more robust ecosystem of partners around it” so that customers 
can go a lot faster on onboarding to OCI.
• Suggests that his sense is that Oracle is “well-positioned," referencing the recent announcements around Stargate, noting that these investments normally could have 
gone 100% to Microsoft, but that some of it going to Oracle now speaks to “the good position they are in and it’s a phenomenal issue to have."
• Notes that his sense is Oracle will be “even better positioned from a bookings perspective”following the Stargate announcement and will be “well positioned for 
years to come” after coming in as the “dark-horse” in establishing itself as the fourth large cloud provider.

• According to the contact, large-scale modernization programs are gaining traction, with more than four deals exceeding $500M in size initiated in 2024.
• Contact estimates that 80% of the projected 3-5% growth improvement in 2025 will come from existing traditional workloads that have recently transitioned

management.
Microsoft Fabric

its appeal across various segments.
• They believe that Microsoft's bundling strategy for these offerings such as Fabric and Power Automate at attractive terms, is leading to improved adoption rates.
• According to the contact, enterprises are gaining better clarity on their technology roadmaps and efficiency strategies, resulting in increased readiness and willingness to 
utilize these tools.
• However, they acknowledge that while there is a positive trend in adoption, specific data points on utilization are still limited, indicating an area for further observation and 
analysis.

Industry Contact 1
General
• Contact works as a technology consultant with a focus on Microsoft, including AI technologies, with insight across other hyperscalers and large-scale software.
Demand
• Describes consumption growth as “pretty much inline with what we expected it to be”so far into the year.
• The contact opines that many industry participants were expecting AI to lead to more growth, particularly via increased workloads, noting that the disconnect in their view 
originates from the fact that in 2024 “most organizations were still pretty deeply entrenched in evaluation and piloting” vs. being in the position to aggressively invest.
• Comments that many customers have yet to embrace AI enablement of applications, which would be a large driver of growth.
AI Adoption
• In discussing why AI hasn’t yet translated into as meaningful of tailwind as some expected, the contact states that the value proposition has been stuck in the “end 
user productivity realm," but that for most customers “that’s not enough… [customers] are looking for something more meaningful," specifically AI use cases that provide an 
order of magnitude improvement to outcomes.
• The industry expert comments that while some customers have achieved these outcomes, it has been in very specific, more narrow use cases.
• When asked whether this is an issue of the technology or the price, the contact suggests that to really gain the value that exists within these tools, such as Microsoft 
Copilot, companies are finding they need to change how employees work and how workflows are structured, making the time-to-value more elongated and 
leading to some to view AI tools as overly expensive.
• “There’s no doubt that there are some [specific companies] who are getting enormous value from AI, but it’s hard to do that generically at scale [and across different 
companies].”
• Provides the analogy of Excel, pointing out that the value that Excel provides to users varies drastically both across different companies / industries and different 
departments within a given company.
• Opines that this challenge is the reason why Agents are seeing so much traction because the Agents can not only interact with the LLMs, but they also can be 
designed to take the insights and translate them into actions, thereby bridging the gap that currently exists with human labor leveraging AI effectively.
• In discussing Microsoft’s competitive positioning with Agents, the contact states thatthey don’t believe Microsoft is behind in any way with Agents but does note that 
the first-mover advantage Microsoft had with Copilots vs. competitors isn’t present with Agents, where there is a tighter competitive dynamic among industry 
players.
• Takes a positive tone on AI adoption over the longer-term, believing it will create significant value for customers over time.
Hyperscaler CapEx
• The industry expert notes that they are seeing adjustments to CapEx spending plans at the margin, but comments that these build-outs are at massive scale and very 
complex, all the while needing to be sensitive to the level of demand and location of where data centers need to be to serve the demand.
• Adds that Microsoft appears to be sticking to its initial publicly stated commitments on the whole and sees Microsoft as having the “benefit of the doubt” in their eyes, 
suggesting that nothing they’re seeing play out in the marketplace is giving them “cause for alarm."
• Continues to see power as one the major constraints for data center buildouts.
Microsoft (MSFT): Deep Dive Interviews (May 2025)

Industry Contact 1 – Cont’d
European Customer Cloud Strategies
• When asked whether there is some dynamic of European customers becoming less enthusiastic about adopting U.S.-based cloud solutions, the contact comments that

• Because of that, the contact expects European customers to continue to rely upon U.S.-based hyperscaler businesses, perhaps with more mixed cloud

• Believes Microsoft is well-positioned in European markets due to penetration of Microsoft’s full stack, particularly with productivity / M365, providing a competitive 
advantage.
Microsoft Scale Motion
• The industry expert explains that Microsoft has been focused on not only being sure it doesn’t ‘miss’ the AI trend, but also that it is at the forefront of it.
• However, most customers who are being pitched AI are not prepared for that step and in some cases are still focused on more traditional cloud migrations.
• As a result, the contact believes that this dynamic has led to some pockets of mismatch with customers and some churn and confusion, resulting in the challenges 
Microsoft has been seeing.
• Comments that they are actively seeing Microsoft try to find the right balance in its approach.
Potential Disruptions from Tariffs, Other Uncertainties
• The industry expert notes that recent U.S. policy announcements as well as public market reactions have been very volatile and caused significant uncertainty; however, 
their view is that this uncertainty is more pronounced in the political and financial spheres, whereas companies themselves have been a little more stable in comparison.
• The contact explains that while there is some negative effect resulting from these dynamics and certain customers have become incrementally more nervous, 
there isn’t yet a“major effect," with most customers more “watching and waiting."

Industry Contact 2 – Cont’d
Public Cloud Commitments
• Indicates that is firm saw a “peaking of the next 12 months committed dollars”on a y/y growth basis [commit growth] in November and December across the 
hyperscaler platforms, while January was “pretty flat” and in February he “started to see a decline."
• Suggests that one of the major factors for this slowdown initially was customers electing to delay renewals by a few months, such as into March, but then “obviously March 
happened," alluding to the increasing uncertainty around tariffs and other geopolitical tensions over the last several weeks.
• In evaluating y/y growth in commits over the last few months, indicates that from January to March of this year, trends have taken a “dive," noting “a lot of that is due 
to non-renewal." In specific numbers, relays that y/y growth in cloud commitments across all customers and clouds has moved from 29% in January, to 27% in 
February, and to 24% in March.
• For April, indicates that the y/y growth in cloud commitments is “probably dropping again," driven by the impacted customer cohorts of “GenAI tourists and 
enterprise migrating segments," while core AI companies remain “quite strong."
• Shares that his firm started to see a “slight deceleration” in commits exiting February and then a “really a steep deceleration” as they hit March, noting that this 
was largely due to customers “putting things on pause”and looking to “wait and see," which he believes could extend until July.
• Notes that his firm is “definitely seeing elongation of cycles on individual contract renewals” as of the week and a half post ‘Liberation Day’(April 2nd tariff 
announcements).
• Believes that if customers do have incremental workloads that they were planning to migrate, they are potentially now considering taking the “low estimate instead of the 
high estimate” as they consider what their commitment is going to look like.
• Notes that this “particular psychology change” has been happening over the course of a few months, even before ‘Liberation Day,' but has “definitely 
accelerated [i.e. worsened/deteriorated] in the wake of that” as of mid-April.
• From a vertical standpoint, shares that some of his customers in the furniture and automotive segments have been under pressure since Q3 of last year, but that “come 
February and March that started to widen out” to more broad e-commerce and some gaming customers as well.
• Comments that in his view, “the 90-day moratorium [on some tariffs] hasn’t changed much”as it relates to the above dynamics.
Digital Natives vs. Enterprise Commits
• Shares that on a percentage basis, the deceleration in y/y commit growth is “sharper” among the enterprise cohort, though on a dollars basis is greater out of the 
digital natives due to “a lot more dollars” driven by this latter group.
• In terms of the magnitude, though with high variance, describes an average deceleration in the enterprise cohort of ~25% going to “sub 20%” (18-19%) in y/y 
commit growth, while on the digital native side, the decel has been a “little bit tighter”and gone from ~25% prior to ~21-22% more recently, on average.
• Describes that enterprises are in a state in which they have moved the data-first apps and have a little bit more of optional spend available today, so when they choose to 
put stuff on pause, “that is very much like a gap down."
• For the digital natives, suggests they are more in the state of identifying their core cost of goods sold related workloads, and while in December they were willing to take 
their “mid forecast,"as of February and March that has shifted toward more commonly taking the “low forecast."
• Notes that AWS and GCP continue to carry a little more digital native exposure, while Azure and others tend to have more migrating enterprise exposure.
• However, identifies that there are still “very consistent, fast growing firms” in the AI space and that “those aren’t going away, and that a lot of the startups, especially with 
“some of the funding markets back," “they’re not really impacted here." While the largest number of legacy dollars continues to be from these enterprise migrating and 
digital natives, and though that’s where most of the “growth dollars” still are, he notes that the “fastest growing segments” are actually outside of that in AI and there 
continues to be a “strong trend there."

Industry Contact 2 – Cont’d
Public Cloud Consumption/Usage
• In contrast, indicates that his firm is seeing a “flattening but no deceleration”in actual cloud consumption, bifurcating relative to some of the cloud commitment 
trends.
• Shares that “even if there is a little bit of a consumption drop” as customers commit less, the realized rates these customers are getting on the consumption is higher than 
what they would have gotten had they renewed at that same commitment level.
• As a result, indicates that even if there is a slight drop in consumption, his firm is seeing that “almost maybe accounted for in full” due to these higher rates and thus the 
“meter dollars aren’t as affected right now."
• Believes that his firm is not seeing a “dramatic deceleration in consumption," “even in the first week and some change of April, post Liberation Day."
• However, highlights some customer-specific exceptions, including one of his firm’s furniture customers going through a “large layoff," though noting they had been under 
pressure since Q3 of last year when they were doing a “flat commit," as well as an automotive customer doing layoffs earlier in the year and having some sensitivity to 
interest rates.
• Suggests that while there continues to be “uncertainty," customers are “going to pay that premium” for higher rates with lower committed volumes until they can lock in for 
the rate they had last year.
• Notes that because of greater on-demand usage, “margins are going to look great” for the hyperscalers because customers are paying higher-rates on demand, 
commenting that “so in that sense," “it’s good for cloud."
• Shares that for the first time since 2023, customers are more interested in a “renegotiation of a private pricing agreement” due to their “shrinking budgets," which 
often leads to the customer’s total number of dollars remaining the same, but the customer looking to add a year to their contract to provide them with more time to fully 
consume their credits. Notes that his firm started to see this discussion around public sector renewals in February, due to “DOGE or whatever else," but that since 
April 2nd, his firm is now hearing this among commercial clients as well.
• On the above dynamic, notes that while hearing discussions, he has not necessarily seen this occur in practice outside of the public sector, though that reps of 
hyperscalers “are definitely aware” of this interest and “willing to work with the customer at this point."
Composition of Migration Activity
• Indicates that “coming out of a very strong 2024," there were also some “year-over-year effects that also made this [slowdown] kind of inevitable, if not in Q1 then in Q2” as 
it relates to some of the softer cloud commitment and consumption trends.
• Shares that, “most sharply in the Microsoft case," a lot of the cloud migrations that were “re-accelerated” or “restarted” in 2024 after a “very tepid 2023” for the migrating 
enterprise segment of his customers were not like the migrations seen in 2021 and 2022, the latter of which were more “cost of goods sold type of workloads” (such as 
serving a website for example).
• In contrast, relays that the workloads he saw moved in 2024 were more related to data sets, ETL processes, and analytics pipelines, which he notes Microsoft branded as 
‘Intelligent Apps,' He notes that these workloads led to a “small increase in consumption in Q1 and Q2” of last year as customers focused on migrating their data before 
then driving a “really strong acceleration” in consumption coming out of Q2 and into Q3 as customers-built compute workloads on top of that data.
• However, shares that once these types of workloads are built, they do not scale with the number of end users nor do they turn into the “digital natives of tomorrow," but 
instead “they have a lever, and [customers] can kind of pull back on it” and if customers are not choosing to invest actively, “that spend growth sort of flattens out."
• Indicates that with comps getting harder in Q2 and Q3, customers not looking to put a “whole bunch more spend in to get a whole bunch more value” on these 
workloads and having optionality to “pull back the lever if [they] want," his firm expected some flattening of consumption growth coming out of the Q4 renewal 
period.

Industry Contact 2 – Cont’d
Microsoft ‘Scale’Motion & Traditional Migrations
• On the previously described dynamic relating to migration activity, notes that he believes Microsoft “understand[s] this”and he is hearing from large partners that 
Microsoft more recently has focused its messaging on “back to the basics," including for example VMware migrations and SQL Server migrations. However, he notes 
this is emblematic not just in the Microsoft ecosystem, but of the broader migrating enterprises across Amazon, and to a “much smaller extent” Google, where there is 
a “forceful” push to get customers to bring those “cost of goods sold” type workloads back in the back half of the year as he does not believe “anything right 
now is happening in the front half of the year” or “at least has not been committed to or planned."
• Suggests that these core workloads, which he refers to as cost of goods sold workloads, are ones that “consistently compound with data gravity and grow," while those 
workloads migrated in the Intelligent App space, at least to date, haven’t been those “explosive, consistent growers” in the same way, in his view.
• Describes that Microsoft’s go-to-market (GTM) strategy over the last few years on the direct side of the business has been “deeper with fewer," while continuing to focus 
heavily on its channel-drive motion. Mentions that a lot of Microsoft’s customers on the startup side all the way through to the legacy software customers on enterprise 
agreements are managed through the channel ecosystem and through direct and indirect CSPs or channel providers.
• Based on his conversations working with a couple dozen of Microsoft’s big channel partners, comments thatlast year Microsoft was heavily focused on incentivizing 
its partner ecosystem to distribute incremental AI licenses and were willing to offer discounts to these partners around some of these services, such as Copilot, Azure 
OpenAI Service, and Fabric, to encourage partners to focus heavily on adoption and engagement of Microsoft’s AI suite.
• Shares that his firm saw “a lot of free Fabric” rolling out from a credit program perspective last year, “even into December."
• However, starting this year, suggests that Microsoft has shifted to a “back to the basics”message potentially under a new person running their channel programs, where it 
is now applying some of those incentivizing credits to accelerating licenses for the migration of traditional platform and database migrations referred to as 
a “migration acceleration program (MAP)," such as migrating a VMware platform into a serverless architecture, SQL Server workloads, or through strategic relationships 
with Oracle to bring “Oracle databases and licenses through at a lower cost."
• Overall, describes that there has definitely been a change from a “programmatic” and “messaging” perspective from Microsoft into the channel, with the shift 
being more oriented toward these longer-term, cost of goods sold type migrations. However, notes that this is not a “long term-oriented commitment 
environment right now," questioning the appetite of customers to run these migrations due to economic uncertainty and the double spend required in the 
short-term as they switch over.
• In a more optimistic view, shares there is a potential that things like VMware migrations “rapidly accelerate” given the cost increases these customers are facing from 
VMware, which could lead migrating enterprise customers to seek savings by bringing those production platform workloads over to the hyperscalers. On VMware-related 
migrations specifically, calls out “inordinate price increases”VMware is passing to its install base making the vendor a “top five cost optimization line item."
Microsoft (MSFT): Deep Dive Interviews (May 2025)

=== PAGE 101 ===

101
Industry Contact 2 – Cont’d
Impacts of Lower-Inferencing Costs / DeepSeek
• Notes that beginning in Q4 and post the DeepSeek R-1 launch, some of the “smart CIOs” of his customers began to discuss how much the software side of AI over the last 
year “has made the tokens so much cheaper."
• Suggests that as it relates to Jevons paradox, increasingly the number of opportunities to go and apply tokens is the limiting factor rather than the “cost of the tokens 
themselves," particularly for traditional enterprises.
• Describes that there continue to be key differences in AI usage among enterprises and digital natives, noting that startups, ISVs, and digital natives are encoding AI into 
their platform and having it as an end-user feature, with every customer hitting their website generating incremental tokens. In contrast, notes that the traditional 
enterprises that are deploying AI today are using it “very much for internal use cases," including relating to R&D.
• Shares that this mindset that tokens for AI can be procured for “cheap” has “permeated the entire ecosystem” and led CIOs to realize they may not have to 
increase their R&D budgets as they consider AI deployments.
• Characterizes AI token usage into three segments, (1) GenAI tourists (migrating enterprises), who may be relatively flat as they still identify where to apply and deploy AI, 
(2) AI startups and R&D heavy companies, who are still growing very quickly and using thousands of GPUs, and (3) support platforms (ISVs for example), who are now 
deploying AI into production and seeing high volumes of token usage to handle support queries.
• Believes that DeepSeek and ensuing models that enable inference-time reasoning for lower costs, including some OpenAI models, have contributed to some of 
the spending slowdown in traditional enterprise commits due to the lower costs, while not having much of an impact on the AI labs and inference focused 
organizations, which are still “stepping to the right."
• References one customer of his firm that offers a support platform that “stepped up their Azure OpenAI commit from $250K to $5M," strictly because they turned 
on a chatbot and that “every single support query” now receives a GenAI response.
• Shares that there is a new race that has started among these “fast-scaling inference folks” including code-generation, support, and enterprise search customers that are 
generating “for every end user query, some token usage that just kind of explodes." Provides an example of a code-generation platform provider that “upped 
their commit by 10x and they’re doing all the clouds," “getting as many cloud tokens as possible, maxing it out and having to get more capacity."
• Indicates that now customers can get OpenAI o1 level inference reasoning at a “tenth of the cost, or even lower” relative to a few months ago and that things have only 
accelerated with the emergence of OpenAI o3-mini and other models, with the increasing competition further driving the price down across the market.
• Shares that in 2023, there was this “massive flood of startups” that moved into Azure and that he had “never seen startups go to Azure” prior as AWS and GCP was 
perceived as more “developer friendly." However, notes the draw of OpenAI “GPT” led Azure to become the second or third cloud for this cohort; however, notes that while 
that is still true for several startups that need OpenAI, the average startup is now “slowing down on its rate of multi-cloud” as they benchmark tokens from all the 
model providers.
U.S. Federal Exposure
• Estimates that as it relates to US Federal government exposure among the hyperscalers, he believes AWS is “slightly more on the federal side” as it relates to his 
customer cohorts, while “Azure is a little bit more distributed”with state and local government contract exposure.
• As a result, believes that from a DOGE perspective, “it would be AWS that’s seen a little bit more of the PPA [private pricing agreement] flow down versus the max 
flow down from Azure," though notes that is “actually kind of more universal” as the spending efficiency efforts are “hitting both sides of the ledger, the state and the 
federal."
Microsoft (MSFT): Deep Dive Interviews (May 2025)

• Suggests that there has been a “lot of efficiency gains that is driving the inference demand," noting dynamics such as increasing price performance gains per hardware 
generation.
• Describes that as he evaluates per model generation growth, he is seeing examples of “3xing, 5xing, even 10xing” in certain client instances as it relates to 
model performance, speaking to the speed of advancement in AI.
• In terms of the use cases driving the increase in inferencing activity, describes further model enhancement as a popular focus area, in addition to customers building or 
deploying more agents, for which the use cases have “exponentially exploded in the last four to five months." Describes some of the common focus areas of 
Agentic deployments including many business-led functions and IT DevOps, but increasingly broadening to other areas as these tools and capabilities mature.
• Relating to this growth in cloud infrastructure demand and deployment of AI tools like Agents, characterizes the Observability market as a “bright spot," noting that these AI 
applications have “APM and infrastructure monitoring needs," and thatthe strength he is seeing in core cloud infrastructure and inferencing activity is “definitely 
correlated” to benefits to Observability vendors, such as Datadog and Dynatrace.
Microsoft Copilot
• In terms of Microsoft Copilot adoption rates, indicates that it varies by customer size and industry vertical, noting that in high-adoption subsegments he has 
seen adoption among customers between 60-85%; however, notes that for full-scale enterprise deployments, adoption rates remain lower in the range of 10-
15% on average.
• In terms of enterprise adoption and the “cross expansion of Copilot across existing enterprises," suggests that this is “still trending slightly lower than what we expect it to 
be”; though notes that the“adoption is higher” within the “mid-market and SMEs” (Small and Medium-sized Enterprises).
• Looking forward, believes that from an enterprise adoption and expansion standpoint, expects the 10-15% adoption rate to be “inching toward 20% or so”in the 
next six to 12 months, though notes that this dynamic “takes time."
Microsoft Cloud Apps Demand
• In terms of the areas of the tech stack being impacted negatively because of recent budget changes, points toward the application software stack, including 
tools oriented around “enterprise collaboration” and apps on the “back-office side," including relating to ERP.
• As a result, mentions Microsoft Dynamics and Office 365 as some of the products “slightly getting deprioritized”in terms of recent customer spending, noting 
that some customers perceive a lower “return on investment“relative to other potential investment areas.
• In discussing broader macro trends, indicates that “there’s a lot of uncertainty” and thatclients are either keeping their budgets or are delaying projects, including 
those related to “Dynamics, Office 365, and Teams”in some cases.
• Indicates that he expects faster infrastructure software growth to be partially offset by slower application software growth “for the remainder of the year” for his 
firm’s Microsoft practice, and that he doesn’t believe “we will get out of this” until likely next year when budgets will be refreshed.
• Suggests that this “slight deprioritization” is likely to “continue to sustain," which will in turn result in an “expansion in sales cycles” and deal elongations.
• Clarifies that this dynamic as it relates to Microsoft’s cloud app suite is not occurring across “every single” customer of his firm and that he continues to have customers 
actively deploying some of Microsoft’s cloud products, such as Dynamics, noting that“it’s not like everybody has put a freeze on it."
Microsoft (MSFT): Deep Dive Interviews (July 2025)

Industry Contact 1
General
• The contact observes $4.7B of annualized spend data related to the hyperscalers and an array of SaaS products. Notably, about 50% of the spend is AWS, 25% is Azure,
