Demand Environment
• “On the Azure side, it’s been a little bit more universally up and to the right… consumption has been consistently going higher after a little bit of a flattening in 
May and June… 31% growth in May, 31.5% in June. We ended August at 33.1% on the consumption side, so that's been steadily picking up, par ticularly in September… 
With the commitments, we saw a low of 26% growth with some pushed renewals in April/May. Those ended up getting signed in June/July, a little larger than 
expected. We are ending ~29.2% in August.”
• Expects consumption to continue accelerating for Azure, although they anticipate commitment acceleration to slow down a bit with some of the September 
contracts. 
Microsoft “Back to Basics” Migrations & Why Azure is Landing Production Workloads
• “To the credit of Microsoft, they started banging the drum on this… cloud platform migrations, VMware migration, SQL Server m igrations. They started doing 
that in February… first -party SQL Server, the deal with Oracle… first -party VMware service, first-party Nutanix service… They wo n a disproportionate number of 
these in the back half of Q1 and through Q3 relative to AWS.”
• “Back in Q1… [Insight], the biggest CSP/MSP in the Azure ecosystem… all they were saying is back to the basics - VMware migration, SQL Server migrations… a big push 
there and a lot of contracts were signed through Q2… they were early, Microsoft had that tune at the beginning of the year.”
• Notes that while data/AI played factors, on the margin, there were many other things that made the decisions easier for enterprises to put production workloads on Azure 
related to AWS, opposite of what was seen in ’21 and ’22.
• “We're already seeing now in September, and that's going to accelerate through October and November, those big OLTP/VMware-exit migrations start to land, 
and that's going to accelerate, and that's why I think Azure, particularly, because they were earlier on this, is already seeing consumption acceleration that will 
continue through October/November.”
Oracle–Microsoft (OCI - Azure) Partnership Impacts
• Explains that part of the reason Azure was winning migrations is due to a deal Microsoft had with Oracle, which resulted in 1) better pricing on Oracle server migrations into 
Azure, and 2) allowed customers to use Oracle OCI spend to burn down committed spend on Azure, which launched this year, and he mentions was “the craziest deal” he 
had seen in cloud.
• "That partnership basically has driven some marginal workloads, mostly R&D, not very sticky to Oracle, very small from some of these enterprise migrating folks. But 
it's driven a lot of Oracle, especially with some of these OLTP type migrations. Then, a lot of like Oracle servers that we've seen land on Azure. We're seeing a lot more 
now with Oracle at AWS land on AWS as well. So, there's been a definite acceleration on that side of the business that we're seeing through the hyperscalers themselves."
OpenAI on Azure / Microsoft Training Bump
• Observes that Microsoft has “definitely got the bump” from capturing workloads on the training side of AI. He notes that part of the strength is due to the proprietary 
OpenAI API available in the Microsoft ecosystem which he says Azure “definitely saw a continued acceleration” from.
• “We’re seeing provisioned-throughput deals - 5, 10, 20 million - which has just been consistently growing throughout the quarter for Microsoft.”
• “We work with an ISV that's been around forever... and they had to add AI in the last few years. They were fully based on AWS, but because they based their AI product 
around Azure, they had to replicate that whole environment into Azure to serve GPT tokens. They had a $5 million commit they started the year with just for provision 
throughput units on Azure. That's gone up to like $12 million of committed spend provision throughput units on Azure right now."

• “It’s been a constant flow of deal opportunities in June. In terms of just Monday overall, still fairly healthy demand.”

Monday’s AI Offerings More Likely to Be Adopted by SMB
• With respect to Monday’s AI features, he has observed that his SMB customers are more interested rather than his enterprise clients.
• “Our larger customers have to bring their own LLM if they want to use Monday’s AI solutions and because of that, they don’t use the Monday AI credits. 
Monday knows that they aren’t going to get everyone on this [AI credits], but they are going to get the SMB players, the people who aren’t as restricted when it 
comes to data security.”
• He notes that rather than using a Monday AI credit, enterprises can leverage Monday’s infrastructure, bring their own LLM, and then just make an API call 
outbound to “trigger events” within Monday. Additionally, he mentions that enterprises get 250K API calls with Monday every month.
Observes Changes to Monday’s Pricing for Automation
• While doing implementation work with a customer, the partner noticed that Monday started to charge customers for any automation, even if that automation can also trigger 
some other automation.
o “Historically, Monday automations charged one action per API call. So, when data arrives, then do A, B, C, D, or E. You can do like five or six different 
actions at once based on when data arrives, like a status change or an invoice. They [Monday] used to charge you only one action per API call, but now 
they are charging you for every action or trigger. So, a single event that triggers five other steps will cost you 5x rather than just one. It’s still pennies 
per action, but that can add up.” [It’s worth noting that we are thus far unable to independently confirm or deny the partner’s claims].
• While the partner notes that the customer was okay with paying the unexpected cost for now, that customer was still spending below their total budget for Monday.

Industry Contact 1
General
• The contact is an APAC customer in the energy sector.
Adopts OneStream as Part of ERP Upgrade Cycle
• The contact’s firm serves around 1M customers, which makes the financial forecasting and budgeting process complex.
• Her company used to have a legacy financial forecasting system that was in place for 10-15 years [IBM based]. The system couldn’t handle the complexity in terms of her 
firm’s budgeting, forecasting, and reporting, leading the customer to review its related software stack.
• Roughly two years ago, her company decided to adopt SAP for their ERP system, which displaced Ellipse.
• “The amount of data just exploded in terms of what the tool needed to handle, and the people on my team were fiddling around with spreadsheets trying to 
handle a million customer data records; it just wasn’t viable. We, internally, had data scientists building a python sort of regression analysis to do financial 
forecasting around customer revenue, and that was a really painful experience.”
• She notes that her team evaluated leading ERP suppliers in the Gartner Magic Quadrant and tested with all of them.
• The ERP upgrade was a catalyst for her company to adopt a new forecasting and budgeting system. Her company went with OneStream because it met her 
firm’s requirements around budgeting, forecasting, and ingesting large amounts of data without “reducing the granularity” that her firm needed.
• “The python code was pretty much a black box. Nobody understood why the numbers came out the way they did.”As such, she indicated that her company initially 
adopted OneStream’s AI module [SensibleAI forecast] to resolve this.
Expansion Plans with OneStream, Potentially Doubling Spend in 3-5 Years
• She is currently on the “back end” of implementing OneStream’s core platform,which will replace her firm’s legacy technology and will go live in early 2026.
• She notes that her team currently produces a lot of financial reports and management reports manually that sit in between IBM T1, spreadsheets, and e-mails. Her team 
would like to use OneStream to take away a lot of that manual process so that self-service reporting is enabled.
• “So hopefully we can eliminate that time to report and so that we can free up people’s time. But also at the same time,I think it’s the agility of our operations and our 
business that our stakeholders can get access to financial results much quicker and get to a level of granularity that we just couldn’t do with spreadsheets.”
• While the core solution for OneStream will be used next year for the finance teams, she opines that OneStream can be used for much more. For instance, she notes that 
OneStream can also ingest data around HR planning as well as project planning outside of financial systems.
• The customer indicates that it’s possible that her company’s spend with OneStream could “at least double” in the next 3 - 5 years. She adds “the data always 
grows over time in terms of what we want to use AI on, on the forecasting side. I think for licenses, the core platform is based on the number of users. I don’t think our 
business users will grow particularly that much, but potentially a little bit.”
• For financial consolidation, her company is currently using spreadsheets, but is moving that into OneStream as well.
OneStream (OS): Deep Dive Interviews (November 2025)

116
Industry Contact 1 – Cont’d
OneStream Stood Out Amongst Many Options
• Notes that her company evaluated Anaplan, IBM (TM1), SAP, and Oracle, with the latter being the runner-up to OneStream.
• “We definitely spent a lot of time [with OneStream and Oracle] and I feel that the time we invested was definitely worthwhile.I do think we made the right choice in terms 
of where we landed.”
• Indicates that she was satisfied with how OneStream implemented the AI module for her company. She adds, “they [OneStream] promised a 12 week implementation 
for the AI module and it really took us six weeks...We were running parallel processes with the legacy tool in two months and had enough confidence that 
OneStream AI was giving us the results we were expecting.”
• “The best part is that they give you the transparency around the regression models so that we could explain which driver was actually relevant to the forecast, which we 
couldn’t do with our legacy model or any other tool that we saw on the market at that time.”
• “SAP works for the core ERP transaction level but for budgeting, forecasting, planning, or financial analytics you need that flexibility, which is not their [SAP] strong suit.”
• She notes that the budgeting and forecasting process with SAP is a lot of “trial and error.” In addition, she adds that there is also a lot of maintenance and support around 
SAP too, which makes it less agile and costly.
Commentary on AI
• Indicates that OneStream’s AI module is charged based on the number of units, which is the intersection of the data and the forecasting drivers. “The more sub-segments 
of data that we want to forecast, which is where the intersection happens, the cost [for OneStream] goes up.”
• “I still think the quicker ROI [from AI] would be from automating a manual process today and then overlaying some of the very simple judgement decisions that a team 
needs to make, and a lot of the time it’s simple calculations… But with that, everyone I speak to is a bit nervous around how AI is reliable… We want to start with 
areas where if the AI gets it wrong, it doesn't matter as much. It's low risk.”
• She notes that the payback for any AI project needs to be short, given the rapid pace of innovation from AI (e.g. ChatGPT 4 to ChatGPT 5). “The payback period 
for any AI project we’re doing is going to take two years at a minimum.”
OneStream (OS): Deep Dive Interviews (November 2025)

Industry Contact 1
Background
• Contact works for a leading national electrical contractor; contact’s work focuses primarily on data centers.
• Contact’s firm works with hyperscalers, data center owners, and others involved in the data center supply chain.
• Contact is in the middle of doing the electrical construction work for Stargate at the Abilene, Texas site.
Abilene, TX Facility & Data Center Buildout Constraints
• Contact states that construction has been going on for a little over a year now.
• While there are about 10 buildings intended for the Abeline site at the moment, the contact’s firmwas able to get the first building, which will require a little over 
100MW of power, up and running in under a year and described this as an “incredible accomplishment."
• The contact explains that there are 10 buildings in total expected at this point. With each requiring ~100MW of power, this should translate to at least 1GW of power 
needed once everything is completed, and notes that the pace of the buildout represents a “record time."
• “Everything’s operational, everything’s running…how or what they’re using it for I don’t know. What I can tell you is we got the first 100 megawatts up and 
running in record speed. Had it up and running two months ago, and we'll have the second 100MW+ building up and running this month.”
• When asked whether the industry expert believes the building they completed is already being utilized by the owner, they comment that they don’t know for certain, but 
that data center vacancy rates are very low right now and “so I can’t imagine they’re not using it."
• The industry expert opines that power bottlenecks in the industry right now for these large AI training data centers “can be problematic," and can cause some 
projects to get delayed or scaled back a little bit as they scale.
• Adds that the original plan for the Abeline site was for 20 buildings with 100MWs each, but power challenges arose. Currently, there is line of sight to 10 buildings, and 
notes that “everything is on a quick, quick schedule."
• It is also explained that plans for the Abeline site are evolving in real time and that there is potential “to go to 20 buildings once the power problem becomes 
alleviated."
• Contact comments that their firm is already starting execute purchases and work on the next Stargate site, “which we expect to be even bigger… as long as 
they don't run into power bottlenecks."
• Broadly, the contact’s firm does work across different regions, including Northern Virginia, and work in North and Northwest Texas, including Dallas Fort-Worth. Contact 
notes that a lot of work is moving out West, where land is cheaper and power is more readily available.
• “The next big Stargate Project that we're kicking off is in Amarillo. That's the bigger one.”
Oracle (ORCL): Deep Dive Interviews (September 2025)

118
Industry Contact 1 – Cont’d
Data Center Buildout Activity
• Regarding activity among the hyperscalers, the contact calls out Amazon, Google, Meta, and Microsoft as being very active. They note that Amazon has the most going on, 
which may be due to the scale of Amazon’s AWS business, and Meta is described as being “very aggressive."
• “Microsoft has had starts and stops on some of their projects… Microsoft probably runs into more of the power constraints than others.”
• Opines that at times, industry participants will hear about a Microsoft project being shelved and begin to worry about what that means for the AI buildout trend. 
However, the industry expert comments that Microsoft is “casting many, many nets… and they’re not expecting every one of those projects [to come to 
fruition]” and, more importantly, Microsoft doesn’tneed every project to complete because of its approach of ‘casting more nets than they need,'
• In discussing the AI data center buildout more broadly, the contact points to two key indicators for the robustness of demand: vacancy rates and pre-leasing activity.
• Regarding vacancy rates, this metrics appears to be sitting in 2-3% range, which is quite low and described as “extremely tight," and even lower in certain geographies.
• Pre-leasing activity is quite strong as well, with the statement that locations are being pre-leased “much faster than before . We've seen that you can pre-lease 
data centers today just by having land and power, that's all you need. You can pre-lease it. No longer do you have to have all the civil site work [and other 
related site work] done… if you just have the land and the power agreement, you can lease it now early.”
• Data center demand is characterized as still “very robust” with contractors turning down work because of human resources constraints.
Supply-Demand Balance
• When asked whether supply-demand for data centers is going to come into balance soon, the contact again emphasizes power as a key constraint.
• Regarding power, it is explained that while there are efforts to get more power online, there are also countervailing factors, such as the administration removing incentives 
and otherwise slowing clean energy development, meaning that some potential power is being lost there.
• Alternatively, the industry expert notes that other potential sources of energy, such as gas plants, face constraints as well, with certain OEMs quoting 2032 for the deliver of 
gas turbines needed at natural gas plants.
• “Power is going to continue to be the bottleneck.”
Oracle (ORCL): Deep Dive Interviews (September 2025)

Industry Contact 2
Background
• Contact leads an Oracle-focused practice that provides advisory services across Oracle’s ecosystem. 
• The contact works with large customers based in the US and internationally.
OCI, Data, & AI
• Contact calls out closer partnership between OpenAI and Oracle to enable deeper, more seamless ChatGPT usage across the Oracle software ecosystem.
• Contact opines that Oracle’s strategy in this regard is to emphasize that “it is all about the data” because AI needs data to function optimally, which Oracle believes will 
accrue to its advantage.
• Contact adds that Oracle is going to continue to be the database of record for many large enterprises.
• Contact states that Oracle made a big move toward verticalization a little while ago, but that it is now rolling that back somewhat and focusing more so on accounts with 
large on-prem footprints and databases and working to migrate them onto Oracle OCI.
• It is explained that Oracle has made good progress shifting customers from on-prem to Oracle’s cloud environment, with ~50-60% of licenses being on-prem 
compared to 80-90% several years ago.
• The contact comments that Oracle is very concerned with ensuring existing database customers continue to utilize Oracle for that purpose, even if it means they will run 
those workloads at competitors’ data centers.
OCI Commitment Dynamics
• The contact explains that Oracle carries a very large RPO balance that includes large OCI-related business deals.
• However, it is noted that some of these OCI contracts are “not an absolute commitment” and that it is important for Oracle to ensure customers start driving utilization 
and consumption to actually realize the contracted values.
• Expands that Oracle is leaning on its partner ecosystem for just this purpose, urging partners to find more and more workloads for customers to consume on OCI, including 
working to get customers to migrate third-party workloads onto OCI.

AI Adoption and Impact (cont’d)

• [SI Contact]: “I find that for most enterprises, the barrier to adopting AI is having fragmented data. I think Oracle’s AI Data Platform brings it together. You have frontier

• [SI Contact]: “AI itself isn’t forcing cloud migrations. But it amplifies the need for cloud-based data access. Making a cloud transition a pre-requisite to use AI.”
• [SI Contact]: “AI’s scalability won’t be constrained by technology, but more by energy availability and governance frameworks. If I’m a utility, how am I 
supposed to allocate power? ”

• [SI Contact]: “I think software as we know it today won’t exist. AI is the new interface rather than applications. Middleware will remain relevant because 
middleware helps with securing orchestration and governance to connect data sources and manage access between AI models and systems.”
• [SI Contact]: “The AI adoption curve is steady but cautious. Oracle does have the architectural advantage, but clients are taking more time than I would think to

124
Industry Contact 1
General
• Contact works for a global systems integrator (GSI) as a senior consultant.
• Contact has a particular focus on marketing technologies (MarTech), with high-level insight across the broader business.
Demand Environment
• The industry expert opines that the demand environment has been neutral-to-negative through the year and into the October / November period overall, and 
negative for the MarTech practice specifically.
• The negative trending of customer software buying patterns is attributed primarily to two vectors: (a) an increasingly complex, opaque, and confusing array of product 
SKUs and (b) customers worried about what their IT estate should look like and how they can future-proof investments given the rise of AI and Agentforce 
specifically.
• When asked to expand on the thought of Agentforce potentially causing some customer future proofing-related caution, the contact confirms this is the case and 
states that customers are very impressed with the demos they’re shown with Agentforce, but that this also causes them to question existing spending and 
intended spending.
• Expanding on the thought, the contact explains that Agentforce is pitched as a “game changer,” so it is logical that the customers that buy totally into the vision 
might look at their prior spending plans and question whether the investments are going to become obsolete.
• The downstream effects of this are evident in the business as implementation work has decreased while advisory work has increased, which reflects less ‘doing’ 
and more ‘strategizing and planning.’
• The contact also explains that Salesforce is in the process of consolidating its platform, in some cases looking like it will sunset old acquired products more generally into 
the respective clouds, which is good in the long-run but is again adding another layer of confusion for customers who don’t know what they should or shouldn’t be buying.
• Industry expert opines that while this all sounds negative, they do believe the platform consolidation is the right move and customers genuinely seem excited about 
Agentforce.
• Partner takes a more positive view on the long-term, stating that as customers get comfortable with Agentforce, tech strategies become clearer for customers, 
and FY25 starts a new budget cycle.
Agentforce
• Contact is quite positive on Agentforce, commenting that the demos they have seen have been quite impressive and show “more understandable” applications 
and use cases of AI.
• Partner continues that this perception is similar among customers as well who have started to see the art of the possible and appreciate the more digestible ROI that some 
of the use cases demonstrate.
• The industry expert does think that the Agentforce excitement is real and palpable, while also noting that Salesforce is pitching the product extremely hard, but also 
believes that “it will take time,” indicating that excitement and enthusiasm can only help so much when coming up against the usual enterprise sales cycle and 
compliance, governance, and similar considerations.
Salesforce (CRM): Deep Dive Interviews (January 2025)

Industry Contact 1 – Cont’d
Agentforce (cont’d)
• Agentforce is described as helping improve Salesforce’s reputation for innovation again as it is seen as really driving the “agentic” narrative.
• Contact says that, outside of hearing about some small-scale POCs, they have heard of no bona fide, live Agentforce deals at this point, though they add that they 
aren’t particularly surprised given how early on we are in the lifecycle of the product: “it just launched.”
• Partner states that one other area they have seen a bit of pushback is how Agentforce is a bit of a “black box” at this point and has limited customizability, and customer 
stakeholders see that as ultimately limiting to their AI ambitions.
• Contact adds that this is likely a dynamic of the product being very early in its development, as Salesforce is typically known for having highly customizable products.
Marketing Cloud
• Contact comments that Marketing Cloud continues to be well-received by customers overall, though demand for marketing solutions continues to be subdued as it has 
been for a while now.
• They add that Marketing Cloud is seeing some confusion too as, for example, Salesforce has introduced the new ‘Marketing Cloud Growth’ product which, though attractive 
is confusing to customers and makes them question their existing investments.
• Overall, they think Salesforce is on the right track in terms of technology and mind share among customers, but continues to see challenges for marketing-related spend 
until there is incremental economic stability / growth.
Other Feedback
• The partner emphasizes the importance of data and the concept of ‘garbage in, garbage out’ in situations where customers don’ t have a proper data estate.
• They note that Salesforce is aggressively selling Data Cloud and giving attractive pricing to customers on the product since it is one of the stepping stones to AI.

General

Agentforce Customer Interest
• Describes Agentforce as being “on an absolute tear," noting higher levels of interest and engagement from customers since launch than any other in-house products 
Salesforce has released in the past.
• Shares that as a recent example, he met with six customers who all initially were “skeptical” of Agentforce as well as of Salesforce’s “broader AI story," however that after a

• Expects “maybe 15%” of his customers to be meaningful users of Agentforce by the “end of [CY25]”with structured contracts but shares that “if you ask [him] in a

• Believes that Agentforce came to market “right as many customers realized they were being too slow” as it relates to their AI roadmap, and that he is seeing customers 
“leapfrog” directly to Agentforce without having previously deployed any Copilot type tools.
• Indicates that in his recent customer conversations with enterprises, it is clear to him that “agents across the board are what enterprises need, not Copilots” and that 
he believes this is when “the next paradigm shift really takes off” like many have been “hoping or even praying for."
Agentforce Pricing
• Believes that a price point of $2 per conversation “actually makes a ton of sense at face value” and that while customers are hesitant “initially” when they consider 
how many conversations they have per day, they “very quickly see the ROI” as they deploy these agents into production.
• Suggests that he expects customers to quickly “burn through all forms of AI consumption credits” they have with Salesforce,as in his view customers will use 
products like Agentforce to handle “high-intensity tasks” with “deep level prompts," which will require calling back to more context rich and robust LLMs that necessitate 
“high levels of compute."
• Shares that in the few days following the GA launch of Agentforce, his firm closed “four deals for over $500K” that included Agentforce, but also other AI-
related products such as Data Cloud and Einstein.
• Notes that after the initial POC and testing phases, he believes that when some of his customers are ready to commit to a sizable contract for Agentforce, “they are 
hoping they can pay for it by using some of their leftover credits from the cloud providers."
Agentforce Use Cases
• Shares that for his firm’s practice, most of the use-cases for Agentforce have been related to Service Cloud, more specifically for service-oriented tasks within customers’ 
contact center operations.
• Believes that the “immediate driver” of larger-scale Agentforce deals will largely be tied to Service Cloud and that nearly all of his first five POCs showed the 
“strongest, more clear value” in service tasks rather than sales or marketing.
• Indicates that “eventually Agentforce will be a game changer for Sales and Marketing too," but that this requires a shift in the customer’s approach and further refinement of 
the product to better understand context, workflows, and differentiation in these areas of the market.
• Due to the “frequency” and “variety” of service requests, shares that he isseeing the most “interest and use” among his customers within the retail sector with 
“heavy contact center footprints."
Salesforce (CRM): Deep Dive Interviews (February 2025)

=== PAGE 127 ===

127
Industry Contact 1 – Cont’d
Labor Impacts
• Shares that with one of his existing customers who recently signed a deal for Agentforce, they “expect to reduce headcount by 5% this year and 10% next” within their 
contact center (located overseas), and that “even at $2 per conversation, it would be massive cost-savings” for them.
• Indicates that while many of his customers have moved their contact center operations to international regions due to a lower cost of labor, he believes that it will 
be “relatively impossible” for any decrease in labor costs due to offshoring to “come anywhere near”the downward trend in the cost of AI usage as these products 
evolve and scale.
• As customers potentially start reducing seats due to AI tools like Agentforce, notes that companies like Salesforce “could in the short term see a drop in TCV”with 
certain customers due to volume discounts aimed at adoption and seat cannibalization, but that over time “this will balance out” and “TCV will grow” as “the 
consumption really kicks off."
Data Cloud
• Believes that “Data Cloud is not necessarily a requisite” for Agentforce, though in many of his recent deals that have included Agentforce “in some capacity," it 
was often bundled with an “existing conversation or plan to launch Data Cloud”for the customer.
• Indicates that “packaging Agentforce with Data Cloud” is a “much easier sell”to the customer as it “alleviates” several aspects of the initial burdens of “deploying AI” 
where customers in the past felt like they lacked the in-house capabilities to get started and gives them “tools for all stages” of the process.
• Shares that “without Data Cloud, the output of Agentforce is not terrific sometimes”due to the fact that low quality inputted data does not help to generate “refined, 
contextual output” from the autonomous agents.
• However, shares that with some of his customers “who had very clean data due to Snowflake, Databricks or someone else," Data Cloud was “not needed at all” and 
customers were able to see “very impressive output” from Agentforce “nearly immediately."
Salesforce (CRM): Deep Dive Interviews (February 2025)

128
Industry Contact 1
General
• Contact works at a major systems integrator focused on Salesforce products, particularly on sales and marketing technologies. 
• Their efforts are primarily in the US and focused on Retail, Travel, and some other consumer-based businesses, though with insight into other verticals as well.
Demand Environment
• The contact notes that the demand environment is complex: coming off the US election in November, they note that customer sentiment directionally improved and 
became more optimistic, with some more increased customer activity, discussions, and inbounds. 
• However, going 2025 there appear to be more questions that have come up around interest rates, inflation, and policy, with the biggest worry factor around 
potential tariffs, partially offsetting some of the aforementioned positive dynamics. 
• Expanding on the topic of tariffs, the industry expert explains that certain customers, particularly large retailers, are concerned about what tariffs mean for 
prices of many of the products they sell coupled with consumer frustration with inflation and willingness, or lack thereof, to absorb ongoing rising prices. 
• While the industry expert acknowledges that these tariff fears have yet to actualize in a large-scale manner at this point, it is causing discussion among certain customers 
and remains a risk factor.
• It is also acknowledged that the contact’s focus on retailers and consumer-oriented industries perhaps influences the perception of tariff-related risks. 
• On the whole, the contact believes that customer sentiment has incrementally improved, inclusive of more customer activity, while caveating that seeing a 
material shift in spending patterns will “take time” and likely require sustained improved sentiment.
• Summing up the view, the industry expert self-describes as positive overall, but keeping a close eye on key risk factors, such as tariffs.
Agentforce
• Contact comments that, “as you should expect," Salesforce is hyper-focused on Agents across the entire organization and is working to integrate Agentforce throughout its 
entire platform. 
• The industry expert states that they are seeing “a lot” of activity and engagement by customers with Agentforce, though much of the activity is customers “piloting it, 
kicking the wheels, maybe a few doing slightly larger trial runs."
• However, they do know there are some substantial Agentforce deals out there and adds that it is “[positive to] see big deals so early on… it’s not surprising that most 
[customers are] just piloting at this point." 
• States that the large Agentforce deals they are aware of are in the Retail and Travel industries, though that might be biased a bit because of their focus on those 
verticals, and there are likely large deals in other verticals as well
Salesforce (CRM): Deep Dive Interviews (February 2025)

=== PAGE 129 ===

129
Industry Contact 1 – Cont’d
Agentforce – cont’d
• Goes on to say that they’re not surprised to see certain Retail and Travel companies leaning in due to competitive pressures in that space. 
• Speaking to the product, the industry expert opines that the productivity potential is pretty substantial with Agentforce: “[for example], when you’re looking at [end-to-
end] workflows for say, getting a marketing campaign up and running… there are situations where you can turn a process that would take two weeks [including 
waiting for different teams to coordinate and respond] into a process that takes a day or less," explaining that it isn’t just the hours saved by one person’s labor, but 
also leverage from fewer steps in the workflow process since Agents can work simultaneously with employees. 
• It is acknowledged that not all customers have the sophistication, know-how, and experience to realize this level of needs immediately.
• There do appear to be situations where customers feel like they’ve been a bit overpromised, with the contact commenting that “a lot of this is the fact that Salesforce 
markets Agentforce [very aggressively]… so some of this [is bound to happen] .”
• The industry expert notes that overall customers are impressed with Agentforce and seeing good ROI in the cases that they are deploying. They also add that the 
product is still relatively new and will be further developed and refined over time.
Data Cloud
• It is indicated that Data Cloud is seeing increased traction among customers, with the primary driver being that Data Cloud is effectively required for most customers. 
• Customers appear to be willing to adopt Data Cloud for the promise of Agentforce and the potential value that could come from Agents. 
• The contact explains that there are situations where Data Cloud is displacing incumbent CDPs as those customers see Agentforce as a priority and feel it is easier 
to just consolidate onto Salesforce. 
• “Sometimes the customers aren’t happy they have to change [their CDP] strategy, but they do it… in reality, it’s not that hard of a sell since Salesforce is 
ranked [very highly] for CDP.”
Consumption Pricing
• The contact explains that consumption pricing has been a challenge with customers, particularly when there are multiple sources of consumption-type spending and that is 
on top of multiple other products they are paying for with a given vendor already. 
• “[Customers] don’t have anything fundamentally against consumption-pricing… but there is uncertainty with it and it [becomes cumb ersome] to deal with so 
many [different pockets of spending].” 
• Interestingly, the industry expert suggests that Salesforce seems to be working its way through and solving the consumption problem; by our understanding, this 
seems to be done by Salesforce rolling up a customer’s total spend with Salesforce and putting guardrails around what the spend would most likely look like over a given 
period, both simplifying visibility across Salesforce spend and helping derisk the spending envelope. 
• Salesforce appears to be working closely with customers to solve these concerns and finding amenable solutions, potentially helping mitigate a key hurdle for customers.
Other Feedback
• Contact notes that Agentforce seems to be bringing a consistent, positive energy at Salesforce itself.
Salesforce (CRM): Deep Dive Interviews (February 2025)

• Looking forward, the industry expert opines that customers are continuing to move forward with investments “for the most part," with the assumption that the tariff-related

• It is noted that “it’s hard to say” that the environment is not riskier / more uncertain now than it was at the beginning of the year, despite no major disruptions so

Agentforce
• The partner continues to be constructive on Salesforce’s Agentforce product, stating that it has created real excitement in the market.
• The industry expert does add that customers are starting to feel “a little overwhelmed” by all the options for AI apps out there and aggressive selling by software 
companies; on one hand, this is posited as a positive dynamic for Salesforce as customers will typically focus on trusted incumbents in those cases, while on the other 
hand it does mean potentially competitive alternatives if customers should start shopping around for any reason. 
• The contact notes that most of what they’ve seen so far is still in the testing and piloting phase, with varying degrees of success, which is “natural," but 
emphasizes that the Agentic products are finding much more resonance vs. the Copilot products, suggesting that the value proposition of Agents is clearer to 
customers. 
• When asked whether they are hearing of customers having suboptimal or failed Agentforce implementations, the industry expert states that they themselves have not 
seen this, while caveating that they wouldn’t be surprised as there are “so many pilots out there” and there are bound to be bad experiences, particularly given 
that many customers are still climbing the learning curve of AI / Agents.
 Other Commentary
• Comments that ServiceNow is very aggressive in the market right now across their traditional focus areas, such as ITSM, as well as moving into the front-office; it is noted 
that ServiceNow’s success and proactivity is attracting partner attention, as partner consultants are being sold the dream of a rapidly growing ServiceNow marketplace.
• In discussing software spending and utilization, the contact makes an interesting point that while software spending has felt relatively measured since the start of the ‘tech 
recession’ in the 2022 / 2023 timeframe, “companies are utilizing SaaS more than ever” and that it is becoming “standard practice," suggesting that over time if 
software companies continue to deliver on the promise of Agents and optimizing pricing models toward consumption / usage, there could be upside through 
that vector.

could start to impact customer software investments going forward.
Agentforce

• When asked whether there are customers who are choosing not to proceed with Agentforce after piloting it, the contact comments that they haven’t seen 
customers reach that stage yet, but that they “wouldn’t be too shocked” because of this contact’s personal opinion that Salesforce has very aggressively 
promised and over-promised in some cases on what Agentforce can deliver.

difficult positions; the contact states that “Agentforce is not bad… it is good," but it still needs refinement and improveme nt as nearly all Agentic products do, 
yet customers have been promised “a ready solution."
• Echoing other feedback we’ve received, the contact emphasizes that there is also a need for customers to adapt to the new requirements of AI technologies and implement 
change management, stating that “some who are more [tech forward]… will have better experiences."  
• Overall, the contact is still positive on Agentforce and sees it as “quite promising” for their Salesforce practice group. 
Other Commentary
• Opines that there continues to be traction across Salesforce’s industry products as the more tailored solutions provide line of sight toward ROI.

=== PAGE 132 ===

132
Industry Contact 1
General
• Contact is a software industry expert, with insight into Salesforce and other enterprise applications. 
AI & Customer Behavior Patterns
• The contact sees a split in how companies are approaching AI, with some leaning into it while other companies are being slow, in their view. 
• The industry expert observes that certain companies are taking a wait-and-see approach to AI, holding the view that the technology is developing so rapidly they can 
simply ‘wait’ until the next big thing and therefore avoid costs in the near-term without losing edge. 
• The individual takes a negative view of this wait-and-see approach because AI has a learning curve associated that has to be overcome and the advantage AI can give is 
powerful: “I think those companies [that wait] are going to be in trouble." 
• Interestingly, the industry contact notes that a lot of large digital spend, especially associated with AI, is “no longer coming from the typical CIO budget… its now 
coming from the CEO’s office, there is a lot of strategic focus… that has made the sales cycles a little more complex, but it also means that the [deals are 
bigger and seen as more critical / less likely to get cancelled]."
• In terms of impacts from tariffs, does note some tightness earlier in the summer among certain sectors, specifically calling out manufacturing, consumer 
packaged goods, and retail, but notes other sectors are showing continued momentum, like life sciences. 
Can Salesforce Get Back to Double Digit Growth
• On the topic of whether Salesforce can get back to 10%+ growth, the contact says that while it is difficult to say for certain, they are “bullish” but adds that it will inevitably 
depend on Agentforce, Data Cloud, and other emergent products. 
Salesforce Strategy Around Data
• The contact explains that Data Cloud is integral to Salesforce’s longer-term strategy around AI as it helps facilitate smoother AI workflows within the platform and unlock the 
data that people expect to leverage through Salesforce as a system of record. 
• When asked whether Data Cloud competes with other data software companies like Snowflake, the industry expert explains that this isn’t Salesforce’s current strategy, nor 
are the personas similar: those who are leveraging Data Cloud tend to be salespeople, marketers, Service Cloud users, and they want to use Data Cloud and don’t want to 
be in something like Snowflake, while there are personas who want to live in something like Snowflake and don’t want to use Data Cloud. 
• In sum, it is communicated that Salesforce doesn’t want to compete with other data software companies for the foreseeable future, but over the longer-term 
“who knows." 
• Contact states that currently requests that run through Data Cloud typically cause two layers of usage costs for customers, Salesforce Data Cloud plus another platform 
such as Snowflake, but notes that Salesforce is working to configure Data Cloud in a way such that it can access the data held in Apache Iceberg tables without having to 
incur costs on intermediate data software platforms.
LLM Usage Within the Salesforce Platform
• Contact explains that OpenAI continues to be the predominantly used LLM among customers leveraging AI through the Salesforce platform, noting that it is 
what customers are most familiar with and go to first. 
• However, the industry expert notes that Salesforce is constantly opening the aperture for more customer choice in terms of LLMs, with Anthropic making some headway 
with customers. 
• It is added that as customers get deeper into developing AI use cases and workflows, they’re more likely to start to fine-tune LLMs or use a bring-your-own LLM structure, 
which helps fit their specific needs.
Salesforce (CRM): Deep Dive Interviews (September 2025)

Quotable Quotes Sourced From Multiple Industry Contacts
AI/Agentforce Commentary, Adoption & Use Cases
• [CRM Ecosystem Contact]: “We have a customer and we spoke to one of their sales managers. We took all of their training materials and everything like that, 
digested the information for an Agent and created a sales coaching Agent. The next day he said ‘it’s awesome, we love it and we have to have it,' He used to 
have to do the coaching himself, so he’d do the coaching maybe a few times a year for a given rep because he’s only one person with a bunch of salespeople. 
Now it continuous, weekly even. Need to brush up on sales negotiation? The sales coaching agent can do that. Need to learn how to improve first 
conversations with prospects? Sales coaching agent. It’s even better than one-on-ones because people get nervous talking to their direct reports about how to 
improve, don’t want to look bad. So it’s available all the time and, even the customer said this, it does a better job [than a human coach].”
• [SI Contact]: "I've been talking to a couple of people at Salesforce, and I think the opinion of leading with Agentforce might be mixed slightly. It makes sense from a 
commercial perspective to lead with Agentforce, because that's what everyone's talking about. Therefore, that's what they're looking for. But it's probably really just the front 
window into the other possibilities. An agent without the right data, without the right structure, without the right application, is useless. I would say Agentforce has 
to be an opening conversation, and it has to lead to that roadmap. The challenge is the landscape that we have now was very, very different just six months ago, and, 
in another six months, it's going to be different. So, how are you now helping customers to develop a roadmap?"
• [SI Contact]: "Customers are in the testing phase of AI. For example, how do you perceive AI as of now? ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini; we use it on a personal basis, right? 
Just text responses for the most part and it could hallucinate. But when you bring AI into the enterprise and use AI to automate certain business to generate more things 
that a human may not be able to do or maybe in a faster period of time, that set of expectation is quite high. Buzz is high. But delivery is also important, right? 
Expectation is high. Delivery is currently not very impressive. So, that is why people are a little bit skeptical of directly making a purchase."
• [SI Contact]: "We are seeing production use cases of Agentforce. They don't all make it, and some customers are better than others. Some are doing thoughtful 
experiments, while others are a bit scared and are organizing their data a bit more. I would say it’s mixed. In life sciences, Agentforce is used, for example, for validation 
stuff. Stuff that's intensive, stuff that's potentially prone to errors. When you're looking at something the whole day, you're going to miss a character here and there, right? 
So Agentforce is used to reinforce and then do an intelligent balance of automation, but [with] the human in the loop at the right time."
• [SI Contact]: “In terms of interesting Agentforce use cases, I have customers using Agentforce to create 100% personalized website experiences for end 
customers. It can write code in a near-real time way so that people who are logged in will see a user-specific website portal based on data that Salesforce 
collected about the customer. It’s remarkable, it does it autonomously, there is no developer writing code. Now, this was a pilot and right now it’s cost prohibitive 
based on how much data and processing it takes, but the costs of AI are going down and that sort of experience will become the norm.”
• [SI Contact]: "Top enterprise customers who are very much focused on the service management / CX part, they are using Agentforce for two reasons. One is to give a 
faster resolution to the customer, and second is to obviously reduce the manpower so that they can optimize the cost. Apart from the service management part, other 
areas include core manufacturing process, order management, fulfillment, or any core operations processes. Agentforce is being used in bits and pieces, but is not 
able to tangibly help to transform or automate or reduce in a significant way. It is doing it in bits and pieces."
• [CRM Ecosystem Contact]: “You have to think of what’s possible. I think we’ll be able to make an Agent that can plan a whole sales trip for you. You’re going to 
Manhattan, here is a list of meetings you can have over the course of eight hours, optimized route, organized by best prospects – who is ready to buy and what 
are they interested in – and then a list of talking points. The technology isn’t there yet, but the foundations are there, and Salesforce has all that data. That is 
the power of Salesforce.”
• [SI Contact]: “For the Agentforce use cases I see in production and at scale, a lot of it is contact center or support. That’s just the lowest hanging fruit and easy to show to 
customers. But there are others. A lot of uses cases for internal processes especially, and that has a lot of potential in my field of focus [that is healthcare]. There are 
customers using it at scale and they are seeing real value, they’re happy.”
• [SI Contact]: “There are a lot of cool Agentic and AI use cases. One utility customer is using it to analyze areas that could be at risk for wildfires starting and they’ll 
proactively address that. Another customer uses it to analyze and find risks they didn’t even know were there, finding unknown unknowns. There’s a lot of 
value there. It’s honestly astonishing what AI can do, and like I said I think it’s still immature.”
Salesforce (CRM): Deep Dive Interviews (October 2025)

• [Digital Travel Agency Customer]: “The concept of AI-driven personalization changes a lot with how we can interact with customers through our apps or website. There is a

• [SI Contact]: “Customers are going through a learning curve with AI. Big technology changes are always this way, happened with move to cloud too. The trend 
emerges, customers need time to learn how to manage it. Once they do, they’ll be more confident on spending on AI, and on non-AI software as well, it just takes time.”

• [SI Contact]: "The core business is going to be pretty standard and flat growth going forward. The big bet for Salesforce and for us is Agentforce. It has to work

Data Readiness & Data 360
• [SI Contact]: "We have to pick a very specific or finite use case where we feel like a customer's data is ready to go. So a lot of our projects are plugging into a

• [SI Contact]: "The key point here is that for Agentforce to work, a very solid data strategy behind the scenes is necessary. Agentforce can deliver if you have a

maturity for the data."
• [Non-Profit Customer]: "We aren't using Agentforce because we have yet to have a proper data strategy. With all the AI stuff, you need to have the data correct, such

• [SI Contact]: "I remember 20 years back also, we used to talk about data maturity. We are still talking about data maturity, but things have also evolved. Earlier it 
used to be relational database. Now we have all sorts of databases. So that is where most of the organizations would be spending their money."

• [SI Contact]: “Data 360 is a good solution. It’s robust, it’s scalable - but whether it’s Data 360 or any other data-oriented solution, the problem is usually data. Remember,

• [SI Contact]: "We have large customers and their data is a mess. No Agents, no AI can solve that. They need to climb the curve and get everything in order. It

Pricing & Consumption Model
• [SI Contact]: "I don't know if Agentforce helps Salesforce accelerate past 10%. Using it as a technician assistant, as a financial advisor assistant, as an example. 
All those things are great, but everything boils down to consumption credits. Earlier they used to have per user, per month licensing model. They are switching 
that all to consumption model, and it's actually a good thing. If you have 100,000 users, if you buy enough credits, and you manage your credits well, then it's a 
good thing. If you blindly keep on buying credits, then you might end up paying more for those credits. So you have to be very careful. It's like how big of a tap 
will you open, right? You can open the faucet as much as you want."
• [SI Contact]: "You have to be careful in terms of how much you consume. If you start with small, internal use cases, keep implementing, keep iterating, keep observing, 
keep monitoring. You will be razzled and dazzled by all the demos that they give you, but it's very important to be grounded, and it's important to make sure that you are 
finding business value."
• [Agricultural Science & Products Customer]: “The consumption model is hard. It’s unpredictable. It reminds me of the old cell phone plans they had decades ago 
where it’s the 25th of the month and the parents are saying ‘don’t you text anyone for the rest of the month’. If they can make it work, an unlimited-type plan 
would feel much better.” 
• [SI Contact]: “What is a big one [Agentforce deal]… probably a million… maybe two. A big Agentforce deployment isn’t contributing more than a million in revenue 
(up front). It’s all consumption-based and slow to show up. Just because you’re live doesn’t mean you’ve consumed a million dollars worth of consumption.”
• [Agricultural Science & Products Customer]: “Yes, Salesforce has increased prices. But it’s usual, we negotiate with them. We end up paying more, but we’re getting more 
product too. You can’t really live without them after a certain point.” 
• [CRM Ecosystem Contact]: “I really like the unlimited approach for Agentforce. It’s much more interesting. Without that, each step of the way the customer is thinking 
about price, always. This approach should be much better.” 
• [SI Contact]: "Why bother with consumption? You're taxing the customer. Buying behavior changes as soon as you remove a roadblock. The all-you-can-eat 
model could be really good in that regard."
• [Agricultural Science & Products Customer]: “Salesforce doesn’t give anything for free. Whatever deals Salesforce is making, they’re going to get their cut over the long 
run.” 
• [SI Contact]: "In order for Salesforce to accelerate growth from here, they really need to get a better grip on their pricing strategy around Agentforce and Data 
Cloud."
• [SI Contact]: “Customers are getting more comfortable with consumption spending; Salesforce is working closely and playing nice with customers to get it figured out”
• [SI Contact]: "Customers aren’t fully sure how to manage the price of these offerings yet and can get frustrated when they run into budget constraints early on."

integration within ServiceNow, which is a benefit.

Moveworks Acquisition – Viewpoints (cont’d)
• Moveworks is a logical and exciting move that strengthens [ServiceNow’s] agentic AI capabilities and positions it as a leader in enterprise automation. Moveworks’

ServiceNow Major Product Offerings - Areas of Rapid Growth/Strong Demand and Vice-Versa
Mentions of Strong Product Vectors
• Gaining the most traction: ITSM Pro / Enterprise and ITOM – still the entry point for most customers and for Now Assist adoption.
• Now Assist/Pro-Plus – early buzz is strong; customers like the “out-of-the-box” nature instead of custom LLM work and are starting to see more value since [ServiceNow] 
reduced the pricing for assists.
• Customer Service Management (CSM) continues to show strong demand due to increasing emphasis on customer experience, automation, and self-service channels 
across industries. Companies are seeking scalable solutions to streamline customer support and reduce case wait times -- CSM offers that efficiency with native AI and 
integration capabilities.
• Most required: Now Assist Gen AI, SAM [Software Asset Management], HAM [Hardware Asset Management], ITOM.
• NowAssist AI capabilities are a very big driver across customers currently using ServiceNow.Adding AI capabilities to ITSM, ITAM, CSM and virtual attendants has sparked 
new hope for further efficiencies in the workplace, better customer support and quick ROI.
• Winners: CSM/CRM, AppEngine, FSM [Field Service Management] Business focused not IT department.
• IT Operations Management (ITOM) – Strong Growth. Why it's winning: Platform consolidation: Customers are tired of managing fragmented monitoring, discovery, and 
event tools. ITOM helps them reduce tool sprawl. Hybrid visibility needs: Demand for CloudOps, hybrid infrastructure, and cloud-native app monitoring is growing. 
ServiceNow’s acquisition of observability partners and deeper integrations with AWS/Azure bolsters this.AIOps demand: The push for proactive issue detection and 
resolution via ServiceNow AIOps is accelerating adoption. Evidence: Most enterprise deals now bundle ITOM with ITSM. We’re seeing customers start with Discovery and 
Event Management and later add Service Mapping and Health Log Analytics. ITOM Visibility is especially popular in regulated industries and manufacturing, where CMDB 
hygiene is tied to audit requirements.
• Customer Service Management (CSM) – Strong Growth. Why it's winning: Front-office digital transformation: Enterprises want to streamline customer and partner 
experiences beyond traditional CRM. Complex service workflows: ServiceNow shines in B2B and post-sales support environments where CRM alone can’t handle case-to-
resolution workflows. Evidence: We’re seeing strong [CSM] uptake in telecom, healthcare, and financial services — especially when integrating with Salesforce or custom-
built CRMs. The addition of Field Service, Virtual Agent, and GenAI for Case Summarization is making CSM more attractive and competitive.
• Security Operations (SecOps) – Moderate to Strong Growth. Why it’s doing well: Audit and risk alignment: Ties well with IRM, allowing security teams to manage threat 
response and risk from one dashboard. Incident response workflows: Organizations want automation around SOC processes without buying another SIEM/SOAR platform. 
Evidence: Customers already using Splunk, Palo Alto Cortex, or Sentinel are layering SecOps for workflow integration. Strong pull from compliance-heavy sectors (finance, 
healthcare, public sector).
• GRC / Integrated Risk (IRM) – Moderate Growth. Why it’s still winning: Mandates for risk visibility: Risk frameworks (NIST, ISO, CMMC) are driving organizations to 
implement platforms like ServiceNow. Cross-functional alignment: The power of IRM grows when integrated with ITSM/CSM — especially for policy exceptions and risk 
mitigation tracking. Evidence: We’re seeing mid-market and enterprise customers replacing Archer and LogicGate with ServiceNow IRM due to better usability and 
integration across workflows.
• AI-powered solutions and Security Operations (SecOps) / IRM/ TPRM growing.

Quotable Quotes Sourced From Multiple Industry Contacts – Cont’d
ServiceNow Major Product Offerings - Areas of Rapid Growth/Strong Demand and Vice-Versa (cont’d)
Mentions of Strong Product Vectors
• We are seeing rapid growth in NOW Assist and Source-to-Pay. While working with [ServiceNow] reps, we are seeing most new proposals going out with some portion of an

in incorporate their ERP systems, but the move is a bit slower as the conversation is different than traditional ITSM, ITAM. Further the buyer for this as well as NOW Assist 
is different, so pulling those different areas of the business together can be a challenge.
• Seeing demand across the business lines of Source-to-Pay and Vendor Management. Still bullish on the Risk and Security side of the platform due to shift for us at a GSI 
partner.
• ServiceNow's AI products are definitely seeing an uptick. Mainly their AI Agents. I think companies are just looking to do more automation, and AI is a huge buzzword right 
now.
• With the boom of the AI market, I believe the AI driven solutions within ServiceNow can be expected to grow. AI-driven solutions, ITSM, and CSM are poised for rapid 
growth due to strong market alignment, enterprise adoption, and ServiceNow’s leadership in automation and AI integration. These products benefit from the platform’s 
cross-functional capabilities and the global push for digital transformation and personalized CX.
Mentions of Slower Product Vectors/Market Saturation
• Not as hot right now: ITSM Standard – most prospects skip straight to Pro; existing Standard customers keep getting upsell offers.
• Standalone Discovery without the broader ITOM suite - not viewed as being overly valuable without the other tools.
• Less Required: IRM | SPM | FSM. I think the lack of interest is coming mostly because clients are moved towards automation as many as possible and what is the top topic 
for now which is AI.
• Losers: ITSM | Price/delivery competition with other partners, market coverage.
• HR Service Delivery (HRSD) – Slowing Down. Why it's losing steam: Saturated market: Many customers already implemented HRSD during COVID-19 as part of remote 
work transitions. Competition: Players like Workday, UKG, and ServiceNow’s own licensing costs make it harder to justify upgrades or net-new purchases. Limited 
differentiation: Outside of onboarding and case management, most orgs don’t see a lot of extended value. Evidence:Many customers are pausing HRSD expansions 
unless they tie into broader employee experience (EX) initiatives or consolidate multiple HR help desks.
• App Engine / Creator Workflows – Mixed Signals. Why it's facing headwinds: Internal competition: Some organizations already use low-code platforms like [Microsoft] 
Power Platform or Mendix. Enablement gap: Customers want to build apps, but lack skilled resources or governance frameworks to do so safely and scalably. Evidence: 
While App Engine Studio has improved, many customers struggle with adoption post-purchase. It’s often shelfware unless there's a defined use case or partner-led 
innovation.
• Procurement / Source-to-Pay – Nascent Adoption. Why it’s slow: Entrenched ERP systems:Oracle, SAP, and Coupa dominate this space. ServiceNow isn’t seen as a 
source-to-pay leader yet. Integration burden: Customers hesitate to move procurement into ServiceNow unless they’re undergoing a broader ERP transformation. 
Evidence: We rarely see greenfield adoption. When it happens, it’s often as an enhancement to existing ServiceNow workflows, not a true ERP replacement.
• Traditional ITSM & ITOM is slower.

executing its agenda.
• Adds that this was rolled out on a staggered basis across different agencies, not at once, to their knowledge.
• Because of this, the contact anticipates that it expects the start of Q2 to be soft.
• The industry expert relates that government agency customers seem to think that by the end of May some of the changes that are being executed will “settle 
down” and deals in the pipeline may potentially resume.
• Contact states that they have lost no deals so far and that their practice may be doing better than others in the marketplace in terms of bookings / pipeline.
• Adds that their pipeline this year is multiples of what it was last year.
2025 Practice Expectations
• When asked about any revisions to their 2025 plans, the partner comments that revenue has been revised downward due to deals shifting right, but the full 
year bookings target remains.
• In explaining the rationale, the contact states that they expect federal deal-making to be even more back-end / Q3 weighted than usual due to disruptions that 
began with the new administration.
• The industry expert does caveat that “there is no [historical] data” for what is happening right nowand is simply assuming that these procurement delays will resolve 
through the end of the government fiscal year.
Government Efficiency Efforts
• Partner states that DOGE is currently in the phase of reducing costs, particularly headcount costs since they are a significant part of the budget and indicates headcount 
reductions in the range of 10-20% in certain cases.
• Calls out a certain customer agency that wiped out all mid-level procurement employees in one fell swoop on a single day.
• The contact senses that DOGE is aiming to wrap up these major, disruptive changes quickly and return to some sense of normalcy.
• Opines that despite near-term headwinds, ServiceNow is in an “absolute prime position” due to its ability to make more efficient and augment human labor and 
existing market share / incumbency with Federal agencies.
• The industry expert sees their firm and ServiceNow as positioned to benefit “tremendously” from the focus on efficiency after the period of disruption passes.

legacy solutions. But there are some obvious economic uncertainties (e.g. impact of tariffs) outside of NOW's control that could impact customer budgets.
• It will grow. The more AI features you add to every product, that will attract more new clients and will make existing client add AI. I think whatever is happening in the 
world if you don’t use AI nowadays, you are not advanced and behind of everyone.
Unclear Confidence in Close to 20% Growth
• Although ServiceNow is currently leading the market, their rising pricing structure could eventually position them as their own biggest competitor. This trend may make their 
offerings increasingly unaffordable for organizations outside of the Fortune 500.
• Not really and would be surprised if it did as 20% revenue growth seem unsustainable.

Quotable Quotes Sourced From Multiple Industry Contacts – Cont’d
ServiceNow Major Product Offerings – Areas of Rapid Growth/Strong Demand and Vice-Versa
Mentions of Strong Product Vectors
• Recently, there has been growing interest in Customer Service Management and HR Service Delivery applications, particularly when paired with NowAssist.
• Still seeing a lot of interest in AI and how it can be leveraged, but also a lot of interest in standard implementations... ITSM, SAM, HAM, ITOM, etc. I don't see any lack of 
interest in ServiceNow as a whole. This is mainly due to the macroeconomy.
• We’re seeing the strongest growth in ServiceNow’s AI-powered ITSM and Hyperautomation offerings, driven by cost efficiency and productivity gains. Customers are 
prioritizing automation to reduce manual work.
• IT Service Management (ITSM)
• AI is everywhere. All the clients are looking for it and that is being applied to module license they have.
• We’re seeing the strongest demand in core ITSM and CSM, specifically customer workflows. In the CRM/customer workflow segment, we've seen a lot of interest around 
the new Financial Services Operations products, but haven't signed any deals quite yet.
Mentions of Slower Product Vectors/Market Saturation
• While IT Service Management (ITSM) remains a consistent presence in the market, it is becoming increasingly saturated. On the other hand, IT Asset Management (ITAM), 
including Software Asset Management (SAM) and Hardware Asset Management (HAM), as well as IT Operations Management (ITOM) and Event Management, continue 
to face challenges in market adoption, primarily due to difficulties in demonstrating the ROI of their implementation.
• On the other hand, legacy HRSD and CSM modules seem to face headwinds due to market saturation and stiff competition from more specialized vendors. Adoption here 
is slower as customers consolidate platforms or delay non-core transformations.
ServiceNow (NOW): Deep Dive Interviews (July 2025)

=== PAGE 167 ===

167
Industry Contact 1
General
• Contact is with a global system integrator partner that works with ServiceNow and has a focus on HR-related products, with insight across ServiceNow’s broader portfolio.
• The partner works with large enterprises primarily based in the US.
Demand Environment
• Contact describes the demand environment as stable-to-slightly positive despite ongoing uncertainty, noting that customers are having to accept and “come to terms 
with” a certain level of volatility in the current environment.
• It is explained that uncertainty spiked in April and that while deals slowed down for a few weeks, most customers seem to have weathered it relatively well and seem to be 
focused on deploying the budget that they do have and, on the whole, are continuing to execute projects on their roadmap, bot h new and existing.
• When asked whether there are any tariff impacts that they are seeing, the contact opines that there are weaknesses in pockets such as manufacturing, retail, and CPG, 
which in some cases are offset by strength in other verticals.
• Adds their view that customers who are choosing to be more measured / restrained in their spending are being selective about where they pull spending, choosing to focus 
on consulting services or less critical solutions while maintaining spending on AI and other core modernization projects, “[customers] have [certain] priorities they’re 
sticking to no matter what.”
ServiceNow HR Service Desk (HRSD) Competitive Dynamics
• The contact explains that in many cases, ServiceNow’s HRSD automation offerings have become the “default” for many of their clients as they’ve come to trust 
ServiceNow.
• In addition, the partner comments that many of their clients have come to see the incumbent system of records offerings in the HR space as “clunky,” though they note 
that many of the HR system of record companies are investing in Agentic offerings as a way to try to “close the gap” that ServiceNow has developed over time.
• The expert states that demand for ServiceNow’s HRSD is “robust” as customers see it as an area ripe for efficiency and improvementand is growing “a good bit” 
faster than the ITSM practice, though ITSM is substantially larger and growing at a healthy rate.
AI & Agents
• The contact states that customers are “hyper-focused” on AI and trying to find areas to integrate new tools, including Agents, wherever it makes sense as there is 
“definitely a fear of falling behind their competitors.”
• The contact expands that despite the high level of interest, two dynamics remain true: (a) customers are only engaging in projects where there is clear, demonstrable 
ROI on a nearer-term time frame (e.g., not years out) and (b) most of the projects continue to be mostly smaller scale, as customers remain a bit tepid regarding 
implementing AI in a broad manner.
• The partner believes that customer readiness for AI at scale remains a key hurdle to broader AI and Agentic adoption, with challenges ranging from data readiness to the 
change management required for wide AI adoption – “it’s a lot of change at once for customers.”
Other Feedback
• The industry expert opines that ServiceNow has done a good job of winning mindshare among customers who view ServiceNow as one of the leaders of “practical, 
high quality” AI, meaning that there is customer willingness to try the products that ServiceNow is / will launch.
• Notes that customers are now expecting (more so than in the past) to see software efficiencies manifest in either reduced headcount or at least offsetting the need for 
additional hires, providing customers a tangible ROI on software spending.
ServiceNow (NOW): Deep Dive Interviews (July 2025)

168
Industry Contact 1
General
• Mentions that his practice does not just implement ServiceNow, but after doing so, also operates the end-to-end business processes for clients. As such, he believes that 
his practice has “more visibility or better line of sight [than other partners]”when it comes to how ServiceNow is being used inside organizations.
• The partner’s practice has exposure to Financial Services, Manufacturing, and Consumer Packaged Goods.He does not have exposure to the US public sector.
Positive About “Upcoming Months” for ServiceNow Practice
• “So, I’m actually feeling pretty confident about our ServiceNow practice for the upcoming months precisely because of this [AI-related demand for 
ServiceNow].”
• Since the end of last quarter (June end), the partner shares that ServiceNow’s AI Pro Plus license tier is “getting a lot of attention.”The contact notes that when Pro 
Plus first came out, all it gave the customer was Now Assist with Generative AI with it. However, he indicates that AI Pro Plus has recently seen greater customer 
interest, specifically for capabilities like AI Agent Studio, AI Agent Orchestrator, Agent Control Tower, and Agent Fabric.
• He observes “a lot of interest” for ServiceNow’s CRM solution as well as ServiceNow’s industry vertical solutions.
Vibe Coding Is Not Displacing Third Party Software Applications, For Now
• He does not observe that his clients are displacing third-party software applications with vibe coding tools.
• The contact mentions hardly any of his clients are vibe coding to begin with, as his clients are using existing ServiceNow modules and applications that are 
already meant for specific business purposes. He adds that “ServiceNow takes you [the customer] 60-70% there [for the business process] and then we [his practice] 
configure it from there.”
• He opines that the “whole concept” of vibe coding a software application makes sense when designing a custom app, but not necessarily for a business use-case.
• With respect to vibe coding, he adds that “In most of the areas that we [his practice] operate in, we’re not actually building custom apps. We are using existing ServiceNow 
applications and configuring them for our clients for their business processes. We try to minimize customization as much as possible.”
Highlights Productivity Gains with ServiceNow for the Back-Office
• “There’s a lot of customer service use-cases [as it relates to ServiceNow’s AI Pro Plus], and we see a lot of back office operations use-cases as well...We’re 
seeing a lot of productivity gains there. Customers are able to run operations with fewer people because you have capabilities that you can do through AI. So, 
we are seeing very positive results there.”
• In one instance, the partner indicates that his team operates the back-office business processes for a wealth management client. With ServiceNow’s AI Pro Plus offering, 
his practice has been able to operate the same back-office functions with fewer people than before.
Clients Remain Wary of the Promises AI Can Deliver in General
• The contact observes that in general, his clients are a “bit wary” of the promises that AI can potentially deliver, although ServiceNow has thus far been an 
exception, as the company has been able to demonstrate value with its AI solutions.
• When asked why some clients are wary of AI and the promises it can deliver, the partner explains that it has to do with the “last leg of the process.” He notes that “if you 
have a process and are able to automate a large percentage of it, you still need people. Many organizations are not really seeing the immediate benefits.”
• He opines that his clients will measure the success of implementing AI by either being able to reduce headcount, meaning they deliver the same value with 
fewer people, or through AI capabilities to improve revenue growth, thus increasing incremental profitability.
