172
Industry Contact 1
General
• The customer is a F50 Media/Technology/Communications company.
• The contact describes a longstanding relationship with Snowflake, dating back to their initial contract pre-2020. His Snowflake deployment sits on top of AWS.
• His company also uses Databricks.
Leaning Into Snowflake in the Near-Term
• The contact notes that his company’s spend on Snowflake is slightly over $20M per year, while it is slightly under $20M per year with Databricks.
• Both platforms play a central role in security data analytics use-cases for his company, and the customer also partners closely with his firm’s enterprise data organizations 
to maximize value from these tools.
• Indicates that neither Snowflake nor Databricks is fully deployed across his entire organization. He estimates current adoption for each platform at roughly half of 
critical workloads, reflecting the ongoing nature of their broader cloud migration and data modernization initiatives. He anticipates further adoption ahead.
• Explains that his company will be allocating modestly more dollars and resources to Snowflake over the next 3-5 years and some of that may come from other 
enterprise-scale data engineering and ML stacks. Additionally, he opines that other organizations will do the same, given Snowflake’s ease of use, governance 
features, and cost transparency advantages.
Anticipates Broader Enterprise Adoption from Rise of “Citizen Data Analysts”
• He notes that Snowflake has further differentiated itself by enabling data applications to be built directly on its platform.
• The contact anticipates a shift toward broader enterprise adoption of Snowflake, in part because these capabilities allow not only engineers but also “citizen 
data analysts” to develop business-specific applications. While he has not yet seen widespread internal adoption of this citizen developer model, the customers thinks 
it is likely to materialize, potentially driving higher stickiness for Snowflake.
Customer Compares Leading Cloud Data Platform Providers
• The customer compares Snowflake and Databricks, noting that in the 2019-2020 period, the two were often viewed as complementary. He adds that Databricks positioned 
itself as the AI platform and would point customers to Snowflake for data warehousing and BI capabilities.
• Opines that Snowflake provides “really great visibility in terms of logging, fine-grained user access management, and transparent billing. You can go in there, 
you click a button, and it shows what it’s costing you. They [Snowflake] take care of you.”
• The contact notes that Databricks offers a lot of engineering flexibility, but in his opinion, with less built-in governance and cost visibility.
• Opines that Databricks is working to converge functionality with Snowflake’s “core areas,” including ease of deployment, robust governance, data clean rooms, and data 
sharing.
• Observes that there are tools available in the market that make it easier to optimize Snowflake usage, including select.dev.
• On the other hand, he indicates that there are not many tools that help you optimize Databricks usage, which makes it difficult to manage from a cost perspective.
• “SageMarker [AWS] is starting to get okay enough that you could move workloads off of Spark-native data and AI systems.”
• According to the customer and his conversations with colleagues in the industry, sentiment toward Snowflake has improved over the past year.
Snowflake (SNOW): Deep Dive Interviews (August 2025)

=== PAGE 173 ===

173
Industry Contact 1 – Cont’d
Inputs on Snowflake’s Recent Product Innovation
• The customer shares that his company leverages nearly all of Snowflake’s solutions, though adoption of Cortex has been limited so far, with plans to explore it in the near 
future.
• He comments that Snowflake’s recent product initiatives in AI, particularly with Cortex, have made it easier for organizations to leverage existing data for AI 
workloads.
• Expresses a measured view of Snowpark, as he notes that adoption is limited within his company.
• He highlights his interest in OpenFlow for data ingestion, which he notes emerged from Snowflake’s acquisition of Datavolo.
• The customer expresses his opinion that the former Snowflake CEO, Frank Slootman, was highly effective and closely engaged with customer use-cases. But during the 
CEO transition, he recalls an abrupt slowdown in a division of Snowflake that his company was working with, resulting in some communication challenges. However, over 
the past year, communication with Snowflake has improved and the customer acknowledges that Snowflake’s recent product strategy appears stronger [than a 
year ago], as momentum is “shifting back” into the company’s favor.
Iceberg Implementation Drives Data Platform Interoperability
• He indicates a shift in his company’s architecture toward open-table formats like Apache Iceberg, which enables interoperability and flexibility in workload 
placement. Historically, his company would put workloads in Databricks and replicate them to Snowflake, but now, with open formats, BI users are directed 
straight to Snowflake, while AI/ML developers use Databricks as needed.
• The contact describes his organization’s Iceberg implementation as “smooth” thus far, noting that it enables greater interoperability between Snowflake and Databricks.
• Explains how Iceberg allows both Snowflake and Databricks to access a single copy of data stored in S3, supporting a “decoupled query engine” model where BI 
workloads can run via Snowflake, while AI/ML workloads can leverage Databricks, all without duplicating data.
• Opines that Iceberg is a positive development, in general, as it removes the “lock-in of proprietary formats and allows multiple query engines to operate on the same 
datasets...This is a significant industry shift.”

• Expanding on the point: “I think these companies raised prices so much [during the rapid inflation a few years back] that they probably have some profit margin to give 
back by not raising prices for consumers. In some cases they are just passing on tariffs to consumers.”
• Regarding rising consumption and utilization of data clouds: “Everyone needs data for AI, sometimes it’s that simple.”
Product Commentary
• Notes that Cortex is seeing significant momentum among customers and the use cases are varied: “Cortex gives customers a very easy and logical place to 
integrate AI with all the data they’ve been migrating. It just makes sense.”
• Expresses excitement for Snowflake Intelligence: “Snowflake Intelligence is basically a better Cortex. It takes it to the next level, where Cortex lets you talk to your data 
[through AI], Intelligence takes that next step where it can provide recommendations, give you analysis you didn’t think to ask, provide deeper analysis, and so on. It’s [not 
generally available] now but I think it will be a big deal.”
• On OpenFlow: “It’s a powerful product and it helps give access to data that you couldn’t get to before. Helps open up data that’s available to use in Snowflake, 
so it removes another barrier to Snowflake usage.”
Why Snowflake is Winning
• In describing the seamless experience: “Snowflake stands out vs. competitors because it’s so easy to implement and start using it. Sometimes customers see what’s 
required for other platforms and immediately ask for alternatives. They ask how fast I can get Snowflake up and running for them, and the reality is I can do it in a matter of 
hours for some use cases and they can start using it.”
• On the Snowflake ecosystem that facilitates data utilization: “Snowflake has a lot in its ecosystem that makes data analytics and utilization easy. You have 
OpenFlow, clean rooms, offerings like Snowflake Retail Data Cloud. All of this together just makes it so easy for customers to move data around, share it, sell 
it, whatever they need. Customers start seeing it as the platform they can go to and get done what they want quickly and without much issue, it becomes a 
habit.”
Snowflake (SNOW): Deep Dive Interviews (October 2025)

Industry Contact 4 – Cont’d
Snowflake & Databricks
• In discussing Snowflake and Databricks market positioning: “They’re pretty much the same now and moving towards each other in any area they aren’t the same. They’re

on how much tech competency a company has, you basically need at least 500 or maybe 1,000 employees before it makes sense to use Databricks for a lot of customers.

• On larger companies: “A lot of the larger customers I have use both. They’re very competitive with each other. Some companies use one or the other more. I 
think Snowflake has a slight edge [in certain use cases] because they’re focused on making usage easier, and customers follow the path of least resistance.”
Other Commentary
• On Snowflake’s potential for expansion into incremental workloads: “I think the Snowflake platform is powerful, and not fully utilized sometimes. But [customers] come 
around over time. I have a customer that stores data with Snowflake, ports it into Tableau to do analytics and then visualization. I think it’s only a matter of time before [the 
customer] starts using Snowflake for the analytics and just uses Tableau for the visualization output only."

Customer Expects his Company’s Snowflake Spend to Double in Seven Years

bring into Snowflake and we did it over a period of time.”
• The contact believes that his company’s spend with Snowflake may double in about seven years from today, implying ~10% annual organic growth.
• Highlights two reasons why his company will continue to expand with Snowflake: 1) The natural growth rate of data for his organization and 2) newer use-cases with 
Snowflake. He adds that Snowflake has transformed into more of an ecosystem rather than a “simple database.”
• “When data volumes increase, two things happen - one is your hardware can’t keep up and your software can’t scale even if your hardware can keep up. We were hitting 
the ceiling where we weren’t able to look across our customer portfolio to drive analytics. We needed something that’s going to be a little bit more flexible, something that 
doesn’t require capital investment in the data center every time we see there’s this growth in data.”
• “I like the approach that Snowflake has taken so far with their products. But also, they are a company that culturally fits with us. They are customer centric.”
Contact Highlights that Snowflake is the Most Important Piece of Their AI and Data Strategy
• “What we really need is a unified AI and data strategy because at the end of the day, you can’t separate the two. If your data is bad, the AI isn’t really going to help 
you. And the way that I think about Snowflake is that Snowflake is sort of the most important piece of this puzzle and that’s because it is our data platform.”
• Mentions that his company has not yet made any “major platform strategic decisions” as it relates to AI. Notes that the market is still evolving and his company is naturally 
conservative when it comes to materially changing its IT budget.
• Indicates that Snowflake is tied to his company’s AI budget.

• The contact observes that Workday is experiencing some level of market saturation in the U.S., particularly among large enterprises since the majority have already 
adopted Workday into their IT ecosystem, leading to a strategic pivot toward government projects and potential international expansion.

• The contact identifies Workday as gaining traction against competition from legacy platforms such as PeopleSoft and UKG, with many clients transitioning from 
these systems to Workday for more modern solutions.
• They mention they have seen limited competition from other HR systems like SAP, Dayforce, Paycor, Paychex, or Paylocity when working to land new deals, 
suggesting Workday’s strong position in its target markets.
• The contact notes that Workday’s primary focus remains on its HRMS and FINS+ modules, which are the most commonly implemented solutions. They believe this helps 
Workday stay competitive amongst their core offerings, but leaves little room for advancement in their less emphasized products or highly customized applications like 
Workday Student, Extend, Orchestrate or Prism Analytics.

• The contact highlights that integration projects involving healthcare data and procurement processes are vital, underscoring Workday's role in providing essential solutions

The New US Administration and AI
• They mention that many companies had previously limited their spending on non-essential projects, focusing instead on optimization efforts starting in 2022. However, 
with the election uncertainty resolved, they believe there is a sentiment that businesses may be more willing to invest in strategic initiatives, including software 
upgrades and new implementations.
• They have not observed any significant impact from the new U.S. administration on Workday’s business but express a belief that the administration’s policies 
could be beneficial for the software industry as a whole as they believe the administration will focus on digital transformation and modernization of government 
infrastructure and lead to increased demand for software solutions, including those offered by Workday.
• They anticipate that AI will play a crucial role in this transformation, as organizations look to leverage technology to improve efficiency, reduce costs, and enhance decision-
making processes. The contact believes that as AI becomes more integrated into business processes, it will create new opportunities for software companies 
like Workday.

Industry Contact 1 – Cont’d
In-house AI Development as a Threat to Existing SaaS Solutions
• The partner describes a complex interaction between certain enterprises developing their own AI solutions and the downstream impact to the existing software stack.
• The clients who are most likely to develop internal AI solutions appear to be the largest of enterprise customers.
• It is clear that internal AI development is “not meant to replace an existing system” at this point, but rather developing smaller solutions around the platform for specific 
workflows; theoretically, this type of behavior could offset incremental spend that could have gone to a provider such as Workday to provide the given solution.
• At a higher level, the partner does see potential advantage of native AI solutions (e.g., OpenAI, Anthropic, etc.) if they bypass the need for users to log into and interact 
with a traditional UI system (e.g., Workday, Salesforce, etc.) and directly ‘talk’ to the AI solution (e.g., OpenAI, Anthropic, etc.) and initiate workflows through natural 
language; however, incumbent SaaS platforms do have advantages in the form of security, governance, integrations, and other benefits of being scaled third-party SaaS 
solutions.
• Industry expert notes that with ‘AI native’ type solutions, “it’s the data security that’s a big concern."
• When asked how long it would take for AI solutions to gain the security and other requirements to be on par with existing SaaS platforms, the contact suggests that it 
would likely take 4-5 years and that the SaaS incumbents aren’t going to “just sit around” in the meantime.

• The contact works with 20,000 customers and 400 technology providers, including Zoom. His expertise spans telephony (unified communications), collaboration (Zoom 
Meetings), and Contact-Center-as-a-Service, delivering 20-30 UCaaS/CCaaS projects per year personally and ~250 peers executing similar volumes at his firm.
• He notes that his firm is particularly active in mid-market and lower-enterprise migrations off legacy Avaya/Cisco/Shoretel systems into cloud-based UCaaS/CCaaS.
Demand Environment
• Shares that his UCaaS activity is up meaningfully versus last year, with ~18% more opportunities y/y and average deal size ~30% larger, driven by migrations from 
long-standing on-premise systems.
• Notes that Contact Center activity is also accelerating, particularly at the upper end of the market (5k-20k agents), where customers must modernize legacy platforms, 
respond to end-of-support timelines, and increasingly adopt AI-enriched workflows.
• “AI has moved from the top 10 to the top 5, probably closer to 3” priority for CCaaS evaluations, with customers shifting from general interest to concrete deployment 
discussions.
• Among customer segments, mid-market carries the most velocity, while enterprise is growing but presents more structural barriers, mainly due to Microsoft 
Teams’ entrenchment in large accounts.
Enterprise vs. Mid-Market Dynamics
• He explains that Zoom faces a difficult path into the upper enterprise, where Microsoft 365/Teams is the default for meetings and telephony.
• “Unless they’re already using Zoom, they’re very unlikely to consider Zoom for contact center.”
• States Zoom rarely participates in 10,000-25,000 seat contact center deals, though they can win at the lower end of enterprise (e.g., 1,000-2,000 agent 
environments).
• Despite this, he believes Zoom “has a seat at the table,” supported by favorable economics and large planned investments in Contact Centers through 2026. He 
characterizes Zoom as sitting in the “second row” of vendors considered for enterprise RFPs, with the possibility to move up, but unlikely to displace Teams at scale.
• Emphasizes that Zoom wins consistently in the mid-market, where customers are generally unaware Zoom even offers Phone or Contact Center beyond Meetings. He 
notes that if Zoom were hypothetically able to convert 40-50% of its Meetings base into Phone + CCaaS customers, “they will crush their numbers.”
Product & Vendor Landscape
• Notes that Zoom is ahead of the other UC/CC providers (RingCentral, 8x8, Dialpad) in roadmap progress and innovation velocity.
• Among enterprise CCaaS players, he ranks tools such as NICE and Five9 highly and in the “first row.” He views Zoom as still “second row” in enterprise.
• He views Zoom’s pace of AI-native enhancements as a key differentiator.
Pricing & Licensing
• Among the three SKUs for Zoom Contact Center, which price at $59, $99, and $129 per user per month retail, the contact notes that realized pricing typically lands at $50-
55, ~$90, and $120, respectively.
• States that Zoom does not need to discount meaningfully, unlike peers, “because it’s already so appreciably less expensive.”
• Within his deployments, the mix is roughly 50% entry-tier, 35% mid-tier, and 15% top-tier, as a reference to the $59, $99, and $129 SKUs.
• He believes the average CCaaS deployment size for Zoom specifically is 150-500 agents.
Zoom Video (ZM): Deep Dive Interviews (November 2025)

=== PAGE 191 ===

191
Industry Contact 1 – Cont’d
Growth Expectations
• For his firm, Zoom Contact Center MRR is growing 40-45% y/y, accelerating from ~30% the prior year. He expects 35-40% growth next year, with upside 
depending on current ‘in-flight’ opportunities.
• His firm likely represents low-to-mid single-digits percentage of Zoom Contact Center revenue, though Zoom is trending toward one-third of all new Contact Center 
deployments they manage in the mid-market.
AI & Contact Center Transformation
• He has not seen any customer use AI to reduce agent headcount.
• “Zero… nobody is asking us to design AI to reduce agents by X%.”
• AI uplift instead adds 20-30% to spend depending on whether it is layered onto an existing deployment or included at initial sale.
• He expects modest long-term reductions in contact center staffing, but not the large declines some predict.
• “You’re not going to see a thousand-seat contact center go down to 200 people.”
Zoom Video (ZM): Deep Dive Interviews (November 2025)
